☕ Java Tips — Cracking the Java Coding Interview

396 Java interview questions — straight answers, real code

Every entry follows the spoken format of the series: a Short answer and a Less short answer, plus the code shown in the video.

Transcribed and edited from the Cracking the Java Coding Interview shorts by the cours-en-ligne channel. All credit for the questions and explanations goes to the author.

2

What is the Object class in Java?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The superclass of every class you can create.

Less short answer

When you create a class, you can explicitly extend another one with extends — you can even declare extends Object, which is valid... and dumb, because it is unnecessary. Remember: in Java a class extends only one class — multiple inheritance does not exist. If you declare no extension at all (the norm), the compiler makes your class extend Object for you. In the end, every Java class extends Object.

class A { }                    // compiler: class A extends Object { }
class B extends Object { }     // legal... and unnecessary
// class C extends A, B { }    // ERROR: one superclass only

Object o = anything;           // universal polymorphism
3

Can you cite methods from the Object class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

toString, equals and hashCode.

Less short answer

There are more. getClass returns the class of the object. wait, notify and notifyAll — used in concurrent programming; never call them unless you really know what you are doing. finalize: do not override it anymore — deprecated, and whatever you put in it is, most of the time, buggy. And the last one: clone — stay away from it too. In a nutshell: toString, equals and hashCode are the most used.

Object o = "text";
o.getClass();      // class java.lang.String
o.toString();      // "text"
o.equals("text");  // true
o.hashCode();
// wait()/notify()/notifyAll(): monitors — experts only
// finalize(): deprecated; clone(): avoid
4

What are the differences between a String and an array of chars?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A String is an object full of useful methods; an array only has the ones from Object — not very useful.

Less short answer

A String is non-modifiable: once created, it never changes. The size of an array is fixed — once created, you cannot add elements (only replace one with another). How do you compare them? For strings, just use their equals. Comparing an array with its equals does not do what you expect — use the equals from the Arrays factory class; the same goes for toString: use Arrays.toString to print an array properly. One last word: you can create a String from an array of chars with new String(array).

char[] cs = {'j', 'a', 'v', 'a'};
String s = new String(cs);            // char[] -> String

cs.equals(otherCs);                   // references: NOT what you expect
Arrays.equals(cs, otherCs);           // content: correct!
System.out.println(cs);               // prints the chars (special case!)
Arrays.toString(cs);                  // [j, a, v, a]
5

How do you sum the elements of an array?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Two solutions: an old-fashioned one everyone knows — and a great one.

Less short answer

The first one: iterate over the array, adding the elements to a sum variable — do not forget to initialize it to 0 — and you are done. The second one: create a stream from your array and just call sum() — the result is the same. And, by the way, on a stream you can also call min(), max() and average(). Yes — this is the presenter's favorite: simpler, cleaner, more readable.

int[] values = {1, 2, 3, 4};

int total = 0;                       // 1) classic: don't forget the zero!
for (int v : values) total += v;

int total2 = Arrays.stream(values)   // 2) favorite: clean and readable
                  .sum();
double avg = Arrays.stream(values).average().orElse(0);
6

What is the JVM?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Java Virtual Machine.

Less short answer

The piece of software that runs your Java code — and it does much more than run your application. Among many other things, it handles the memory of your application (with the garbage collector), it optimizes the running code (with the Just-In-Time compiler, the JIT), and it handles the security, making sure no undesired code can sneak in and be executed on your behalf. One last word: if someone talks to you about HotSpot, that is the name of the open-source JVM developed by the folks at the OpenJDK.

// the JVM in one diagram:
// .class (bytecode) -> loading/verification (security)
//                   -> interpretation -> JIT (C1/C2) = native speed
//                   -> heap + GC = automatic memory
java -XX:+UseZGC MyApp    // you choose the GC; HotSpot is the JVM
7

How can you sort an array?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that: Arrays.sort. Pass your array to it and that's it.

Less short answer

When you need to sort something, forget about writing a sorting algorithm yourself. What about the best sorting algorithm? That is actually a tough question — but you do not need to worry about it, because Arrays.sort does the job for you. Now, if you really want to shine in an interview, you can cite three algorithms: quicksort, heapsort and Timsort. The one used by Arrays.sort (for primitives) is called dual-pivot quicksort. One last word: never cite bubble sort... unless you want to say that it is a terrible algorithm.

int[] n = {5, 1, 4, 2, 3};
Arrays.sort(n);                 // [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] dual-pivot quicksort
Arrays.sort(n, 1, 4);           // sorts only the range [1, 4)

String[] s = {"b", "a"};
Arrays.sort(s, Comparator.reverseOrder()); // objects: Timsort (stable)
8

What is the main characteristic of the String class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

This class is non-modifiable.

Less short answer

There is no way to modify an instance of the String class once it has been created. So if you call toUpperCase, it does not change the string — it creates a new one holding the result. If you really want a string of characters you can modify, there is a specific class for that: StringBuilder — which is not a String; it actually wraps an array of chars/bytes whose content you can modify, and you can create a String from it by calling toString(). One last word: use + to concatenate strings. Forget the old "new StringBuilder on every line" — it is useless... most of the time.

String s = "java";
s.toUpperCase();        // does NOT change s; returns a NEW string
s = s.toUpperCase();    // this works (reassignment)

StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("java");
sb.append("!").insert(0, "> ");   // modified in place
sb.toString();                    // "> JAVA!"
9

What is the difference between == and equals()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

== compares references; equals() calls a method.

Less short answer

If you compare strings with ==, you may get false even if the strings hold the same value. In a nutshell: == is good to compare things that are not objects — ints, longs and the like. You should never compare floats or doubles with ==: what you should do is compute the absolute value of the difference and check whether it is smaller than a very small value. One last thing: everything (object-wise) is a reference in Java, so most of the time calling == on objects is wrong.

String a = "x", b = new String("x");
a == b;          // false (different references)
a.equals(b);     // true

int i = 1;  i == 1;                // OK: primitive
// double d = 0.1 + 0.2;
// d == 0.3;                       // false! never == with double:
Math.abs(0.1 + 0.2 - 0.3) < 1e-9;  // true: this is the way
10

What is the relationship between equals() and hashCode()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The Java specification says: if two objects are equal, then they should have the same hashCode.

Less short answer

It means that when you override equals, you should always override hashCode as well. The spec says two objects that are equal must have the same hashCode — but the other way around is not true: you can have two different objects with the same hashCode (the collision). One last thing: always use your IDE to write these methods. First, your IDE will not forget to override both; and second, it will give you a better implementation than the one you will write... well, most of the time. (Records generate both.)

record Point(int x, int y) { }   // consistent equals + hashCode, free

// the contract:
// a.equals(b) == true  -> a.hashCode() == b.hashCode()  (MANDATORY)
// same hashCodes       -> equal OR collision (equals decides)
// breaking the contract -> HashSet/HashMap "lose" your objects
11

How can you duplicate an array?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Two solutions: Arrays.copyOf and System.arraycopy.

Less short answer

There is a clone() method on arrays — internally it returns an Object that is cast, so performance-wise it is not ideal. System.arraycopy needs a destination array to copy your source into. Arrays.copyOf truncates your source array if it is bigger than the destination length you ask for, or pads it with zeros if it is smaller; System.arraycopy has a richer API — it can copy a portion of your source array into the destination. One last word: Arrays.copyOf calls System.arraycopy internally — so use whichever you prefer.

int[] a = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5};

int[] b = Arrays.copyOf(a, 3);        // [1, 2, 3] (truncates)
int[] c = Arrays.copyOf(a, 7);        // [1..5, 0, 0] (pads with zeros)
int[] d = new int[2];
System.arraycopy(a, 1, d, 0, 2);      // [2, 3] (a portion, rich API)
int[] e = a.clone();                  // works (internal cast: less ideal)
12

How can you reverse a string?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Wrap it in a StringBuilder and call reverse.

Less short answer

The String class is non-modifiable, so you cannot change what the string is — all you can do is create another string that is the reverse of the first one. Looping over the letters of your string to reverse it is actually what the reverse() method of StringBuilder does — so do not bother reinventing the wheel; it is there for you to use. StringBuilder is a modifiable class, so the letters are reversed internally. One last word: to get the reversed string, you just need to call toString() on your StringBuilder.

String s = "java";
String reversed = new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString(); // "avaj"

// manual equivalent (what reverse does for you):
char[] cs = s.toCharArray();
for (int i = 0, j = cs.length - 1; i < j; i++, j--) {
    char t = cs[i]; cs[i] = cs[j]; cs[j] = t;
}
13

What is a functional interface?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface with only one abstract method.

Less short answer

Functional interfaces can be implemented with lambda expressions. Java is a statically typed language, so all your variables have a type, and all these types are known at compile time — and the type of a lambda is always a functional interface, no exception. A functional interface can contain any number of default or static methods, and it can have any method from the Object class as abstract methods — but only one abstract method of its own: the one your lambda has to implement. One last word: add the @FunctionalInterface annotation and the compiler will tell you whether you got it right.

@FunctionalInterface
interface Validator<T> {
    boolean valid(T t);               // the ONLY abstract method

    default Validator<T> and(Validator<T> other) {  // default: as many
        return t -> valid(t) && other.valid(t);     // as you want
    }
    static <T> Validator<T> always() { return t -> true; }
    boolean equals(Object o);         // Object doesn't count!
}
Validator<String> v = s -> !s.isBlank();
14

What is the difference between overriding and overloading?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Overriding has to do with inheritance; overloading does not.

Less short answer

Overloading is the same method with different parameters. Overriding is the redefinition of a method from a superclass in a subclass. When your code calls a method that has several overloads, the compiler decides which method to call — so overloading is resolved at compile time: this is called early binding. Overriding is not: the compiler cannot resolve the call — it is resolved at runtime: late binding. One last word: you can prevent a method from being overridden by declaring it final; you cannot prevent a method from having different overloads.

class A {
    void m(int x) { }        // overloads: the compiler chooses
    void m(double x) { }     // (early binding)
    void m() { }             // overridable: runtime decides (late)
}
class B extends A {
    @Override void m() { }   // overriding: same signature
    // final in the superclass would block the override; never the overload
}
15

What is a map?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object that maps keys to values.

Less short answer

Map is an interface from the Collections framework; its canonical implementation is the HashMap. You cannot have duplicates among the keys: a given key maps to a single value — there is no "multi-map" concept in the framework; if you need one, map the key to a list of values. Use non-modifiable objects for your keys — small strings are enough, or longs — prefer them over integers, even for small values. One last word: the HashMap class supports null keys and null values... but please don't do that. It will put you in trouble.

Map<String, List<Order>> byCustomer = new HashMap<>();
byCustomer.computeIfAbsent("ana", k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(order);
// a hand-rolled "multimap": key -> list

Map<Long, User> byId = new HashMap<>();  // long > integer for keys
16

What is an ArrayList?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An implementation of the List interface backed by an array.

Less short answer

An ArrayList is sometimes called a dynamic array because when the internal array becomes full, it is transparently copied into a larger array. Be careful though: this array can only grow — it never shrinks. So a large ArrayList in your application can eat up a lot of memory. One last thing: ArrayList is your best choice for a list implementation — forget about LinkedList (LinkedLists are good for stacks; we will talk about that another time).

List<String> l = new ArrayList<>();
l.add("a");   // internal array full? copied to a bigger one (~50% growth)
              // it NEVER shrinks on its own: l.trimToSize() if needed

// ArrayList: get O(1), add at the end amortized O(1)
// LinkedList: forget it (except as a deque)
17

Explain finally

▶ Watch video
Short answer

finally marks a block after a try block that is always executed when the try block exits.

Less short answer

Always means: even if there is a return, a continue, a break or an exception causing the try block to exit. The finally block is the key tool to properly release resources that are not AutoCloseable — for AutoCloseable resources, your best choice is the try-with-resources statement, which takes care of closing them for you. Non-AutoCloseable resources should be released in a finally block — that is the case for ReentrantLocks, for instance, or for semaphores. One last word: in case of a return in the try block, the finally block is evaluated after the return.

lock.lock();
try {
    process();             // even with return/continue/exception...
} finally {
    lock.unlock();         // ...this ALWAYS runs
}

int test() {
    try { return 1; }
    finally { System.out.println("runs BEFORE the return!"); }
}
18

How to make a class immutable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Use records if you can.

Less short answer

If you cannot use a record: make all the fields of the class final — and do not forget to make the class final too, because if you don't, someone could add some mutable state to it using inheritance and pass it around using polymorphism. Do not forget defensive copies of the modifiable objects you get as arguments in your constructor (lists, maps and the like) — and the same for your accessors: make sure they do not leak references to modifiable internal state; use a defensive copy wherever you need one. One last thing: String, Integer, Long and all the wrapper classes of the JDK are non-modifiable — you can check them to see how they work.

public final class ClassGroup {               // final class!
    private final List<String> students;      // final fields
    public ClassGroup(List<String> students) {
        this.students = List.copyOf(students); // defensive copy (in)
    }
    public List<String> students() {
        return students;                       // already immutable: no leak
    }
}
// and the short path: record ClassGroup(List<String> students) { } + copy
19

What is the difference between String, StringBuffer and StringBuilder?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

String is non-modifiable; StringBuilder is modifiable; StringBuffer is thread-safe.

Less short answer

StringBuffer is rarely used. StringBuilder is nice for certain string manipulations — like reversing a string or inserting a character. Should you use StringBuilder to concatenate strings? Most of the time, no: on Java 9 and later, string concatenation with + has been optimized; and on Java 8, in many cases concatenation with + was in fact compiled with StringBuilder under the hood. One last word: if you are joining string elements from a list or an array, consider using String.join, the StringJoiner class, or Collectors.joining.

String s = "a" + b + c;             // Java 9+: already optimized (invokedynamic)
new StringBuilder(s).reverse();     // manipulation: builder is right here
String.join(", ", names);           // joining a collection: the right tool
// StringBuffer: forget it (unless you need per-method synchronization)
20

How to make a singleton?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

One pattern: use an enum with a single value.

Less short answer

The pattern that uses a class with a private constructor, a private static field and a factory method that tests whether the field is null... is more complex — either non-thread-safe, or buggy, or both. Making it thread-safe with double-checked locking is also buggy — just look for this bug on your favorite search engine. Using an enum is super simple, thread-safe, guaranteed bug-free at the JVM level, and recommended by the best experts. One last word: this enum-based pattern is used in many places in the JDK — you can check the source code of the Comparator.naturalOrder() factory method, for example.

enum Configuration {                // singleton: simple and bulletproof
    INSTANCE;
    private final Map<String, String> props = load();
    public String value(String key) { return props.get(key); }
}
// usage: Configuration.INSTANCE.value("timeout")

// double-checked locking: full of historical subtleties — avoid
21

What is a static method?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A method declared with the static keyword.

Less short answer

A method that you can invoke without creating any object — you invoke it with a class name; you can even invoke a static method through a null reference (the compiler swaps it for the class). A static method cannot call a non-static method from the same class. They are useful to implement the main method — classes like Math, Arrays or Collections only have static methods. One last word: static methods are sometimes called factory methods because they can be used to create objects in a certain way — List.of(), for instance; classes with only static methods are sometimes called factory classes.

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) { }  // main is static!
    static void util() { }                       // no this
    // void instanceMethod() { util(); }  // static does NOT call instance
}

Math.max(1, 2);        // utility "factory"
List.of("a");          // factory method: creates the special way
Integer.parseInt("7"); // classic static
22

How can you find a character in a string?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that: indexOf.

Less short answer

It has several overloads. One takes the character as an int (not a char — Java supports Unicode and some characters cannot be encoded on a single char); another one takes a String, because indexOf can also find small strings in bigger strings. Both can also take an index used to start the search from. indexOf returns the index of the first occurrence of the character or the string — so if you want to find all the occurrences of a single letter, you can loop in that way. One last word: if the character or the string is not found, indexOf returns -1.

String s = "abracadabra";
s.indexOf('a');              // 0
s.indexOf('a', 1);           // 3 (search starts at index 1)
s.indexOf("cad");            // 4 (substring!)
s.indexOf('z');              // -1

int pos = 0;                                 // all the occurrences:
while ((pos = s.indexOf('a', pos)) >= 0) {
    System.out.println(pos); pos++;
}
23

What is a method reference?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Another way of writing a lambda expression.

Less short answer

There are four kinds of method references. Static method references (Type::staticMethod); bound method references — the classical System.out::println; unbound method references — they may look like static calls, but they are not, so be careful (String::length); and constructor method references (ArrayList::new). One last word: to translate a method reference to a lambda, you need to know its exact type. And one very last word: method references are there to improve the readability of your code — so if you feel your code is actually less readable with them, then don't use them.

Integer::parseInt             // static
System.out::println           // bound (known object)
String::length                // unbound (1st argument becomes 'this')
ArrayList::new                // constructor

// the translation depends on the target type:
Function<String, Integer> f = Integer::parseInt;   // s -> parseInt(s)
ToIntFunction<String> g = String::length;         // s -> s.length()
24

What is a default method?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a regular method written in an interface.

Less short answer

Default methods were added to the language in Java 8. Prior to that, all the methods in an interface had to be abstract (the abstract keyword is not even mandatory for them). For regular concrete methods, you need to add the default keyword — hence their name. Apart from that, default methods are just regular methods: a default method from an interface can be used by any object that implements that interface, and they can be overridden in any concrete class. One last word: in Java 8, default methods could only be public; starting with Java 9, they can also be private.

interface Health {
    void register(int heartRate);        // abstract (abstract is optional)

    default boolean critical(int b) {    // concrete, with a body
        return b > 120;
    }
    default int doubleIt(int b) { return 2 * level(b); }
    private int level(int b) { ... }     // Java 9+: private for defaults
}
25

What does JIT mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

JIT stands for Just In Time compiler.

Less short answer

What you have in a class file is the bytecode of your class. This bytecode is first interpreted by your JVM — and running your Java code that way is super slow. So a first JIT compiler, called C1, compiles it to machine language. C1 does a nice job, but your compiled code can be optimized even further — that is the job of a second JIT compiler, C2: it carefully analyzes the way your code is running and compiles it again to leverage some more optimizations. One last word: if at some point C2 sees that it can do better, it will de-optimize your code and optimize it again.

// the life of your code:
// bytecode -> interpreted (slow)
//         -> C1 compiles (fast, with profiling)
//         -> C2 recompiles (flying, aggressive optimizations)
//         -> if the assumption breaks: DEOPT and start over
int sum(int[] data) {
    int t = 0;                       // hot loop -> C2 unrolls/vectorizes
    for (int x : data) t += x;
    return t;
}
26

What kind of method can you override?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Instance methods.

Less short answer

Static methods cannot be overridden because you call them using an explicit class name — so even if a class B extends class A and defines the same static method, you can still call each of them (it is hiding, not overriding). Instance methods can be overridden as long as they are non-private and non-final, and their class can be extended — the obvious way to prevent extension is to make the class final, but you can also declare its no-arg constructor private. One last word: the safest way to override a method is to just copy its declaration in your extending class — return type, parameters and exceptions... but you can be more subtle than that. (Another video.)

class A {
    public void m() { }            // overridable
    private void secret() { }      // private: not seen, no override
    public final void fixed() { }  // final: forbidden
    public static void util() { }  // static: hiding, not overriding
    private A() { }                // private ctor: class cannot be extended
}
class B extends A {
    @Override public void m() { }      // OK
    // @Override public void fixed() {} // ERROR
}
27

What is the signature of a method?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Its name and its parameters.

Less short answer

Neither the return type of a method nor its declared exceptions are part of its signature. Because all the methods of a given class must have different signatures, you can have several methods with the same name — as long as they have different parameters (overloading) — and these methods can have different return types. But it is not legal to have two methods with the same name, same parameters, and just a different return type: they would have the same signature. One last word: this rule holds even if one of these methods is abstract — it would force the implementation to break the rule.

int add(int a, int b) { ... }
double add(double a, double b) { ... }   // OK: different signatures

// double add(int a, int b) { ... }  // ERROR: same signature
// (the return type is NOT part of the signature — JLS §8.4.2)
28

What is a marker interface?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface with no method, that is just here to mark a class.

Less short answer

Being a marker interface is not really a language or an API notion — it is just a way of describing the nature of some interfaces. There are several examples in the JDK: Serializable, of course (which allows the instances of a class to be serialized), among others. One thing: because there is no method in these interfaces, the way to use them is to check whether the object you have is an instance of the interface — with instanceof, or directly at the class level, with the Class.isAssignableFrom method.

class Order implements Serializable { }  // the mark: "I can be serialized"

if (order instanceof Serializable) { writeToStream(order); }
Serializable.class.isAssignableFrom(Order.class);  // true

// other JDK markers: Cloneable, RandomAccess, EventListener
29

What is the difference between a collection and a set?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are no duplicate elements in a set.

Less short answer

Collection and set are interfaces from the Collections framework; Set extends Collection. All the instance methods defined in the Set interface override methods from Collection and are redefined with a different semantic — they do not work the same way (the "no duplicates" semantic changes everything). The Set interface is implemented by HashSet, which has another very interesting property: its implementation of contains is super fast — much faster than the one from an ArrayList. One last word: if you need both the ordered iterability of a list and a fast contains, take a look at the LinkedHashSet class — which, unfortunately, is not a list.

Collection<Integer> c = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 1, 2)); // dupes OK
Set<Integer> s = new HashSet<>(c);   // [1, 2]: duplicate dropped

s.contains(2);    // O(1): hash
c.contains(2);    // O(n): scan

new LinkedHashSet<>(c); // insertion order preserved + fast contains
30

How does finalize() work?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It does not work. (the presenter's demolition short answer)

Less short answer

It was deprecated in Java 9 and deprecated for removal in Java 18 — so stop using it. Finalize is supposed to be a method called by some undefined element when the garbage collector detects that an object is not reachable anymore. Here are two of its major issues. First, the thread running finalize is not specified — odds are you can get race conditions in this method. Second, it may take a lot of time for this method to be called — the correctness of your code should never rely on finalize being called. One last word: there is a replacement pattern, the Cleaner pattern — check the Cleaner class for more information.

@Deprecated(since = "9", forRemoval = true)  // and JEP 421
protected void finalize() { }   // unspecified thread + slow

// the replacement: Cleaner
private static final Cleaner CLEANER = Cleaner.create();
// CLEANER.register(this, () -> release()); + AutoCloseable
31

What were the main features of Java 8?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Lambda expressions.

Less short answer

Lambda expressions were added to the language — but many other things came along. The Collections framework was rewritten, with many new methods; the Stream API is a very clean implementation of map/filter/reduce, usable in parallel; the CompletableFuture API brought an asynchronous programming model to the JDK; and many, many small things here and there to make your developer life better. One last word: Java 8 was released in March 2014 — at the time of this recording, nine years ago; if you are still working on this version, you should really consider moving to a more recent one.

// Java 8 in four lines:
Runnable r = () -> { };                                 // lambdas
list.stream().map(...).filter(...).collect(toList());   // Stream API
Optional<Order> p = findById(id);                       // Optional
CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(this::load);              // async
32

Do streams and collections have common methods?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Yes and no. They do have one method that is called the same and takes the same parameters.

Less short answer

This method is forEach: it takes a consumer, and you have it on both the Stream interface and the Iterable interface. That being said, even if they are basically doing the same thing, these methods are not implemented in the same way — and most of the time you can call collection.forEach(...) directly instead of collection.stream().forEach(...): it saves you the creation of the stream object. If you don't use it, why would you want to create it? One last word: even if collections and streams are there to process data, there are many differences between them — but that's for another time.

list.stream().forEach(System.out::println);  // creates a stream for nothing
list.forEach(System.out::println);           // direct: same thing, better
33

How can you tell that a string is an anagram of another string?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The easy way: sort the letters of the strings and just compare them.

Less short answer

There is actually a much smarter way of doing that. You can map each letter to a prime number — for instance, map A to 2, B to 3, C to 5, etc. — then multiply all these numbers for each string. If the two numbers you get are the same, the strings are anagrams — it works because the decomposition of an integer in a product of prime numbers is unique. The first method has a complexity of n log n; the second one is just n, which is faster. One last word: be careful not to overflow when doing the multiplication — it can happen for long strings of characters.

// classic way: sort and compare — O(n log n)
Arrays.equals(
    a.chars().sorted().toArray(),
    b.chars().sorted().toArray());

// smart way: product of primes — O(n)
int[] primes = {2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, ...};  // A, B, C, ...
long prodA = a.chars().reduce(1L, (p, c) -> p * primes[c - 'a']);
long prodB = b.chars().reduce(1L, (p, c) -> p * primes[c - 'a']);
prodA == prodB;   // anagrams! (careful: overflow on long strings)
34

On what kind of source can you build a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is no short answer: there are many sources.

Less short answer

Collections, of course — this is the most common way. But the JDK offers much more than that: you can also create a stream on the lines of a text file, on the characters of a string, on the elements of a string you split using a regular expression, and several others. Plus, the Stream API gives you what you need to connect a stream to your own source of data. One last word: be careful — the processing of a stream should not modify its source while it is consuming it; if you do that, the result you will get is unpredictable.

collection.stream();
Arrays.stream(array);
Files.lines(path);                        // lines of a file
"abc".chars();                            // characters (IntStream)
Pattern.compile(",").splitAsStream(csv);  // lazy split
Stream.generate(supplier);                // infinite source
// your own source: StreamSupport.stream(yourSpliterator(), false)
35

What is the groupingBy()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A collector you can use to build maps that are in fact histograms from the elements of a stream.

Less short answer

The pattern is stream.collect(Collectors.groupingBy(fn)): the function maps each element of your stream to a key, which is added to your map — and the corresponding element is added to the list bound to that key. So if several elements of your stream are mapped to the same key, you will find them together in that list. One last word: you can post-process this list with another collector, passed as a second argument to the groupingBy — the so-called downstream collector.

Map<String, List<Person>> byCity = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Person::city));

// with a downstream: instead of the list, count per city
Map<String, Long> histogram = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Person::city,
                                       Collectors.counting()));
36

What is the difference between FIFO and LIFO?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

FIFO is a queue; LIFO is a stack.

Less short answer

FIFO stands for first in, first out; LIFO stands for last in, first out. There are two interfaces in the JDK to model these: Queue, which models the FIFO — extended by Deque, the second interface, which models the LIFO. The preferred implementation for both is ArrayDeque — which is not thread-safe; if you need a thread-safe implementation, use a ConcurrentLinkedQueue (Queue) or a ConcurrentLinkedDeque (Deque). One last word: there is a Stack class in the JDK — it extends the Vector class; both have been deprecated for so long that you should not use them anymore.

Deque<String> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // LIFO
stack.push("a"); stack.push("b");
stack.pop();                                // "b" (the last one)

Queue<String> queue = new ConcurrentLinkedQueue<>(); // FIFO, thread-safe
queue.offer("a"); queue.offer("b");
queue.poll();                               // "a" (the first one)

// new Stack<>(); // 1998 called: don't
37

What is the GoF?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The nickname of the fundamental book on design patterns.

Less short answer

The full title of this book is Design Patterns: Elements of Reusable Object-Oriented Software, published in 1994. The four authors are Erich Gamma, Richard Helm, Ralph Johnson and John Vlissides. It contains 23 design patterns in three categories: creational (Builder, Factory, Singleton), structural (Adapter, Decorator, Proxy) and behavioral (Iterator, Strategy, Visitor). One last word: this book is a must-read — the examples are written in C++, but it is easy enough to convert them to Java or to your favorite language.

// you use the GoF every day:
List.of(1, 2, 3).iterator();      // Iterator (behavioral)
new BufferedReader(r);            // Decorator (structural)
enum DB { INSTANCE; }             // Singleton (creational)
38

How can you create a comparator?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Just use the factory methods of the Comparator interface.

Less short answer

This interface has a bunch of factory methods to create comparators that compare objects using one of their fields, and default methods to modify the behavior of a given comparator. For instance, you can compare people using their last name; in case you have people with the same last name, you can then compare them using their first name; and in case you want to sort in the reverse order, just call reversed() on an existing comparator. One last thing: null values are always painful to handle when sorting — the Comparator interface also has nullsFirst and nullsLast methods.

Comparator<Person> byName = Comparator.comparing(Person::lastName)
        .thenComparing(Person::firstName)      // tie-breaker
        .reversed();                           // reverse order

Comparator<Person> withNulls = Comparator
        .nullsLast(Comparator.comparing(Person::name));
39

Can you cite functional interfaces that existed before Java 8?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Runnable, Comparator, Iterable.

Less short answer

And there are more. The definition of a functional interface is such that some existing interfaces became functional with Java 8 — and because you can implement a functional interface with a lambda, all the interfaces in your application that qualify can be implemented with lambdas without having to recompile them. That is why the @FunctionalInterface annotation is not mandatory. You want more? Executor. Even Comparable is a functional interface — "though I'm not sure you want to implement this one with a lambda".

Runnable r = () -> System.out.println("pre-Java 8, already a SAM!");
Comparator<String> c = (a, b) -> a.length() - b.length();
// no recompilation: lambdas apply to any old SAM interface

@FunctionalInterface  // optional: just validates the rule
interface MyCallback { void onEvent(String e); }
40

How can you open a file for writing some text?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a factory method for that in the Files class.

Less short answer

There are actually two patterns: the old one from Java IO and the less old one from Java NIO — and this is the one you should prefer. The exact pattern is Files.newBufferedWriter(path), which has several overloads: you can specify the charset used by your text file (the default being UTF-8), and you can specify some open options — whether you want to create the file if it does not exist, and if it does, whether you want to erase its content or append to it. One last word: what you get is still a regular BufferedWriter — you can still decorate it if you need.

try (var w = Files.newBufferedWriter(
        Path.of("log.txt"),
        StandardCharsets.UTF_8,                    // charset
        StandardOpenOption.CREATE,                 // create if missing
        StandardOpenOption.APPEND)) {              // append vs TRUNCATE
    w.write("a log line");
}
41

What is the difference between a sorted and an ordered collection?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Sorted means that you can compare your elements; ordered means that you can access them using an index. (as the video puts it)

Less short answer

It has to do with how you iterate over the elements of your collection. A sorted collection gives you the smallest element first, up to the largest one — adding an element may change the iteration order. An ordered collection, on the other hand, respects the order in which you add your elements: the first element is the first that has been added, up to the last one. One last word: in the framework, ordered collections are modeled by lists (your favorite implementation is ArrayList), and sorted collections are modeled by navigable sets, implemented by TreeSet.

List<Integer> ordered = new ArrayList<>(List.of(3, 1, 2));
// iteration: 3, 1, 2 — INSERTION order (index: get(0) == 3)

NavigableSet<Integer> sorted = new TreeSet<>(ordered);
// iteration: 1, 2, 3 — COMPARISON order
42

How can you open a file for reading binary data?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a factory method for that in the Files class.

Less short answer

The old patterns from Java 1.1, based on the decorator pattern, are still there — but you should use the Files factory class to create your readers, writers, input and output streams: the implementations you get are built on top of Java NIO, and they are going to save you some time. You can still use the decorator pattern if you need to build DataInputStream/DataOutputStream, gzip streams, or streams that mix text and data. One last word: the patterns from the Files factory class also give you fine control on the opening of files — on the charsets you can use to read your text files — and they are built to use the Path object instead of File objects, which is better.

try (var in = Files.newInputStream(Path.of("data.bin"))) {
    // decorate as you need:
    var data = new DataInputStream(new BufferedInputStream(in));
    int n = data.readInt();
    double d = data.readDouble();
}
// built on NIO, with Path (not File)
43

How can you print an array on the console?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Use the toString method from the Arrays class.

Less short answer

An array is an object, but with a default toString method that you cannot override — printing it directly gives you something you do not want (the hashcode). Call Arrays.toString(array): it prints the content of your array, calling toString on each element. If you are not happy with the formatting, you then need to iterate over the elements of the array and create the strings of characters you need. One last word: you can use arrays in the for-each pattern — the pattern works for any object that implements the Iterable interface... now, Iterable declares a forEach method, but you cannot call it on arrays (arrays do not implement Iterable).

int[] n = {1, 2, 3};
System.out.println(n);                    // [I@1b6d3586 (default: useless)
System.out.println(Arrays.toString(n));   // [1, 2, 3]
System.out.println(Arrays.deepToString(new int[][]{{1},{2,3}}));
                                                         // [[1], [2, 3]]
for (int x : n) { ... }                   // for-each works!
// n.forEach(...);                        // doesn't exist: not Iterable
44

How does a SortedSet work?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a set that keeps its elements sorted.

Less short answer

First point: it is a set — no duplicates; if you add two elements that are equal, the second one will not be added. Second point: the elements you add must be comparable — or, if they are not, you must provide a comparator when you create your sorted set. If you provide a comparator, it will be used even if your elements are comparable. And when you iterate over the elements, they come in increasing order. As of Java 6, you should favor a NavigableSet instead of a SortedSet — it gives you many more methods. One last word: the default implementation is TreeSet, which implements a red-black tree.

SortedSet<Integer> s = new TreeSet<>(Set.of(5, 1, 3));
s.add(1);                  // not added: duplicate
s.first();                 // 1 — always sorted

NavigableSet<Integer> nav = new TreeSet<>(Set.of(1, 3, 5, 7));
nav.ceiling(4);            // 5 (the first >= 4)
nav.headSet(5);            // [1, 3] — a view
// TreeSet = red-black tree, everything O(log n)
45

What pattern has been used to create the Java I/O API?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The Decorator pattern.

Less short answer

A structural pattern from the GoF book. The decorator is in fact a wrapper on an object that extends the class of that object — it may add more methods or change the way the existing methods work. Wrapping an existing object has a trick in the constructor: it takes an object of the superclass as a parameter — so it is both an extension and a composition. For instance, BufferedReader extends Reader and adds the readLine() method — plus the reading now works with a buffer. One last word: you should use the Files factory class to build the base objects you need to read and write content from files... but you can still decorate them to add features to the base objects you get.

// Decorator: extends Reader AND takes a Reader (extension + composition)
Reader base = Files.newBufferedReader(Path.of("a.txt"), UTF_8);
LineNumberReader withLines = new LineNumberReader(base); // +getLineNumber()
var withPushback = new PushbackReader(withLines);         // +unread()

// each layer adds behavior WITHOUT changing the interface
46

What are the different categories of design patterns?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Three categories: creational, structural, behavioral.

Less short answer

It refers to the Gang of Four book — a must-read for every developer. Some creational patterns: Factory, Builder, Singleton. Some structural patterns: Decorator, Facade, Proxy. Some behavioral patterns: Iterator, Template Method, the well-known Visitor. The GoF gives you C++ implementations — some of them are dated, but the ideas are still excellent. One last word: there are many patterns that are so widely used that you use them even if you don't know them — that is the case for the iterator pattern or the factory pattern.

List.of(1, 2, 3).iterator();            // Iterator (behavioral)
new BufferedReader(reader);             // Decorator (structural)
Optional.of(x);  List.of(...);          // Factory (creational)
StringBuilder b = new StringBuilder();  // Builder (creational)
47

How can you count how many times a letter appears in a string?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You need to iterate over the string in one way or another.

Less short answer

Two patterns. First, you can look for your letter in the string with indexOf, until you don't find it anymore — indexOf may take an index where it will start its search; that's a quick and efficient pattern. Second pattern: you can create a stream on the letters of the string, removing the letters that do not match and counting the result — efficient, more functional, more readable... "this is the one I prefer". One last word: the count() method returns a long that you can safely cast to an int — a string of characters is backed by an array, so there is no way you can have more than Integer.MAX_VALUE letters.

String s = "abracadabra";

// pattern 1: indexOf in a loop
int pos = 0, total = 0;
while ((pos = s.indexOf('a', pos)) >= 0) { total++; pos++; }  // 5

// pattern 2: stream (the presenter's favorite)
long n = s.chars().filter(c -> c == 'a').count();            // 5
48

What is a bucket in a map?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A cell in an array.

Less short answer

The HashMap class from the Collections framework is backed by an array. When you add a key to a HashMap, a special hash code is evaluated for that key to choose which cell of this array will receive the key-value pair. If there is already a different key-value pair in this cell, this is called a collision — and then a linked list is created to hold the pairs; and if there are too many, this linked list is actually replaced by a red-black tree. To minimize collisions, when the amount of key-value pairs reaches 75% of the size of the array, the content is copied into a new, larger array. One last word: this copying incurs a rehashing of all the keys present in the map, which may be costly — so be careful about that, and try to create maps with the right size.

Map<String, Integer> m = new HashMap<>();
m.put("ana", 1);      // hash("ana") & (n-1) -> a cell
// cell occupied by a different key? linked list
// more than 8 in the same cell? becomes a red-black tree (O(log n))
// 75% full (load factor)? resize + REHASH of everything

HashMap.newHashMap(1000);  // right size upfront: avoids rehashing
49

What does synchronized mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A keyword that prevents more than one thread from executing a given block of code at the same time.

Less short answer

Synchronization has to do with concurrent programming. This block of code can be a delimited block inside a method, or it can be the whole method, static or not. All synchronized blocks need an object that they use as a key — and you can use the same key for more than one synchronized block. In the case of a synchronized method, the key is the object itself — or the class if the method is static. There are limitations with synchronized blocks that you don't have with locks (ReentrantLock...) — which you can use instead. One last word: never expose your keys or your locks — it will help you prevent deadlocks, a situation that you absolutely want to avoid.

class Cache {
    private final Object key = new Object();

    public void put(String k, Integer v) {
        synchronized (key) { map.put(k, v); }  // block with its own key
    }
    public synchronized int size() { ... }     // key = this
}
// synchronized limitations: no tryLock, no timeout, not "fair"
// -> ReentrantLock (tryLock/timeout), always unlock() in the finally
50

What is the difference between a collection and a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A collection contains objects; a stream is empty.

Less short answer

A collection is meant to carry objects around, with methods to handle them. Most of the time, a stream is an empty object that you connect to a source of objects to process them — this source can be a collection, an array and many other things; you can even connect a stream to your own source of data. It will then process the elements one by one, lazily — and some methods like findAny or allMatch can interrupt the processing of these objects. One last word: there are actually two methods that need to remember the processed objects — distinct and sorted: if you use them, your stream will not be empty anymore.

List<String> names = List.of("a", "b");  // carries the objects

names.stream()                          // stream: empty, bound to a source
     .filter(s -> !s.isEmpty())
     .findAny();                        // short-circuit: stops early

// streams "with memory": distinct and sorted store the elements
51

What is the difference between a File and a Path?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A File is a class; a Path is an interface.

Less short answer

The File class is from Java 1.0; Path is from Java NIO, released with Java 7. An instance of File is the same no matter what file system you are using — Path being an interface, the instance you build depends on the file system, and you can get attributes that are specific to these file systems, like the security attributes. As of now, you should favor the use of Path objects over File objects. One last word: creating an instance of File or Path does not create the corresponding file or directory on your disk — you need to call createNewFile() on the File class, or the Files.createFile(path) factory method, to do that.

File f = new File("data.txt");    // 1995: a single class
Path p = Path.of("data.txt");     // 2011 (Java 7): an interface

// actually create it on disk:
f.createNewFile();                // legacy
Files.createFile(p);              // preferred
Files.getAttribute(p, "size");    // attributes of the real FS (zip!)
52

What is the difference between a checked and an unchecked exception?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You need to write special code to handle the checked exception — not for unchecked exceptions.

Less short answer

All your exceptions extend the Exception class. If an exception actually extends RuntimeException, then it is an unchecked exception; if it does not, then it is a checked exception. You need to handle your checked exceptions, either by catching them or by declaring them in the throws clause of your methods and constructors. You do not need to do that for unchecked exceptions. One last word: all these classes extend the Throwable class, also extended by the Error class — errors are also unchecked exceptions, but reserved for serious problems like OutOfMemoryError or StackOverflowError.

void readFile() throws IOException { ... }  // checked: declare or catch
try { readFile(); } catch (IOException e) { ... }

// unchecked (RuntimeException): no obligation
List.of().get(0);       // IndexOutOfBoundsException
Integer.parseInt("x");  // NumberFormatException
// Errors (also unchecked): OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError
53

What is a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object that implements the map/filter/reduce pattern.

Less short answer

The stream consumes elements from a source and can run several operations. These operations are organized in a pipeline, where each element is passed from one step of the pipeline to the next one. Mapping an element consists in transforming it; filtering consists in deciding, based on a predicate, whether this element should be transmitted or not; reducing may consist in many things — summing the elements, extracting the min or max, or adding them to a list or a map. One last word: the Stream API allows you to conduct these computations in parallel — still, you need to be careful with that: as always when it comes to optimizations, measure, don't guess.

double avg = orders.stream()          // source
        .filter(Order::paid)          // filter: pass or not
        .mapToDouble(Order::amount)   // map: transforms
        .average()                    // reduce: aggregates
        .orElse(0);

orders.parallelStream()...            // parallel: measure first!
54

What is the hash code of an object?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A mapping of an object to an integer.

Less short answer

The idea behind the hash code is to represent any object within an int, so that you can quickly tell if two objects are different by just comparing their hash codes. Indeed, the specification says that two objects that are equal must have the same hash code. But it turns out that two different objects may also have the same hash code. So: different hash codes ⇒ the objects cannot be equal, for sure; same hash code ⇒ you need to compare them with equals to decide. One last word: there are many ways of computing hash codes, some better than others — if you're not sure about what you need, relying on your IDE to generate this hashCode method is probably your safest choice.

record Customer(String name, String taxId) {
    // hashCode and equals generated by the IDE (Objects.hash):
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(name, taxId); }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) { ... }
}
// equal -> SAME hash (mandatory)
// different hash -> definitely different (quick shortcut)
// same hash -> equals decides
55

What is an ExecutorService?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object to which you can submit tasks to be executed in another thread.

Less short answer

ExecutorService is an interface, part of the java.util.concurrent API added to the JDK 5 in 2004. It is the preferred way to run tasks in another thread — in fact, you shouldn't be creating threads by calling new Thread anymore. A task is created by the user, and can be either a Runnable or a Callable — both implementable with a lambda expression. Submitting a task gives you a Future object — you can use it to get a result, an exception if something goes wrong, or to interrupt the running of this task. One last word: an ExecutorService is sometimes called a "pool of threads" — and most of the time it is; but it can also create threads on demand, which is what it does for virtual threads.

ExecutorService ex = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor();

Future<Integer> f = ex.submit(() -> compute());  // Callable -> Future
f.get(1, TimeUnit.SECONDS);  // result OR exception
f.cancel(true);

ex.shutdown();               // the lifecycle managed by it
56

What is an Iterator?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object used to iterate over the elements of a collection.

Less short answer

Iterator is an interface with three methods. hasNext — tells you if there are more objects to iterate over; you should call this method first. Then, if it returns true, you can call next to get the next object and move the iterator one step. It also has a remove method — which can throw an UnsupportedOperationException, depending on your collection being immutable. Most of the time, you use iterators for collections — but iterators can actually be created without them: for instance, you can create a range iterator, that can iterate over a range of integers. One last word: the iterator pattern is actually a pattern from the Gang of Four book — did I already mention that this book is a must-read?

for (Iterator<String> it = list.iterator(); it.hasNext(); ) {
    String s = it.next();
    if (s.isEmpty()) it.remove();   // safe removal: through the iterator
}

// an iterator WITHOUT a collection:
Iterator<Integer> range = IntStream.range(0, 10).iterator();
57

What are the 4 Java I/O base classes?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Reader, Writer, InputStream and OutputStream.

Less short answer

These are the 4 abstract classes that define the basic operations for reading and writing characters and binary data. They were designed in the mid-90s — and you should not use them directly anymore. The preferred patterns are the factory methods from the Files class: newBufferedReader/newBufferedWriter for the reading and writing of characters, and newInputStream/newOutputStream for the reading and writing of binary data. These factory methods give you instances built on top of Java NIO. One last word: you can still decorate the instances you get, following the general pattern of the Java I/O API.

// the 4 cornerstones (mid-90s): Reader, Writer, InputStream, OutputStream

// today: Files is the entry point (on top of NIO)
try (var r = Files.newBufferedReader(Path.of("a.txt"), UTF_8);
     var w = Files.newBufferedWriter(Path.of("b.txt"), UTF_8);
     var in = Files.newInputStream(Path.of("data.bin"))) {
    // and decorate if you need:
    var data = new DataInputStream(new BufferedInputStream(in));
}
58

How many objects can you put in a collection?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It depends on the implementation you have.

Less short answer

An ArrayList is backed by an array, so the limit would be Integer.MAX_VALUE. You may be thinking that a LinkedList would allow you to store more objects — but in fact, many things will fail for a LinkedList with more objects than Integer.MAX_VALUE: a LinkedList counts its elements with a field called size, which is an int — bad things will happen if size reaches Integer.MAX_VALUE. Its toArray() method will not work very well either, since arrays are also indexed by ints. One last word: the same limit also exists for maps — but don't worry: there is little chance that you can reach that limit without reaching other limitations in your application first.

// ArrayList: internal array -> limit is Integer.MAX_VALUE
// LinkedList: the size field is an int -> the same ceiling
// size() returns an int: at most 2^31-1 = 2,147,483,647 elements
// (in practice: the heap runs out MUCH earlier — use a DB/off-heap beyond)
59

What is the maximum length of a String in Java?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It cannot be longer than Integer.MAX_VALUE — because it is backed by an array.

Less short answer

First, that would be a very long string indeed. And second, the number of characters may be different from the length of this array — the reason being that many characters are actually encoded on more than one byte. One last word: the String class changed in 2017, with Java 9: the internal array used to be an array of chars, 16 bits — it is now an array of bytes, 8 bits. This optimization is called Compact Strings, and it can tremendously reduce the memory footprint of your application. One more reason to upgrade to the latest versions of the JDK.

// limit: Integer.MAX_VALUE (~2.1 billion) — length() is an int
String s = "a".repeat(Integer.MAX_VALUE);  // if memory allows...

// Java 9+: internal byte[] (Compact Strings)
// "abc" -> 3 bytes (Latin-1), not 3 x 2 bytes as before
60

How does a set know that it already contains an element?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It uses the hash code of this object; if it finds something, then it calls the equals method.

Less short answer

A HashSet stores its objects in the cells of an array. The index of the cell for a given object is computed from the hash code of this object. This process is very fast because it does not depend on the amount of elements you have in your set. Of course, there is a mechanism to handle collisions — that is, if two different objects happen to have the same hash code. One last word: because of that, a HashSet will not work if you override equals for your object without overriding hashCode. And one very last word: do not mutate an object once it has been added to a set, because you will not be able to find it again.

Set<Person> set = new HashSet<>();
set.add(new Person("ana", 30));     // hash(ana) -> cell 7, say

set.contains(new Person("ana", 30)); // same hash -> equals -> true

// mutated the object (hash changed)? search goes to the NEW cell: miss!
// overrode equals without hashCode? equal objects in different cells
61

How is Arrays.asList() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Arrays.asList wraps an array and exposes it as a list.

Less short answer

You can call Arrays.asList with a vararg — but you can also pass an array. Avoid passing an array of primitive types: what you will get is a list with a single element — the array itself. If you modify the array you pass, it will modify the list too, because — as said — there is no defensive copy of this array. You can modify the elements of this list (set), but you cannot add or remove elements from it — because, you know, it is actually an array. One last word: what you get with Arrays.asList is not an unmodifiable list, because you can still change its elements; if what you need is an unmodifiable list, you may consider using List.of.

String[] a = {"a", "b"};
List<String> l = Arrays.asList(a);   // wrapper: no copy!

a[0] = "X";                          // l is now [X, b] (same array)
l.set(1, "Y");                       // changes the array too
// l.add("c");                       // UnsupportedOperationException

int[] n = {1, 2};
Arrays.asList(n);                    // a List<int[]> with 1 element!
62

Can you cite some methods from the Stream API?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The three basic methods: map, filter and reduce.

Less short answer

Odds are that you will not use reduce much — you have many specialized methods to reduce the elements of your stream, like toList, forEach, findFirst or findAny. Other intermediate methods you can cite: flatMap, of course, distinct, sorted. Two things that you absolutely need to avoid: using peek unless you need to debug your stream — peek is useless in production — and second, don't do any side effects in any method of the Stream API; it will put you in trouble. One last word: you can also reduce your stream with the Collector API — there are a bunch of available collectors in the Collectors factory class, and you can even create your own.

stream.map(fn).filter(p).sorted().distinct().toList();
stream.findFirst(); stream.anyMatch(p);  // specialized reductions
stream.peek(s -> log(s));                // debugging only!

// collectors: ready-made or your own
stream.collect(Collectors.groupingBy(...));
Collector.of(supplier, acc, comb, fin);  // yours!
63

What is the var keyword in Java?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A keyword you can use in methods to avoid having to specify the type of a local variable.

Less short answer

var is for local variables only — you cannot use it for fields, for instance. You may use it for anonymous classes: in that case, you will have access to the methods you added in your anonymous class, that are not defined in the original class. The type of an anonymous class is called a non-denotable type — you can add fields and methods to it and access them if you declare your variable with the var keyword. One last word: you can use your IDE to see the type inferred by the compiler — and sometimes the result is unexpected.

var list = new ArrayList<String>();     // local: OK

var counter = new Object() {            // anonymous + var: full type!
    int value = 0;                      // non-denotable
    void inc() { value++; }
};
counter.inc(); counter.inc();           // members of the type itself

// var field;  // ERROR: locals only
64

How can you create an unmodifiable list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

With the List.of factory method.

Less short answer

There are actually several ways to do that, but List.of is the preferred one. Be careful with the behavior of Collections.unmodifiableList(): what it gives you is an unmodifiable view of a list — with no defensive copy of the list you pass as an argument; so if you modify that list, this modification will be seen through the view. On the other hand, you can pass an array to List.of — it will be copied, so no funny behavior there. One last word: be careful, because List.of does not accept null values (why would you put null values in a list?) — you will get an exception at runtime if you pass an array with a null value in it.

List<String> original = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a"));
List<String> view = Collections.unmodifiableList(original);
original.add("b");             // the view now shows [a, b]!

List<String> copy = List.of("a", "b");   // a copy: truly immutable
// copy.add("x");          -> UnsupportedOperationException
// List.of("a", null);     -> NPE (nulls, never)
65

How can you start a thread?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a start method on the Thread class.

Less short answer

Two things. First, a thread is either built on a Runnable or is itself a Runnable — and in both cases, a common mistake is to call the run method instead of start: run will execute the runnable, but in the current thread, not in a new one. Second, calling start is okay for testing, learning and exploring how threads work. In a production environment, you should not launch your threads by hand — you should use the ExecutorService pattern instead. One last word: JDK 21 adds new factory methods on the Thread class to create both platform threads and virtual threads. (Another video.)

Thread t = new Thread(() -> process());
t.start();          // a NEW thread executes run()
// t.run();         // same thread! classic mistake

// JDK 21: factories
Thread.ofPlatform().name("worker-").start(task);
Thread.ofVirtual().start(task);

// production: Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()
66

How can you create a file on a disk with Java I/O?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Use the createFile factory method from the Files class.

Less short answer

There is also createNewFile on the File class — the legacy way of creating files. And creating a writer or an output stream on a file can also create this file for you — once again, the preferred pattern is the Files class: newBufferedWriter / newOutputStream take open options: READ, WRITE, APPEND, CREATE, CREATE_NEW, among others. One last word: depending on your file system, you can also pass file attributes to the file creation — to handle security, for instance.

Files.createFile(Path.of("new.txt"));       // fails if it already exists
Files.writeString(Path.of("log.txt"), "hi",
        StandardOpenOption.CREATE, StandardOpenOption.APPEND);

new File("old.txt").createNewFile();        // legacy (boolean)

Files.setAttribute(p, "dos:hidden", true);  // attributes (FS-dependent)
67

What is the class named Class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The class that models classes.

Less short answer

The JVM creates an instance of this Class class for each class it loads. The simplest way to get a reference on a Class object is to call getClass() on any object — but you can also use the class itself directly, like String.class, or call Class.forName(...) to get a reference from a name. The Class class is the entry point of the Reflection API, which gives you a way to manipulate objects reflectively — a technique that is used by many frameworks.

Class<String> c1 = String.class;
Class<?> c2 = "hello".getClass();
Class<?> c3 = Class.forName("java.lang.String");
c1 == c2 && c2 == c3;   // true: one instance per loaded class

c1.getName();           // "java.lang.String"
c1.getMethods();        // reflection: methods...
c1.isRecord(); c1.isSealed(); c1.isEnum();
68

How can you find duplicates in a list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It really depends on what you need.

Less short answer

You can check if a list has duplicates by putting this list in a Set and comparing the size of the set with the size of the list. You could also use a stream, calling the distinct() method on it and comparing the counts — which also builds a set internally. If you need to find the duplicate elements themselves, then you need to build a counting map: create a stream on the list and group the elements by themselves (groupingBy(identity())), counting them with the counting() collector — the ones that go past 1 are the duplicates.

List<String> l = List.of("a", "b", "a", "c", "b");

// any duplicates?
new HashSet<>(l).size() < l.size();               // true

// which ones?
Map<String, Long> counts = l.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(s -> s, Collectors.counting()));
counts.entrySet().stream()
     .filter(e -> e.getValue() > 1)
     .map(Map.Entry::getKey)
     .forEach(System.out::println);              // a b
69

What is a sealed type?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A type that knows all of its extensions.

Less short answer

It can be any type — an interface, a class, or an abstract class. You need to declare it with the sealed keyword, and then declare the permitted types with the permits clause. Now, there are constraints on these permitted types. First, they need to be declared in the same package — or in the same module. Second, they need to be either final, sealed, or declared non-sealed. Enums and records are actually final types — so you cannot create an open hierarchy by accident; the non-sealed declaration is there for that, when you do want to reopen it on purpose. One last word: sealed types are enforced both at compile time and at runtime.

public sealed interface Shape
        permits Circle, Square, Polygon { }

record Circle(double radius) implements Shape { }   // final by nature
non-sealed interface Polygon extends Shape { }      // reopened on purpose

// a switch on Shape: exhaustiveness checked by the compiler!
70

How is List.of() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It takes an array (or varargs) and makes a defensive copy to create an unmodifiable list.

Less short answer

You can use it with a vararg to create a pre-filled list — but you can also pass an array. In that case, remember: if it is an array of primitive types, what you get is a list with one element — your whole array. This list implementation does not accept null values: sometimes your IDE can warn you about that, but in certain cases it cannot — and you will get an exception at runtime. One last word: you can also create sets with Set.of, that works in the same way as List.of — defensive copy, null values not allowed.

List<String> l = List.of("a", "b");      // defensive copy, immutable

String[] names = {"a", "b"};
List<String> l2 = List.of(names);        // array copied
names[0] = "X";                          // l2 still holds "a" (defensive!)

int[] n = {1, 2};
List.of(n);                              // a List<int[]> with 1 element!
List<Integer> wrong = List.of("a", null); // NPE at runtime
71

What is the difference between Runnable and Thread?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Runnable is an interface; Thread is a class.

Less short answer

Runnable is a model for a task — you can implement it with a lambda that takes nothing and returns nothing; you can execute this lambda by calling its run method. You can then create a Thread object, passing your runnable as an argument, and execute this runnable in that thread by calling the start method of the thread object. Be careful not to use the thread.run() method: it will execute your runnable, but in the current thread. One last word: this pattern is okay for playing with threads and understanding how they work. In a real application, you should use the ExecutorService pattern instead of this one.

Runnable task = () -> System.out.println("hello from thread " +
        Thread.currentThread().getName());

Thread t = new Thread(task);
t.start();   // a new thread calls run()
// t.run();  // runs on the CURRENT thread — classic trap!

// production: Executors.newFixedThreadPool(4).submit(task);
72

What is the difference between map and flatMap?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

These are two methods from the Stream API, to map objects of a given type to objects of another type.

Less short answer

map is super simple: it takes a function — like String::length, that maps strings of characters to their length. flatMap works differently: it maps one-to-many relationships — the mapping function should itself return a stream of something, and these streams are flattened by the flatMap method. For instance, given a stream of countries, you can flat-map this stream to a stream of cities. One last word: a map does not change the number of objects your stream processes, whereas a flatMap does — it can even produce zero elements.

Stream.of("java", "kotlin")
      .map(String::length)                 // 1 -> 1 (4, 6)

Stream.of(brazil, france)
      .flatMap(c -> c.cities().stream())   // 1 -> N
      .forEach(System.out::println);

Stream.of("a", "b")
      .flatMap(s -> Stream.empty());       // 1 -> 0: an empty stream!
73

How is synchronization working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It works with a key — the object with which you synchronize your block of code.

Less short answer

This key is called the monitor. When a thread reaches a synchronized block or method, it asks this object for its key. If the key is available, it takes it and runs the code; if not, it will have to wait until the key comes back. You absolutely want to avoid situations where the key never comes back — usually because of deadlocks, but not only. In the case of an instance method, the object that holds the key is simply this; and in the case of a static method, the object is the class. One last word: try to avoid synchronizing on objects that are public — that's a good rule to prevent deadlocks, a situation you absolutely want to avoid.

class Resource {
    private final Object key = new Object();  // PRIVATE!
    void use() {
        synchronized (key) { update(); }   // takes the key, uses, returns it
    }
    synchronized void simple() { ... }     // key = this
    static synchronized void st() { ... }  // key = Resource.class
}
74

What is a generic type?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A type that is parameterized over types.

Less short answer

Once defined along with the class, you can use this parameter in the class itself to define instance fields or instance methods — and a single type can be parameterized over several types. Generics are massively used in the JDK: the Collections framework, the Stream API — you can check these classes to see how you can use them, and how they were created. One last word: the type T is actually erased (type erasure) — it is replaced by Object at runtime. So there is a number of things you cannot do in your code: you cannot try to get the class of T, because it would just return Object; you cannot call new T() or new T[] either.

class Pair<A, B> {                  // several parameters
    private final A first; private final B second;
    Pair(A a, B b) { first = a; second = b; }
}

// type erasure at runtime:
new ArrayList<String>().getClass() == new ArrayList<Integer>().getClass();
// true! the T disappears (becomes Object)

// these do NOT exist: new T(), T.class, new T[10] (without tricks)
75

How can you stop a Thread?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You can't — and you should not.

Less short answer

There are two methods on the Thread class, stop and suspend, that have been deprecated in Java 2 (1998) — that is more than 25 years ago. They now throw an UnsupportedOperationException, so don't even think about using them. Why? The answer is actually not that simple. In short, they have been deprecated mainly for security reasons: being able to stop a thread could put your application in an unpredictable state, something you absolutely want to avoid. One last word: you are not supposed to start or stop your threads by hand anyway. The pattern you should be using is the ExecutorService pattern, that takes care of your thread lifecycle for you.

Thread t = new Thread(task);
// t.stop();    // deprecated 1998; today: UnsupportedOperationException
// t.suspend(); // same

t.interrupt();   // cooperative cancellation + checks in the task

// production: the executor manages the lifecycle
ExecutorService ex = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(4);
ex.shutdown();   // graceful end
76

How can you remove elements from a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Use the filter method. (that is the short answer — and almost all of it)

Less short answer

But be careful: a stream does not contain any object — it consumes objects from a source. So saying "removing objects from a stream" is actually not correct: what you are really doing is telling your stream not to process certain elements. Filtering uses a predicate that tests certain properties of the object and decides if it should be further processed or not — in the video's example, processing only the non-empty strings.

Stream.of("", "a", "", "b")
      .filter(s -> !s.isEmpty())        // "removing" = not processing empties
      .forEach(System.out::println);    // a b

// to really REMOVE (mutating), it's on the collection:
list.removeIf(String::isEmpty);
77

How is Set.of() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It builds the set of its arguments — if there are no duplicates among them.

Less short answer

The arguments are added one by one to an array of the right size, using their hash code to detect any duplicates — if a duplicate is detected, an IllegalArgumentException is immediately thrown. This method only requires one pass over your data, and because duplicates are not allowed in your arguments, it can create an array of the right size upfront, making the memory consumption minimal. So all this is pretty optimal. One last word: as usual, be careful if you pass an array of primitive types, because you will get a set with a single element: your array. Which is usually not what you want.

Set.of("a", "b", "c");              // immutable set, one pass
Set.of(1, 2, 2);                    // IllegalArgumentException!

int[] numbers = {1, 2, 3};
Set.of(numbers);                    // a Set<int[]> with 1 element (the array!)
Set.of(Arrays.stream(numbers).boxed().toArray(Integer[]::new)); // right
78

How can you join strings with a separator?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You have a join factory method for that, directly on the String class.

Less short answer

There are two overloads of this method. They both take a delimiter as a first argument; then you can pass a vararg (or an array) — that's the first overload — or an Iterable, typically a collection — that's the second one. If you need to add a prefix and a suffix to your string of characters, then you can check the StringJoiner class — which you can also reuse if you have many strings in the process.
