One last word: you can also check the Collectors.joining pattern, which does the same for the Stream API — that's useful if you need to create this string lazily, without you having to buffer everything in a collection.

String.join(", ", "a", "b", "c");              // varargs
String.join("-", List.of("2026", "10", "04")); // Iterable

new StringJoiner(", ", "[", "]")               // prefix and suffix
        .add("a").add("b").toString();         // "[a, b]"

stream.collect(Collectors.joining(", "));      // lazy, in the Stream API
79

How can you modify a field using the Reflection API?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You can use the set method of the Field class to do that.

Less short answer

This method takes the instance you want to operate on, and the value you want to set. If you do not have access to this field — because it is private, for instance — you can still change it by calling field.setAccessible(true). It does not make the field public, but it suppresses the access checks made by the language. One last word though: you can actually mutate final fields in regular classes with this pattern — which is really annoying. Records are protected against that: you cannot mutate them. One more reason to use records wherever you can.

Field f = Config.class.getDeclaredField("timeout");
f.setAccessible(true);              // suppresses the check (JPMS: needs opens)
f.set(config, 5000);                // writes through reflection

// in a regular class: even FINAL is mutable this way (annoying!)
// in a record: the field cannot be reassigned — protected!
80

How can you declare a generic type on a static method?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You need to define the type parameter along with the method declaration.

Less short answer

The type parameters you define on the class are only seen by the instance fields and methods — not by the static members. Suppose you have a class Box<T> and you want to define a static method copy: to define the type parameter for this method, you need to add it after the static keyword. Note also that if you have several static methods, each one needs to declare its own type parameter. One last word: you have many examples of that in the JDK — in the Collections class, for instance. They also use this wildcard syntax, ? super T and ? extends T. (Another video.)

class Box<T> {
    T value;                         // T: for INSTANCE members only

    static <U> void copy(Box<U> from, Box<? super U> to) {
        //  ^^^ the METHOD's type parameter (after static)
        to.value = from.value;
    }
    static <V> V identity(V v) { return v; }  // each method, its own
}
81

What does passing by value mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It means that what you pass to a method is the value — not a reference to this value.

Less short answer

The consequence is that if your method modifies the argument it gets, the calling code does not see this modification. If what you pass is a primitive type, changing it in your method does not change the value in your calling code. If what you pass is a reference (to an object), changing the reference itself does not change it in the calling code either — but if you change the content of the referenced object, then this change is seen by the calling code. One last word: Java passes by value. Always, no exception (the reference's "value" is what gets copied).

void try(int x, StringBuilder sb, Person p) {
    x = 99;                    // nothing changes outside (primitive: copy)
    sb = new StringBuilder();  // nothing changes outside (reference: copy)
    sb.append("x");            // THIS the caller sees (object content)!
    p.setName("new");          // and this too
}
int n = 1; var sb = new StringBuilder(); var p = new Person("old");
try(n, sb, p);
// n == 1; sb contains "x"; p.name == "new"
82

What is the difference between an intermediate and a terminal operation?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An intermediate operation returns a stream; a terminal operation returns something else — including void.

Less short answer

The stream processes the data from the source only when it executes its terminal operation — if you do not call a terminal operation, no data is processed. And you can only call one intermediate or terminal operation on a given stream object — it makes storing a stream in a local variable useless. One last word: some terminal operations do not even call the intermediate operations of your stream — one more reason why you should never do side effects in an intermediate operation: there are situations where they will not be executed.

var s = list.stream().map(this::transform);  // nothing happened yet!
s.filter(...).count();                       // NOW it processes
// s.anything();  // IllegalStateException: stream already consumed

Stream.of(1, 2, 3).map(n -> { save(n); return n; }).count();
// count() with no need for the map: the side effects may NOT run!
83

What list can you pass to a method that takes a List of Number?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

List<Number> — and certainly not List<Integer>, even though Integer extends Number.

Less short answer

Even if a type U extends a type T, a List<U> does not extend List<T>. The reason: within the method that takes a List of Number, you could add a Float to that list — but then, if you passed a List of Integer at runtime, you would actually end up trying to add a Float to a list of integers, which would cause an exception. One last word: you could change your method and declare that it takes a List<? extends Number> — and in that case, you will be able to pass lists of Integer, Double and the like (but writes become forbidden).

void process(List<Number> numbers) {
    numbers.add(1.5f);       // a Float in a Number: legitimate!
}
process(new ArrayList<Number>());     // OK
// process(new ArrayList<Integer>()); // does NOT compile — and it's great:
// otherwise: a Float inside a List<Integer>!

void processAny(List<? extends Number> nums) {
    double s = nums.stream().mapToDouble(Number::doubleValue).sum();
    // nums.add(anything);  // writing is forbidden by the wildcard
}
84

What is a deadlock?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A situation where a first thread holds lock A and needs lock B to carry on with its task — and a second thread is holding lock B and is waiting for lock A to carry on with its task.

Less short answer

This situation is really, really bad: most of the time, your solution will be to reboot your application. It can also occur with a second lock that is outside of your application — in your database, for instance. One last word — one word of advice to avoid that: never expose your locks as public elements. Always use private fields to store your locks. It will help you most of the time... but not always, unfortunately.

class Resource {
    private final Object lockA = new Object();  // PRIVATE!
    private final Object lockB = new Object();

    // CONSISTENT acquisition order in ALL the methods:
    void one() { synchronized (lockA) { synchronized (lockB) { ... } } }
    void two() { synchronized (lockA) { synchronized (lockB) { ... } } }
    // two() with the order inverted = classic deadlock
}
85

How can you create an object instance of a given class using the Reflection API?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that: getDeclaredConstructor() gives you a reference on the empty constructor of this class — call newInstance() on it.

Less short answer

The pattern that consists in calling newInstance directly on the class itself has been deprecated in Java 9, mostly because it swallows the exceptions of the constructor. If your class does not have an empty constructor, this pattern throws a NoSuchMethodException. One last word: you can also get a reference on the other constructors of your class by passing the classes of their parameters to the getDeclaredConstructor(...) method.

Object o = Customer.class
        .getDeclaredConstructor()          // the empty constructor
        .newInstance();                    // JDK 9+: the pattern

// with arguments:
Object p = Order.class
        .getDeclaredConstructor(String.class, int.class)
        .newInstance("abc", 3);

// Customer.class.newInstance();  // deprecated since Java 9
86

What is the difference between a collection and a list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A list is ordered; a collection is not.

Less short answer

"Ordered" means that the order in which you iterate over the elements is stable from one iteration to the other, and corresponds to the order in which you added the elements to your list. It is the case for all lists, because that's the specification. But it's not the case for a HashSet — and because HashSet is a collection, not all collections have this behavior: adding elements to a HashSet may change the iteration order of the other elements; it is not even stable from one iteration to the other. One last word: the consequence is that you can access the elements of your list using an index — which would not make sense for sets.

List<String> l = List.of("a", "b", "c");
l.get(1);                   // "b" — index: forever!

Set<String> s = new HashSet<>(Set.of("a", "b", "c"));
// s.get(...)? doesn't exist; iteration order may change on insertion
87

What is the difference between toList() and Collectors.toList()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Both are terminal operations of the Stream API. Collectors.toList() produces an ArrayList; toList() produces an unmodifiable list.

Less short answer

The fact that Collectors.toList() produces an ArrayList is actually not specified — but it is the case. There are cases where toList() is actually more than a convenience — it is more efficient: in a case where you know how many elements your stream is going to process, toList() can create an array of the right size upfront, whereas collect(toList()) relies on the automatic growing of your ArrayList. One last word: the Stream.toList() method produces an unmodifiable list that accepts null values — not all unmodifiable lists do: List.of doesn't, for instance.

List<Integer> mutable = stream.collect(Collectors.toList());
mutable.add(42);                      // OK (an ArrayList in practice)

List<Integer> fixed = stream.toList(); // JDK 16+
// fixed.add(42);                      // UnsupportedOperationException
List<Integer> withNull = Stream.of(1, null, 3).toList(); // nulls OK!
// List.of(1, null);                   // NPE: List.of doesn't accept
88

Can you compare integers and doubles with a comparator of numbers?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Yes.

Less short answer

This is fine, because what you are defining is a method that takes two parameters of type Number — and it's fine to call it with any extension of Number. Actually, you could use such a comparator to define the comparison between integers and floating point numbers, for instance. Such a comparator is also okay to sort any list of Integers or Doubles, because all these types extend Number. One last word: don't make your comparator a Comparator<? extends Number> — because in that case you will not be able to call it with any other simple type. There is a good reason for that... but that would be for another video.

Comparator<Number> byValue = Comparator
        .comparingDouble(Number::doubleValue);

byValue.compare(3, 2.5);              // int vs double: OK!
List<Integer> is = ...; is.sort(byValue);
List<Double>  ds = ...; ds.sort(byValue);

// Comparator<? extends Number> c = ...;
// c.compare(1, 2);   // doesn't compile! wildcard: arguments out
89

How can you model a concurrent task in Java?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You have two interfaces for that: Runnable and Callable.

Less short answer

Both are functional interfaces, so you can implement them with lambdas. Neither of them takes any parameter. Runnable does not return anything and does not throw any checked exception; a Callable, on the other hand, can return a value and throw an exception. Runnable is the only task you can pass to the constructor or the builder of a Thread; Callable and Runnable can be passed to an ExecutorService or to a StructuredTaskScope. One last word: when you submit a task to an ExecutorService, you get a Future object in return — something we will talk more about in the future, precisely.

Runnable t1 = () -> process();                 // void
Callable<Integer> t2 = () -> compute();        // returns, throws

Thread.ofPlatform().start(t1);                 // Runnable only here
executor.submit(t1);                           // Future<?>
Future<Integer> f = executor.submit(t2);       // Future<Integer>

try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnFailure()) {
    scope.fork(t2);                            // Callable in the scope
    scope.join();
}
90

What is an annotation?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface with methods on which you can define default values.

Less short answer

You can define your own annotations easily, with methods that may return default values. Along with your annotation, you need to define two things: where you can add this annotation in your code, and when you want to access it. There are currently 12 places (targets) where you can add an annotation — fields, classes, methods, or parameters among them. Then you can declare that you will need to use your annotation at runtime, only when your class is loaded, or at compile time. One last word: at runtime, you need to use the Reflection API to get the annotations added to an element of your code — a technique used by many frameworks: Hibernate or Spring Boot, just to name a few.

@Target({ElementType.TYPE, ElementType.METHOD})   // where (12 options)
@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)               // when: runtime
public @interface Audited {
    String user() default "system";
}

@Audited(user = "ana")
class Service { }
Service.class.getAnnotation(Audited.class);   // reflection reads at runtime
91

What is a question mark extends T?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a type called a wildcard, that has been created to allow a parameterized class to accept extensions of its parameter.

Less short answer

Suppose you have two types, T and U, and U extends T: a class parameterized on U does not extend the same class parameterized on T. Look at this sum method: it won't accept a List<Double>. We can fix that with a List<? extends Number>, because List<Double> extends List<? extends Number>. One last word: this technique is extensively used in the Collections framework and in the lambda world — comparators, consumers, predicates, functions: they all use wildcards.

static double sum(List<Number> nums) { ... }
sum(List.of(1.5, 2.5));   // ERROR! List<Double> is not List<Number>

static double sumRight(List<? extends Number> nums) {   // wildcard!
    return nums.stream().mapToDouble(Number::doubleValue).sum();
}
sumRight(List.of(1.5, 2.5));   // OK: reading from extends
sumRight(List.of(1, 2));       // OK: Integer too
92

What is a constructor?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An element of a class that is called when an instance of this class is built.

Less short answer

Be careful — a constructor is not a method: it does not have a return type, and it is not compiled in the same way. You can have several overloads of a constructor in a class; all they need is to declare different parameters. Constructors are the right place for validation — to prevent corrupted objects from being built. One last word: you can write compact constructors in records — a very nice and very simple syntax to write constructors that only do validation. One more reason to use records wherever you want.

class Age {
    private final int value;
    Age(int value) {                          // validation at the source
        if (value < 0 || value > 150)
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("age: " + value);
        this.value = value;
    }
}

record Range(int min, int max) {
    Range {                                    // compact: validation only
        if (min > max) throw new IllegalArgumentException();
    }
}
93

What is a short-circuit method?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Several methods from the Stream API are called short-circuit methods.

Less short answer

These methods may interrupt the consumption of the elements of your source. There are two intermediate operations — limit and takeWhile — and five terminal operations — allMatch, anyMatch, noneMatch, findFirst and findAny. Because elements are processed lazily, one by one by the Stream API, short-circuit operations can prevent some elements from being read from your source — which may be really bad if you are doing side effects in your intermediate operations. One last word: doing side effects in a stream intermediate or terminal operation is always a bad idea. Don't do it.

Stream.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6)
      .peek(n -> saveToDatabase(n))   // SIDE EFFECT: 5 and 6 NEVER run!
      .takeWhile(n -> n < 4)
      .toList();

list.stream().anyMatch(this::critical);   // stops at the first true
94

What is an atomic variable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A wrapper on a primitive type or a reference, with methods to mutate it in a thread-safe and efficient way.

Less short answer

Atomic variables are actually mapping CPU operations, also called CAS operations — CAS means compare-and-swap. For instance, on an AtomicInteger, you can call the incrementAndGet() method: it increments the internal value and returns it — atomically. This operation is thread-safe and does not rely on synchronization, which prevents thread contention. One last word: atomic references are used to implement two thread-safe structures from the Collections framework — ConcurrentSkipListMap, which is a map, and ConcurrentSkipListSet, which is a set — all this without any synchronization.

AtomicInteger counter = new AtomicInteger();
counter.incrementAndGet();          // CAS: read-compare-swap in 1 step
counter.compareAndSet(5, 10);       // swaps only if the value is 5
counter.updateAndGet(n -> n * 2);   // atomic function

// no lock, no contention — the basis of ConcurrentSkipListMap/Set
95

What is a record?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something you should use all the time.

Less short answer

It is a class that carries some immutable state, declared along with its declaration — this state is called the components of your record. A record cannot extend anything and cannot be extended, but it can implement any interface. It cannot declare an instance field (static fields are okay), and it can declare any method. The compiler gives you a constructor, accessors — that you can rewrite yourself — and toString, equals and hashCode for free. You can do some validation, and deserialization calls this constructor — so no corrupted records in your application. One last word: use records wherever and whenever you can.

public record Ssn(String value) {
    Ssn {                                        // guaranteed validation
        if (!value.matches("\\d{9}"))
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("invalid SSN");
    }
    // constructor + value() + equals/hashCode + toString: free!
}
96A

Why should you favor composition over inheritance?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Because it's written in the manual. (the presenter's joke: "you read the f***ing manual, didn't you?")

Less short answer

The real question is the following: when you need to add a given behavior to an object, should you create a method on that object, or should you create a delegate that would implement this behavior? Imagine this is the year 2005, and you need to marshal your objects in XML. Solution 1: you create two XML methods on the class; solution 2, you create these two XML methods in a factory class. Now, this is 2025, 20 years later — and you do not need this XML anymore, but you need this JSON. Solution 1: you add the two JSON methods... but the real question is: what do you do with the now useless XML methods? From a functional point of view, this is dead code; and odds are that you will decide to keep it, because it would be too costly to refactor your application to remove all the calls to these methods. In solution 2, you just throw the class away, because nobody should be calling it anymore. Composition makes the decoupling of the different modules of your application much easier. And if you think this is just about organizing your application in a better way — well, you are mistaken: this is about making your code easier to dispose of when you do not need it anymore, thus preventing dead code from staying there and plaguing your application.

// Solution 1: methods on the class itself (dead code piles up)
class Order {
    String toXML() { /* 2005: nobody calls this anymore */ }
    String toJSON() { return "..."; }
}

// Solution 2: composition — disposable delegates
class XMLMarshaller { String marshal(Order o) { ... } } // throw in the trash
class JSONMarshaller { String marshal(Order o) { ... } }
96B

How can you invoke a method using the Reflection API?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is an invoke method in the Method class.

Less short answer

You can get a specific method of a class with the getMethod or getDeclaredMethod method of the class named Class. You need to pass the name of the method you want, along with the types of its parameters. When you have a Method object, all you need to do is call the invoke method on it, passing first the object on which you want to invoke this method (null for static methods), and then the arguments needed by this method. You can invoke a private method from outside the class by first calling setAccessible(true) — which suppresses the access checks made by the language.

Method m = String.class.getMethod("substring", int.class, int.class);
String r = (String) m.invoke("hello world", 0, 5);   // "hello"

Method privateM = Mine.class.getDeclaredMethod("secret");
privateM.setAccessible(true);                         // drops the check
String s = (String) privateM.invoke(myInstance);

Note: the series has two questions numbered 96 by the channel itself — this one and Question 96 (composition over inheritance, video 003).

97

What is a ConcurrentModificationException?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An exception raised when you modify your collection while iterating over it.

Less short answer

Be careful — despite the name, it has nothing to do with concurrent programming. Removing elements from a collection while iterating over it is generally not a great idea. There is a remove() method on the iterator — you can use it, just make sure that it is supported by your implementation. If what you need is to remove elements using a predicate, for instance, you can use the removeIf method, that does exactly that. One last word: some implementations of collections are okay with removing elements while iterating over them — especially the concurrent ones, like a LinkedBlockingQueue or a ConcurrentLinkedQueue — so be sure to check the JavaDoc on this one.

for (String s : list) {
    if (s.isEmpty()) list.remove(s);   // CME (same thread!)
}

Iterator<String> it = list.iterator();  // the right way:
while (it.hasNext())
    if (it.next().isEmpty()) it.remove();

list.removeIf(String::isEmpty);         // and the MODERN way
new ConcurrentLinkedQueue<>(list)       // concurrent: they don't even throw
98

What is a collector?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object provided by the Stream API that you can use to reduce streams using the collect method.

Less short answer

A collector is an implementation of the Collector interface — which you can implement yourself. You also have a Collectors factory class, with plenty of pre-built collectors that should cover most of your needs. You can do many things with collectors: you can create any sort of collections or maps; you can group your stream elements using keys and gather them in maps; or you can join them in strings of characters. You even have collectors that can map, filter or flat map the elements of your stream. One last word: sometimes the Stream API uses downstream collectors — a way to compose collectors. (Another video.)

List<String> list = stream.collect(Collectors.toList());
Map<String, List<P>> groups = stream.collect(Collectors.groupingBy(P::key));
String joined = stream.collect(Collectors.joining(", "));
Set<Integer> set = stream.collect(Collectors.toSet());
// and Collector.of(...) for your own
99

What is a daemon thread?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a thread.

Less short answer

When you create a thread, you can decide to set its daemon property to true. The JVM starts its shutdown sequence when it does not have any more non-daemon threads running. The main thread, for instance — the one that runs your main method — is a non-daemon thread: so when you exit your main method and no other non-daemon thread is left, the JVM shuts down. Non-daemon threads keep your JVM alive — they can be created in ExecutorServices, for instance, so don't forget to shut them down, or your JVM will never shut down. Two examples of daemon threads: the ones that are running your garbage collector or your just-in-time compiler. One last word: you can set the daemon property of a platform thread, but a virtual thread is always a daemon thread.

Thread cleaner = Thread.ofPlatform().daemon(true).start(() -> clean());

ExecutorService ex = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(4);
// NON-daemon threads: the JVM won't exit without...
ex.shutdown();        // ...calling this (otherwise, the app "hangs")

Thread.ofVirtual().start(t);  // virtual thread: always a daemon
100

What is a race condition?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A race condition occurs when two threads are writing and reading the same variable at the same time.

Less short answer

This is a situation you need to take care of, because it can put your application in an inconsistent state. Suppose that you are incrementing a simple counter: you first read its value, increment it, and write it back. Two threads doing that on two different cores of your CPU may be reading the same value — and what you will see is one increment instead of two. To prevent race conditions, you need to guard your variables with synchronization or use atomic variables. One last word: in Java, race conditions can only happen in the fields of your classes — not on local variables. It can be different in other languages.

class Counter {
    private int n;                     // field: subject to races!
    void inc() { n++; }                // read + add + write: 3 steps

    // the defenses:
    private final AtomicInteger n2 = new AtomicInteger();
    void inc2() { n2.incrementAndGet(); }        // atomic (CAS)
    private synchronized void inc3() { n++; }    // mutual exclusion
}
// locals (an int x inside a method) never race: they live on the stack
101

How can you check if a class is an extension of another?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that in the Class class.

Less short answer

This method is called isAssignableFrom. You call it on a Class instance — which could represent an interface, by the way — and pass another Class instance as an argument. isAssignableFrom checks if an instance of the argument class you pass can be assigned to a variable of the type you are calling this method on. For instance, you can check if you can assign a String object to a variable of type Comparable with the following code. One last word: this is a method that you should use with types that you don't know at compile time — if you do, then you probably don't need it.

Comparable.class.isAssignableFrom(String.class);  // true
Number.class.isAssignableFrom(Integer.class);     // true
List.class.isAssignableFrom(ArrayList.class);     // true

// for types known at compile time:
if (obj instanceof String s) { ... }
// reflection: Class.isInstance(obj) is the "dynamic instanceof"
String.class.isInstance(obj);
102

What is the type ? super T?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

This type represents all the types that are supertypes of T.

Less short answer

This is the kind of type you need when you need to define the type of an object that you consume. Suppose that you have a list of Polygon objects: you know that all the objects of this list can be cast to the Polygon type — so processing them with a Consumer<Shape> would be okay too (Shape is a supertype). That is why the forEach method takes a consumer that can consume any type that is a supertype of Polygon; and the same goes for methods that take functions — these functions are declared to take ? super T. One last word: as this type can be any supertype of T, in practice, with ? super T you can write T values and only read them back as Object.

List<Polygon> polygons = List.of(...);

// Consumer<? super Polygon>: accepts Consumer<Shape>, <Object>...
polygons.forEach((Consumer<Shape>) s -> draw(s));

// writing OK; reading only as Object:
List<? super Polygon> box = new ArrayList<Shape>();
box.add(new Polygon(...));    // writes a Polygon: guaranteed
Object o = box.get(0);        // reads: Object only
103

What does static mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Static can be added on a field or a method declaration. It means that this member is attached to the type, not to an instance.

Less short answer

Static fields can be defined on classes; static methods can be added to classes or interfaces. Because a static member belongs to a class, you should use it directly with the class name. If it is a field, all your instances share this field — so using mutable static fields usually means trouble. A fun fact: if you invoke a static member with an instance, the compiler will replace that instance with the class — meaning that you can read a static field using a null instance without throwing a NullPointerException! One last word: you can also define static blocks in your class — but please stay away from that. This is an anti-pattern.

class Cache {
    static final int SIZE = 100;   // of the class, shared
    static int counter;            // mutable static = trouble!

    static void clear() { }        // classes AND interfaces
}

Cache c = null;
c.SIZE;        // works! the compiler swaps in Cache.SIZE
// static { ... }  // static block: anti-pattern, avoid
104

What is a downstream collector?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A collector.

Less short answer

Most of the time you will create your collectors with one of the factory methods of the Collectors class. Some of these methods take a collector as a last parameter, called the downstream collector. This collector is used to further process your data. For instance, the groupingBy collector groups your elements using a key; the downstream collector that you use is what you choose to do with these groups — you can put them in a list, a set, you can join them, or you can even create another map. One last word: some factory methods always take a downstream collector — this is the case for the mapping, filtering or flatMapping methods: they actually define intermediate operations, and need this downstream collector to model the terminal operation that will process your stream.

Map<String, List<Person>> byCity = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Person::city));  // implicit toList

Map<String, Set<String>> namesByCity = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
                Person::city,
                Collectors.mapping(Person::name,   // downstream in a chain
                        Collectors.toSet())));

Map<String, Long> counts = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Person::city, Collectors.counting()));
105

Why is Thread.stop() deprecated?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Because it is unsafe.

Less short answer

Being able to interrupt the thread could lead to several problems in your application. If your thread is holding a synchronization key — because it is currently updating some shared in-memory structure — interrupting it would free these keys and expose this structure, leaving it in a state that could be inconsistent. And the same goes if your thread is holding a system resource that needs to be closed: not closing that resource properly could lead to a resource-leaking situation you want to avoid. One last word: this method has been deprecated in Java 1.2, in 1998 — that was a long time ago. It now throws an UnsupportedOperationException to make sure that you don't call it.

// Java 1.0: thread.stop() — killed the thread and released the monitors:
// another thread saw the structure HALF-WAY UPDATED (silent corruption)

// deprecated since 1998; today:
thread.stop();   // UnsupportedOperationException!

// cooperative cancellation:
thread.interrupt();
// and in the task: check isInterrupted() / handle InterruptedException
106

What does Comparable mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It means that you can compare these objects without a comparator.

Less short answer

There are two mechanisms in Java to compare objects and tell if one is greater than the other. First, you can create a Comparator that takes two objects and returns an int. And then you can implement the Comparable interface, that basically does the same: you take another object and return an int. This int should be positive if this is greater than the other, negative if not, and zero if these objects are equal. With a Comparator, you can compare objects with another semantic than the one implemented by Comparable. One last word: remember that the comparison should be symmetric and transitive — even if it is not, you may bump into the famous "Comparison method violates its general contract!" error message.

record Product(String name, double price) implements Comparable<Product> {
    public int compareTo(Product o) {           // natural order: price
        return Double.compare(price, o.price);
    }
}
// another semantic, without changing the class:
Comparator<Product> byName = Comparator.comparing(Product::name);

// contract breaks (not transitive, etc.) =>
// "Comparison method violates its general contract!" (TimSort)
107

How can you create a pre-filled map?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that: Map.of.

Less short answer

Map.of is super simple to use. Just call it and pass a key, its value, then the next key, its value, and again — up to 10 entries. Past 10 entries you need to change the pattern, because you cannot overload this kind of method with a vararg; so another method has been added: Map.ofEntries(...), that takes a vararg of entries that you can create with Map.entry(k, v). The map you get is non-modifiable, and the entry objects are also non-modifiable. You cannot pass the same key twice — if you do that, you will get an exception. One last word: none of these methods accept null — neither for the key, nor for the value, nor for the entry. Really. (Why would you do that?)

Map<String, Integer> small = Map.of("a", 1, "b", 2);   // up to 10 pairs

Map<String, Integer> large = Map.ofEntries(             // 11+: entries
        Map.entry("k1", 1), Map.entry("k2", 2), Map.entry("k3", 3));

// small.put("c", 3);     -> UnsupportedOperationException
// Map.of("a", 1, "a", 2); -> IllegalArgumentException (repeated key)
// Map.of("a", null);      -> NPE: nulls, never
108

What is the return type of max()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An Optional.

Less short answer

Whether you like your nulls or not — or think they are a good thing or not — in that particular case, this is what you need. Suppose you compute the max of a first stream, and then the max of a second stream; and then you need the max of all the elements of both streams. What you expect is that the max of both maxes gives you the max of all your elements — and it will... unless one of the streams is empty. If you choose to handle this case by returning null, then you cannot handle primitive types. And choosing a default value would not work either: Integer.MIN_VALUE will not work, for instance, because you can convert this int to a long — and then the min of a long is even smaller. The empty Optional is the correct answer left. One last word: there are several very interesting patterns to process Optionals that will make Optional look like streams. (Another video.)

Optional<Integer> m1 = Stream.<Integer>empty().max(Integer::compare);
Optional<Integer> m2 = Stream.of(5).max(Integer::compare);

// combining maxes (an empty one doesn't spoil the count):
Optional<Integer> overall = Stream.of(m1, m2)
        .flatMap(Optional::stream)
        .max(Integer::compare);    // Optional[5]
109

What is a Future?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object to communicate with the task that is being executed in another thread.

Less short answer

You get a Future object when you submit a Runnable or a Callable to an ExecutorService. The ExecutorService immediately gives you a Future object, but it will execute your task in another thread — sometimes in the future. At this point, you can use this Future to check if your task is done or not, and to get either the result it produced or the exception that was thrown. There is another overload of the get() method that takes a time out — this may be safer; prefer this pattern over a regular get. One last word: starting with Java 21, you can call state(), that returns RUNNING, SUCCESS, FAILED or CANCELLED.

ExecutorService ex = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(4);
Future<Integer> f = ex.submit(() -> compute());

f.isDone();                          // is it done?
int r = f.get(1, TimeUnit.SECONDS);  // with a timeout: safer
// f.get() returns the result OR rethrows the task's exception
f.state();                           // JDK 21+: RUNNING, SUCCESS, FAILED...
110

What does multiple inheritance mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It means trouble. (the presenter's blunt short answer)

Less short answer

Multiple inheritance is an object-oriented notion — and there are three types of inheritance. Inheritance of type: Java supports multiple inheritance of type — a class can implement as many interfaces as you need. Inheritance of behavior: Java supports it also — concrete methods are inherited from a superclass, and also from default methods in interfaces; and you may run into issues with this one, if you implement two interfaces with the same default method. And the last one is the inheritance of state: that is not supported by Java. One last word: "favor object composition over class inheritance" — that's the introduction of the GoF. A must-read.

interface A { default String m() { return "A"; } }
interface B { default String m() { return "B"; } }

class C implements A, B {          // multiple inheritance of TYPE: OK
    public String m() { return A.super.m(); }  // conflict resolved by force
}
// multiple inheritance of STATE: does not exist in Java (one superclass)
111

What is the no-arg empty constructor?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A constructor that the compiler adds to a class when you don't declare any.

Less short answer

If you create a class and you don't provide your own constructor, the compiler will add one for you — because every class should have at least one constructor. This constructor does not do anything and does not take any parameter — thus its name. This may be confusing: if you create a constructor later, then this empty constructor is not added anymore, and that can create compiler errors somewhere else in your application. Of course, you can still add one by hand to fix that. One last word: many frameworks that rely on reflection are using this no-arg constructor — so there are many cases where it is wise to keep it.

public class Customer {
    // nothing written: the compiler generates Customer() { super(); }
}

public class Order {
    private Order(String id) { }    // declared -> the no-arg is GONE
    // new Order()         -> does not compile anymore
    // a reflection-based framework: needs a public Order()!
}
112

What is Iterable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

This interface is extended by Collection, so it is actually the base interface of many interfaces of the Collections framework. It has only one abstract method, iterator() — so you can implement it with a lambda expression. All Iterables can be used in the for-each pattern — and this is very handy: if you have an iterator in your application, you can use it to create an iterable and then go through all the values of this iterator using your iterable in this for-each pattern. One last word: Iterable also declares a forEach method — so you can also go through the values generated by your iterator using this pattern.

class Numbers implements Iterable<Integer> {
    public Iterator<Integer> iterator() {     // the only abstract method
        return List.of(1, 2, 3).iterator();
    }
}
for (int n : new Numbers()) { ... }           // for-each, straight away!

Iterable<String> fromIterator = () -> myIterator;  // an iterator lambda
fromIterator.forEach(System.out::println);
113

What does Thread.interrupt() do?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It doesn't do what you think.

Less short answer

Thread.interrupt tries to interrupt your thread in two ways. First, it can make one of your calls throw an exception: if your task is blocked on a call to sleep, wait or join, then this method immediately throws an InterruptedException — the same if you are waiting for a permit from a semaphore; there are also many cases where, if you are waiting for some data on an IO request, an exception will be thrown too. In all the other cases, your task should regularly call the isInterrupted() method — and if it returns true, then it is your responsibility to finish your task as soon as possible. One last word: don't try to handle your threads yourself — there are Executor Services and ForkJoin Pools especially for that.

Thread worker = Thread.ofPlatform().start(() -> {
    while (!Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted()) {  // regular check
        try { processBatch(); }
        catch (InterruptedException e) { return; }      // it came from outside!
    }
});
worker.interrupt();   // "asks" to stop; it kills nobody
114

What is the first thing a constructor does? *(version 3)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It calls another constructor.

Less short answer

This other constructor can be a constructor from the same class, or from the superclass. If you don't write any call to any constructor, then the compiler adds a call to the empty constructor of your superclass — so if this empty constructor does not exist in your superclass, then you will get a compiler error. You can also call a specific constructor: a call to this(...) calls a constructor from the same class, and a call to super(...) calls a constructor from the superclass. Constructing an object requires that you call all the constructors, from Object down to your class. One last word: this may be annoying when you need to validate your arguments — and it may actually change soon (statements before super, JEP 513). (Another video.)

class A { A() { } A(String s) { } }

class B extends A {
    B() { super(); }            // the compiler inserts it if missing
    B(String s) { super(s); }   // picks a specific constructor
}

class C {
    C() { this("default"); }    // this(...) delegates in the same class
    C(String s) { }
}   // every chain ends in Object()
115

What are the four fundamental functional interfaces in Java?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Consumer, Supplier, Function and Predicate.

Less short answer

These are the four functional interfaces from the java.util.function package. A Consumer takes an argument and doesn't return anything; a Supplier doesn't take anything and returns something; a Function takes something and returns something; a Predicate is a "function" that returns a boolean. Then there are a number of variations: BiConsumer, BiFunction, BiPredicate — that take two arguments; there are also UnaryOperator and BinaryOperator — for the same type in and out — as well as specializations for primitive types, for instance IntConsumer. One last word: there is also a Runnable, that does not take any argument and does not return anything — it fits nicely in this family.

Consumer<String>  consumer = System.out::println;   // T -> void
Supplier<List<String>> sup = ArrayList::new;        // () -> T
Function<String, Integer> f = String::length;       // T -> R
Predicate<String> pred = s -> s.isEmpty();          // T -> boolean

BiFunction<Integer, Integer, Integer> sum = Integer::sum;
Runnable nothing = () -> {};                        // () -> void
116

What is an enumeration?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class with a set of instances declared at compile time.

Less short answer

Enumerations are interesting when you know that a class can only have a limited number of instances. Enumerations can have a state — even a mutable state ("why would you do that?") — and you can declare a constructor for them. Your enum class is not a regular class: it is final and extends the Enum class — so your enumeration cannot extend anything and cannot be extended, but it can implement interfaces. One last word: because you cannot create any more instances of an enumeration at runtime, using enumerations is the preferred pattern to create singletons. You can see examples of that in the naturalOrder() factory method of the Comparator interface.

enum Operation implements BiFunction<Double, Double, Double> { // interface OK
    ADD { public Double apply(Double a, Double b) { return a + b; } },
    SUB { public Double apply(Double a, Double b) { return a - b; } };
    // extends Enum (final): extends nothing, cannot be extended
}
enum Singleton { INSTANCE; }   // the idiomatic singleton
117

How is List.subList() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It gives you a mutable view of a given part of a list.

Less short answer

It's actually a nifty feature — and probably an overlooked one. First, it's a list, so you can use it as such. But it's also a mutable view, so you can use it to manipulate the first list. For instance, you can insert a list in another one, at a given place, by calling subList(i, i).addAll(other) — passing the same index for the from-index and the to-index — and calling addAll on that view. Or you can clear a range of indexes from a list with subList(from, to).clear(). One last word: if what you need is to select a range of indexes — and you don't need the rest of the list anymore — then you should copy the sublist; because if you don't, you will still have the original list in memory, which can be an overhead.

List<String> l = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "d"));
l.subList(1, 1).addAll(List.of("b", "c"));  // inserts at index 1
// [a, b, c, d]
l.subList(0, 2).clear();                    // erases the range [0, 2)
// [c, d]

List<String> justTheSlice = new ArrayList<>(l.subList(0, 1)); // a copy!
// without the copy, the whole list stays retained by the "view"
118

How can you create a map to hold 20 elements?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a factory method for that: HashMap.newHashMap(n).

Less short answer

Creating a map with a given capacity is more complex than it seems. The HashMap carries an internal array; when the number of used cells reaches a certain level (the load factor), the content of the array is copied into a larger array — a costly operation. Hence the interest in creating an array large enough upfront — but then there is some math to do to get that size, and that math has been written for you in this factory method (JDK 19+): you give it the number of entries you expect, and it computes the right capacity. One last word: be careful — HashMap also has a constructor that takes an int, but that value is the capacity of the internal array, which may be too small to store all your key-value pairs.

// 20 entries without rehashing:
Map<String, Integer> m1 = HashMap.newHashMap(20);   // does the right math!

Map<String, Integer> m2 = new HashMap<>(20);        // TRAP:
// 20 = array capacity; with load factor 0.75 -> only ~15 entries
new HashMap<>(27);  // (int)(20/0.75)+1 = the "old" manual computation
119

How can you use flatMap to filter a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Your flatMap method should return an empty stream when you don't want to pass the corresponding value.

Less short answer

Sometimes the rules for filtering a stream are too complex or too costly — this is the case in this example, where the condition is "parsing your string should not throw a NumberFormatException". The complete code for that is complex and really not nice; using a flatMap and returning an empty stream if the result cannot be computed is probably better. One last word: there is actually another method to do the same thing that will save the construction of the stream object — it's the mapMulti method, that was added in JDK 16.

// ugly: a try/catch inside the filter
.filter(s -> { try { Integer.parseInt(s); return true; }
               catch (NumberFormatException e) { return false; } })

// better: flatMap with an empty stream = "rejected"
.flatMap(s -> parseSafely(s).stream())

Optional<Integer> parseSafely(String s) {
    try { return Optional.of(Integer.parseInt(s)); }
    catch (NumberFormatException e) { return Optional.empty(); }
}

// JDK 16+: mapMulti, without allocating streams
.mapMulti((String s, Consumer<Integer> down) -> {
    try { down.accept(Integer.parseInt(s)); }
    catch (NumberFormatException ignored) { }
})
120

What does reentrant mean for synchronization?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It means that, when your thread already has the lock of a synchronized block, it can enter it again.

Less short answer

To enter a synchronized block, the thread asks for the key of the object that guards this block; if the key is not there, the thread is blocked until the key becomes available again. But there are cases where the thread already has the key: calling a synchronized method from a first synchronized method of the same class, or from a subclass — the thread is already holding that key, so it can execute this code without having to wait. One last word: the synchronization classes from java.util.concurrent are also reentrant — this is the case for the ReentrantLock class, as its name may suggest.

class Service {
    public synchronized void outer() { inner(); }  // already has the key...
    private synchronized void inner() { ... }      // ...enters directly!
    // without reentrancy: a DEADLOCK with itself
}
// ReentrantLock: an acquisition counter per SAME thread
121

What does shallow copy mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It means duplicating an object — but not the objects this object has references to.

Less short answer

There are cases where it makes sense to shallow copy an object, and others where it can be actually dangerous. The rule of thumb that will always work is the following: if the related object is non-modifiable, like a String, then there is no need to duplicate it — in that case, shallow copy is OK. But if your related object is an ArrayList, for instance, it means that anyone with a reference to this ArrayList can modify it — and thus modify the internal state of your object... and most of the time, this is not what you want. One last word: you can protect your objects against that with the defensive copy pattern. (Another video.)

class Order implements Cloneable {
    String number;             // immutable: shallow OK
    List<Item> items;          // mutable: shared!

    @Override protected Object clone() throws CloneNotSupportedException {
        Order o = (Order) super.clone();   // shallow: items UNCHANGED
        o.items = new ArrayList<>(items);  // defend yourself: copy
        return o;
    }
}
122

What is the Vector?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something you should not be using anymore.

Less short answer

Vector is an old API from before the Collections framework — this was before Java 2, released in 1998. With Java 2, the Vector class has been refactored to be included in the Collections framework. Vector is thread-safe, but in a very basic way: all its methods are synchronized. If you do not need thread safety, you can safely use an ArrayList instead; if you do, well — there are better implementations depending on your use case. One last word: this Vector class has nothing to do with the Vector API, which gives you access to the SIMD parallel computing capacity of your CPU. (Another video.)

Vector<String> v = new Vector<>();   // everything synchronized (and slow)
List<String> l = new ArrayList<>();  // no sync: prefer it
List<String> c = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>(); // real concurrency

// and don't confuse: jdk.incubator.vector.VectorSpecies = SIMD (other thing)
123

What is the difference between Runnable and Callable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Both are used to model tasks in concurrent programming.

Less short answer

A Runnable is a task model introduced in the first versions of Java: it doesn't take any argument and does not return anything. Callable was added in Java 5, in 2004, as part of the java.util.concurrent API: a Callable doesn't take any argument either, but it does return something — and it can throw an exception. Runnable is the only one that can be passed to thread creation, including with the factory methods added in JDK 21; both can be passed to executor services and fork-join pools. One last word: structured task scopes only accept Callables. We will talk more about that later.

Runnable task = () -> System.out.println("no return");
Callable<Integer> calc = () -> { Thread.sleep(100); return 42; };

new Thread(task).start();          // Runnable only here
executor.submit(task);             // returns Future<?>
Future<Integer> f = executor.submit(calc);  // Future<Integer>

try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnFailure()) {
    scope.fork(calc);              // Callable only in the scope!
    scope.join();
}
124

Can you create a stream from an Iterable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Yes.

Less short answer

There is actually an API to do that. Most of the time, you will create streams from Collections using the collection.stream() method — but sometimes all you have is an Iterable. In that case, creating a collection or an array and then opening a stream on it would be a waste of memory: you can call StreamSupport.stream(spliterator, false) — the spliterator comes directly from your iterable, and false because you don't want to mess with parallel streams. One last word: in the case your iterable produces an unlimited amount of data, then a stream can handle that with some short-circuit operations — whereas trying to build a collection would just break your application.

Iterable<Reading> sensor = readForever();     // infinite!

StreamSupport.stream(sensor.spliterator(), false) // without materializing
        .filter(Reading::critical)
        .findFirst()                              // short-circuit saves you
        .ifPresent(alarm::trigger);
125

How is a CopyOnWriteArrayList working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Well, as the name suggests: it copies its internal array on each write operation.

Less short answer

CopyOnWriteArrayList is a thread-safe list that is interesting if what you have is mostly read operations. This class works in the following way. First, the internal array is duplicated and mutated within a synchronized block; then, the internal reference to this array is updated. Because this reference is volatile, reading does not need to be synchronized — so no contention on read operations. The major overhead is the array duplication. One last word: there is also a CopyOnWriteArraySet that actually wraps a CopyOnWriteArrayList — so same warnings on this one.

List<Listener> listeners = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>();
listeners.add(l1);          // copies the whole array (expensive write)

for (Listener l : listeners) // lock-free read, over the snapshot
    l.notify();             // safe iteration, no CME — add at will
126

What is a switch statement?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An old thing. That got some attention recently. (a humorous short answer)

Less short answer

Switch used to be a statement that would work on a selector variable that could be an integer type — not long — an enum, or a string of characters; and this selector could not be null. It can now be an expression also; it can accept any object, including null (for the null pattern), as the selector; and it can accept patterns and guarded patterns for the case labels. It can even check for exhaustiveness, and can prevent fall through. So in one word: it has been fixed — and is now much more useful. It could even handle exceptions as case labels in a future. One last word: switch expressions are now part of what is called data oriented programming in Java. (Another video.)

// before: a statement, break, few types
switch (code) { case 1: ...; break; default: ...; }

// now: an expression, patterns, exhaustiveness, no fall-through
String desc = switch (obj) {
    case Integer i when i > 0 -> "positive: " + i;
    case Integer i            -> "not positive";
    case String s             -> "text: " + s;
    case null                 -> "null (allowed!)";
    default                   -> "other";
};
127

What is the difference between an Exception and an Error?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Exceptions are for your application; Errors are for the JVM.

Less short answer

In many cases, you need to handle exceptions explicitly — if your exception is a runtime exception, which is an extension of the Exception class, then you don't. Errors are mostly used to tell you that something went wrong in the JVM — the JVM is loading your classes even before running them: that's the case for the ClassFormatError, NoSuchMethodError, AbstractMethodError, or ClassCircularityError. One last word: here are examples of errors that will put an end to your application: StackOverflowError, OutOfMemoryError — there is even an UnknownError, "but I never saw it, to be honest".

// JVM errors (don't catch!):
// new byte[Integer.MAX_VALUE]     -> OutOfMemoryError
// infinite recursion              -> StackOverflowError
// invalid bytecode/broken jar     -> ClassFormatError
// a method swapped in a library   -> NoSuchMethodError

// application exceptions (handle):
try { Files.readAllLines(p); }
catch (IOException e) { ... }   // checked: handling mandatory
128

What is a red-black tree?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A data structure implemented by the TreeMap class.

Less short answer

TreeMap is actually an implementation of NavigableMap, that extends a SortedMap — and since Java 21, a SequencedMap. A red-black tree is a data structure that stores its elements in a binary tree and keeps them sorted using a nifty trick of colored nodes (rules that guarantee a height ≤ 2·log n). TreeMap keeps its key-value pairs sorted by key while you are adding or removing them — so when you iterate over them, they are already sorted. One last word: on the one hand, a red-black tree is a very efficient data structure, as most of the operations are O(log n); on the other hand, it's a reference-based data structure, so pointer chasing will not play nice with it (cache misses).

TreeMap<String, Integer> tm = new TreeMap<>();
tm.put("banana", 2); tm.put("pineapple", 1);
tm.firstKey();       // "pineapple" — always sorted
tm.subMap("a", "c"); // a view [a, c)
tm.reversed();       // JDK 21+: SequencedMap
129

What are the visibility modifiers for members?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Private, protected and public.

Less short answer

There is also a fourth one, which consists in not putting any modifier at all — package private. A private member can only be accessed from the class itself; a public member can be accessed from everywhere; a protected member can be accessed from the extending classes and from other classes within the same package; no modifier means that you can access it from the same package only. One last word (a very important one): without the module system, you can create a class in a package from any other jar that can give you access to all the protected and package-private members... so the two levels you should really rely on are private and public.

public class Example {
    private int a;      // this class only (reliable)
    int b;              // package-private (fragile!)
    protected int c;    // subclasses + package (fragile!)
    public int d;       // the world (reliable)
}
// protected/package-private without modules: accessible by cheating
// a module-info with exports/opens gives the package real protection
130

What is a sequenced collection?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface added to the Collections framework.

Less short answer

This interface was added in Java 21, to fill a gap there was between lists and sorted sets. Sequenced collections allow you to ask for the first or last element of a collection — and to remove it if the collection is modifiable. You can also ask for a reversed view on the content of the collection. One last word: you also have sequenced maps and sequenced sets — SequencedSet extends SequencedCollection, with a reversed() method that returns itself a sequenced set.

List<Integer> l = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
l.getFirst();          // 1
l.getLast();           // 3
l.addFirst(0);         // [0, 1, 2, 3]
l.pollLast();          // removes and returns 3
l.reversed();          // [2, 1, 0] — a view, no copy

LinkedHashSet<Integer> s = new LinkedHashSet<>(List.of(1, 2));
s.reversed().getFirst();  // SequencedSet: 2
131

What does encapsulation mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It means no public instance field in your classes.

Less short answer

The internal state of your class should be accessed only through accessors — to read it or to modify it. One of the reasons is that your code only depends on the public method of its collaborating objects, not on their internal implementation — meaning that you can change it without changing the client code. Encapsulation is usually an object concern, but you can also apply this principle to APIs or libraries: only depend on them through their interface. One last word: even if records are non-modifiable, you still need to access their internal state through their accessors — there are good reasons for that. (Another video.)

class Account {
    private BigDecimal balance;          // encapsulated: no public field

    public BigDecimal balance() { return balance; }        // read
    public void deposit(BigDecimal v) {                    // controlled write
        if (v.signum() <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException();
        balance = balance.add(v);
    }
}
132

What is a component?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A field of a record.

Less short answer

This definition is very precise: the elements you define along with your record definition are called record components. The word "component" became a Java platform notion when records were added to the language. In the Reflection API, for instance, you have a getRecordComponents() method on the Class class; and when you create an annotation, you can state that it can be added on a record component — and for that, the ElementType enum, that defines all the elements on which you can add an annotation, has a value RECORD_COMPONENT. One last word: when you declare a record component, the compiler gives you an accessor for it — and uses them to create an equals and a hashCode method, as well as a toString method. All that for free.

public record Customer(String name, int age) { }
//            component ^        ^ component

Customer.class.getRecordComponents();  // reflection knows components

@interface NonNull { @Target(ElementType.RECORD_COMPONENT) }
133

What are the main operations of a stack or a queue?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Peek, pop and push. (the video plays with the pronunciation)

Less short answer

These two data structures are the two simplest data structures you can imagine. Peek allows you to check the next available element without removing it — you cannot see the other elements. Pop removes it: now you have it, you can do something. Push adds an element. If it's a stack, then peek will show you the last element that was pushed; and if it's a queue, then peek shows you the first element that was pushed — maybe some time ago. One last word: there are two interfaces to model these: Queue and Deque — which stands for double-ended queue — and a number of implementations, like ArrayDeque for instance, or ConcurrentLinkedQueue, which is thread-safe.

Deque<String> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // LIFO
stack.push("a"); stack.push("b");
stack.peek();   // "b" (the last one)
stack.pop();    // "b"

Queue<String> queue = new ConcurrentLinkedQueue<>();  // FIFO
queue.offer("a"); queue.offer("b");
queue.peek();    // "a" (the first one)
queue.poll();    // "a"
134

What is an entry and how can you create one?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a key-value pair in a map.

Less short answer

This key-value pair is modeled by the Map.Entry interface: from it you can read — and update — the value, and you can read the key (not modifiable). The easiest way to create a key-value pair is to use the Map.entry(key, value) factory method from the Map class: it gives you an unmodifiable entry. You can create an unmodifiable map from entries with the Map.ofEntries(...) factory method. Be careful, because none of these factory methods accept null keys or null values. One last word: you can also get a set of the entries of a map with the map.entrySet() method — this set has very specific properties. (Another video.)

Map.Entry<String, Integer> pair = Map.entry("pen", 12);
pair.getKey();    // "pen" (read-only)
pair.getValue();  // 12

Map<String, Integer> stock = Map.ofEntries(
        Map.entry("pen", 12),
        Map.entry("pencil", 30));     // immutable, no nulls

for (var e : stock.entrySet()) { /* a view on the map */ }
135

What type of ExecutorService can you cite?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

newSingleThreadExecutor(), that creates a pool with only one thread. (...among others)

Less short answer

This question refers to the factory methods of the Executors class — there are some that you probably need to know. newFixedThreadPool(n), where you pass the number of threads you want. newCachedThreadPool(), that creates threads on demand and lets them die after 60 seconds of idleness. Some of these factory methods also take a ThreadFactory as an argument, that you can use to generate the name of the threads, for instance, or to decide whether they are daemon threads or not. One last word: with JDK 21, you even have a newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor() that creates virtual threads on demand and lets them die at the end.

Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor();     // 1 thread (a serial queue)
Executors.newFixedThreadPool(8);         // fixed
Executors.newCachedThreadPool();         // elastic (60s lifetime)
Executors.newScheduledThreadPool(2);     // periodic tasks
Executors.newThreadPerTaskExecutor(factory);  // a platform thread per task
Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor();  // JDK 21+: virtual per task
136

What is a defensive copy?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A pattern by which you create a copy of an object, when you receive it or when you return it.

Less short answer

It is useful when this object is modifiable — like an ArrayList, a HashMap, or a plain array, for instance. If you don't do this copy, someone with a reference to this object will be able to modify it — and thus modify the internal state of your object. In a class (or record) with an array as a component, you need to copy it in the constructor — to make sure that no one keeps a reference on it — and in the accessor, for the same reason. One last word: you don't need a defensive copy with a non-modifiable object — but be careful: you need to make sure that it does not have any reference to other modifiable objects.

record ClassGroup(String[] students) {
    ClassGroup { students = students.clone(); }  // defense on the way in
    public String[] students() {                 // Java 16+: custom accessor
        return students.clone();                 // defense on the way out
    }
}
// List.copyOf(map.values()) — the idiomatic defensive copy for collections
137

What is a for each loop?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A syntactic sugar to iterate over an Iterable.

Less short answer

This syntax works for any Iterable — that includes all the collections — and the arrays. Internally, the compiler creates an iterator for you and uses it to traverse your iterable. You may be thinking that this object is just another overhead — but it can quickly be removed by the JVM through its escape analysis optimization: no one can get a reference to this iterator, so in the end the JVM may decide to just drop it. One last word: if what you have is an Iterable, you can also use its forEach method and pass your consumer — this forEach method uses a for-each loop internally, so it will not make your code any faster, but it can make it easier to read.

// this...
for (String s : names) { ... }

// ...becomes this (the compiler):
for (Iterator<String> it = names.iterator(); it.hasNext(); ) {
    String s = it.next();   // the iterator doesn't escape -> JIT scalarizes
}

// the readable equivalent:
names.forEach(this::process);
138

What is the difference between an Enumeration and an Iterator?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An Enumeration is something you should not be using anymore.

Less short answer

Back in the days — a long time ago, before the Collections framework was added to the JDK in 1998, that was Java 2 — the Enumeration interface was the interface you had to use to iterate over the elements. Now that you have the Collections framework, there is no reason to use Vector anymore: you can use an ArrayList instead, or a concurrent collection from java.util.concurrent if you need thread safety. One last word: as of Java 9, the functionality of this interface is duplicated by the Iterator interface — and if you see an Enumeration, you can call its asIterator() method to use it as an iterator.

// 1998: Enumeration was the only way
for (Enumeration<String> e = vector.elements(); e.hasMoreElements(); )
    use(e.nextElement());

// modern: Iterator (remove, for-each)...
// the bridge: Enumeration.asIterator() (Java 9+)
e.asIterator().forEachRemaining(this::use);
139

What is the diamond problem in OOP?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something that default methods in interfaces brought to the Java platform.

Less short answer

This problem comes with the multiple inheritance of behavior. Before Java 8, Java used to support only the inheritance of type: a class can implement several interfaces. Starting with Java 8, you can have default methods — that is, behavior in interfaces. So if a given class implements two different interfaces that happen to have the exact same default method (but are doing two different things), then what you have is a diamond problem. One last word: how can you fix that? Well, the good news is that the compiler can see this problem — and will ask you which default method you want to call, at compile time.

interface Flyer  { default String move() { return "flying"; } }
interface Swimmer { default String move() { return "swimming"; } }

class Duck implements Flyer, Swimmer {
    @Override public String move() {          // disambiguation MANDATORY
        return Swimmer.super.move();          // an explicit choice
    }
}
140

What is a compact constructor?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a syntax that you can use to write the canonical constructor of records in a much simpler way.

Less short answer

The canonical constructor of a record is simply the constructor that takes all its components; you can have other constructors, but they need to call this one. You can write this constructor in a compact form: you do not declare its parameters, and you do not need to assign the arguments to the fields of your record — the assignment is automatic. It gives you a very clean syntax, especially when all you have to do is validate the arguments you get — but you can also do some defensive copy in a compact constructor if this is what you need. One last word: records are really great to improve the readability and expressiveness of your code — you can even create them on the fly, as local records; you should definitely use them everywhere you can.

record Period(LocalDate start, LocalDate end) {
    Period {                                    // compact: no parameters
        if (end.isBefore(start))
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("end < start");
        // the assignment this.start = start ... is automatic
    }
}

record Password(char[] value) {
    Password { value = value.clone(); }         // defensive copy works too
}
141

What is a Deque?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A double-ended queue.

Less short answer

There are two interfaces that you should use when you need a queue or a stack in Java: Queue and Deque, that extends Queue. You can peek, pop or push by both ends of a deque — it makes it a model for both stacks and queues in Java. The Deque defines the two behaviors you need when the queue is full (push) or empty (pop): react immediately by throwing an exception, or immediately return true or false depending on whether the operation succeeded — there are families of methods for each style (addFirst/offerFirst, removeFirst/pollFirst...). One last word: Deque has an extension, BlockingDeque, that models concurrency — it adds a third behavior: instead of reacting immediately, it can block until your push or your pop can be conducted.

Deque<String> deque = new ArrayDeque<>();

deque.offerFirst("a"); deque.offerLast("b");   // false if it doesn't fit
deque.pollFirst();  deque.pollLast();          // null if empty
deque.addFirst("x");                           // exception if it doesn't fit
deque.push("top"); deque.pop();                // a stack (LIFO) at the head

// BlockingDeque: put/take block until possible
142

What is the deep copy?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A mechanism to copy an object and all its related objects.

Less short answer

There are two ways to duplicate objects: deep copy and shallow copy. Shallow copy just copies the current object — if this object is referencing more objects, they are not copied. Deep copy visits all the graph of the referenced objects and copies them — which can be costly. Because it is hard to know in advance what the cost of a deep copy is, shallow copy is usually the default behavior (of clone(), for instance). One last word: if your objects are non-modifiable, then there is no need to copy them at all — you can check the source code of List.copyOf: it first checks if the list is non-modifiable, and in that case, just returns it.

class Address { String city; }             // mutable
class Customer implements Cloneable {
    String name; Address address;

    @Override protected Object clone() throws CloneNotSupportedException {
        Customer c = (Customer) super.clone();   // shallow: address shared!
        c.address = new Address();               // deep: copies the graph
        c.address.city = this.address.city;
        return c;
    }
}
List.copyOf(immutableList);   // doesn't even copy: returns the same instance
143

What is a Fork Join pool? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is the pool of threads used by parallel streams.

Less short answer

This pool of threads is called the common ForkJoinPool, and there is only one in your JVM: it is created when your JVM starts, and waits for your parallel streams to use it. The ForkJoinPool is named after the algorithm used in parallel streams. First, you split your data set again and again, until the chunk you have is small enough to be processed in a reasonable amount of time — that's the fork phase; and then you merge everything to compute the final result — that's the join phase. One last word: you should be careful with parallel streams — they may be much more costly than what you think. One piece of advice: if you're writing a service bound to run in an application server, you should stay away from them.

list.parallelStream()          // runs on the commonPool (one per JVM)
    .map(this::heavy)
    .collect(toList());
// fork: splits down to minimal chunks; join: merges the results

// on an app server: the pool is SHARED with other deployments...
144

What is an entry set?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a set that contains the key-value pairs of a map.

Less short answer

You can call map.entrySet() to get this set: it is a Set<Map.Entry> — the Map.Entry interface models your key-value pairs. This set is actually a view on your map: you cannot add anything to it (you will get an exception if you try), but you can remove elements from it — either directly or indirectly, removing the corresponding key-value pair from your map. One last word: if your map is a SequencedMap, you can call sequencedEntrySet() on it to get a SequencedSet instead of a regular set.

Map<String, Integer> m = new LinkedHashMap<>(Map.of("a", 1, "b", 2));

for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : m.entrySet()) {
    System.out.println(e.getKey() + " = " + e.getValue());
}
m.entrySet().removeIf(e -> e.getValue() < 2);  // removes a=1 from the map

// SequencedMap (JDK 21): sequencedEntrySet() -> SequencedSet
145

What does fall through mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

With no doubt, trouble. (the presenter's comic short answer)

Less short answer

This is something that can happen if you're using switch in your application... — well, if you used switch: as (he jokes) nobody does, there is very little chance that you can see this problem. In case you do, the problem is the following: suppose you switch on an enum, for instance, but forgot to add a break after each label — then your code will carry on with the next one, which is usually not what you want. One last word: maybe you will be using switch statements and expressions more and more in the future — because it is now part of the data oriented programming paradigm currently being added to the Java language. (Another video.)

switch (level) {
    case HIGH:
        alert();
        // missing break! -> falls into the next case (fall through)
    case MEDIUM:
        log();      // also runs for HIGH. A classic bug.
        break;
}

// the arrow form (->) has NO fall-through:
switch (level) {
    case HIGH   -> alert();
    case MEDIUM -> log();
}
146

What is the Collectors.toMap()? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A collector to create maps.

Less short answer

Collectors are objects you can pass to the collect method of the Stream API. Collectors.toMap takes two functions — both are applied to the elements of your streams: the first one is used to compute the key, and the second one to compute the value. In case two elements produce the same key, then you need to provide the merge function — if you don't, you will get an exception at runtime. You can also provide a supplier that would be used to create your empty map — useful to create TreeMaps, for instance. One last word: you also have the groupingBy collector, which adds the elements that produce the same key to the same list.

Map<String, Integer> byCity = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.toMap(Person::city, Person::age,
                                   Integer::sum));       // merge for duplicates

Map<String, Integer> sorted = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.toMap(Person::name, Person::age,
                                  (a, b) -> a, TreeMap::new)); // supplier

Map<String, List<Person>> groups = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Person::city));  // the alternative
147

What is a Lock? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer
