It is used to model the object you can use to ensure that only one thread can execute a piece of code at a given time. There are several ways to do that in Java. The first one is the synchronized block — it has always been there. And the second one is the Lock interface, implemented by the ReentrantLock class, added in Java 5. The pattern to use this object is the following: create it, then call lock(), then use it, and release it in a try/finally block — to make sure that, in case something goes wrong, your lock will always be unlocked. One last word: you can do many more things with locks than with synchronized blocks — like trying to lock with a timeout, for instance.

private final Lock lock = new ReentrantLock();

void process() {
    lock.lock();
    try {
        // only one thread at a time here
    } finally {
        lock.unlock();              // always in the finally!
    }
}

if (lock.tryLock(1, TimeUnit.SECONDS)) {   // with a timeout: synchronized can't
    try { ... } finally { lock.unlock(); }
}
148

How can you create a collection from an Iterable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Why would you do that? Are you sure that this is what you need? (the presenter questions the question itself)

Less short answer

An iterable is something that can produce elements without having to store them all in memory at once — so you can process many elements with this pattern with a small memory footprint. For instance, you can create a stream on it: unless you call distinct or sorted on your stream, it will not store anything in your memory. So if you are really, really sure that this is what you need, then here is the pattern — the classic stream + collect (or an add loop). One last word: loading your data in memory can dramatically increase your memory footprint — so make sure you need it before doing it.

Iterable<Record> source = hugeSource();      // lazy by nature

// if it can stay lazy: don't materialize!
StreamSupport.stream(source.spliterator(), false)
             .filter(this::valid).forEach(this::process);

// if it HAS to be a collection (does it, really?):
List<Record> all = StreamSupport
        .stream(source.spliterator(), false)
        .collect(Collectors.toList());
149

What is the filter() method?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A method to prevent elements from a stream from being passed to the next stage of your pipeline.

Less short answer

A filter method does not really remove elements from a stream — because a stream is an empty object: there is no element in a stream. A stream consumes elements from a source, processes them one stage at a time, and then reaches a terminal operation. The filter operation takes a predicate as a parameter, that decides if a given element can be passed or not. One last word: you can also filter using two other operations — mapMulti and flatMap — which may be useful if you cannot express your validation as a simple predicate. (Another video.)

Stream.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5)
      .filter(n -> n % 2 == 0)     // 2 and 4 pass; the rest never gets there
      .forEach(System.out::println);

// complex validation? flatMap / mapMulti decide per element:
.flatMap(n -> valid(n) ? Stream.of(n) : Stream.empty())
150

How is quick sort working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Quickly. (the presenter's joke)

Less short answer

The first step consists in choosing a pivot — your best choice is a value that will split your set into equal parts. Then you compare all the elements to the pivot: the lesser elements go to the left, the greater to the right — and then you rinse and repeat on the two halves of your set, again and again, until you have intervals of size 2. One last word: how many operations do you need? Well, each step consists in N comparisons, and you divide your set in 2, again and again — so the number of steps p is such that N / 2^p is still greater than 1: that's log N steps — N log N in total. You knew that already — now you know. (a pun on "now you know")

void quickSort(int[] a, int lo, int hi) {
    if (lo >= hi) return;
    int p = partition(a, lo, hi);   // lesser to the left, greater to the right
    quickSort(a, lo, p - 1);        // recursion on the left half
    quickSort(a, p + 1, hi);        // recursion on the right half
}
// N comparisons per level x log N levels = N log N
// Arrays.sort(int[]): dual-pivot quicksort
151

What is a text block?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a syntax to write strings of characters.

Less short answer

You open a text block with a triple double quote followed by your line terminator. Then all the text that follows in your source code is part of the string you're creating, until you put another triple double quote that closes your string. A text block usually spans over several lines. The blank characters at the beginning of each line are not part of your text — they are called the incidental whitespaces (the common indentation is removed). One last word: you can control the start and end of each line by adding a \s character (and \ to continue on the next line). Text blocks are great — you should use them wherever you can.

String json = """
        {
          "name": "Ana",
          "age": 30
        }""";        // the common indentation is removed automatically

String sql = """
        SELECT * \
        FROM customer WHERE id = ?""";   // \ joins lines
152

How can you loop backward?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

It only works if your collection is ordered — meaning that it is in fact a list; most of the time it is the case, because ArrayList is your favorite implementation. There is a listIterator() method on List that does not exist on Collection, and that returns a ListIterator. ListIterator extends the Iterator: so you have the classical next and hasNext, but you also have previous and hasPrevious, to iterate backward. One last word: you also have a nextIndex and a previousIndex method, as well as set and add — to set the current element and insert an element through this list iterator; all this is supported by the ArrayList implementation.

List<String> l = List.of("a", "b", "c");

for (ListIterator<String> it = l.listIterator(l.size());
        it.hasPrevious(); ) {
    System.out.println(it.previous());   // c b a
}
// Java 21+ alternative: l.reversed() (a view) — SequencedCollection
153

What is a semaphore? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A lock with several permits.

Less short answer

A classical lock can give access to the block of code it protects to one thread at a time; a semaphore can give access to several threads. You create a semaphore by giving it a number of permits; then you can use it to protect a block of code: a thread needs a permit to execute the protected code — and if there is none available, then it blocks until there is one. Don't forget to release your permit in a finally block. Semaphores are useful when you need to limit the number of threads that can access an external resource. One last word: semaphores can also be fair, meaning that the first thread that was blocked is the first one to be given a permit.

Semaphore slots = new Semaphore(10);   // 10 simultaneous accesses

slots.acquire();                       // blocks if no permit is available
try { callExternalApi(); }
finally { slots.release(); }           // ALWAYS in the finally

Semaphore fair = new Semaphore(1, true); // FIFO for the blocked ones
154

What is a method handle?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object to access the members of an object — "reflection on steroids". (our phrasing of the video's idea)

Less short answer

There is a caveat in using the Reflection API with the Field or Method objects: it needs to check access on each invocation — and this is quite slow. A MethodHandle is a reference to an existing member of a class, but the checks are made when you create the method handle. You can invoke public constructors by getting a reference to them with the findConstructor method; the same for public methods with findVirtual, and public fields with findGetter and findSetter. One last word: method handles do not give you access to private members from outside of a class — for that, you need to use the classical Field or Method classes.

MethodHandles.Lookup lookup = MethodHandles.lookup();

MethodHandle length = lookup.findVirtual(
        String.class, "length", MethodType.methodType(int.class));
int n = (int) length.invoke("java");   // the checks were made at creation

MethodHandle constructor = lookup.findConstructor(
        ArrayList.class, MethodType.methodType(void.class));
155

Why should you prefer private fields and accessors?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Because it is safer.

Less short answer

A private field must be accessed through an accessor — meaning that you can control the access, and there are two things that you can easily do. The first one is the defensive copy: sometimes you need to protect your internal state from outside modifications — even if your field is final, it can be a reference to a mutable container that you need to copy when you build your object. And the second one is concurrent access: fields can be declared volatile, but it only makes the reference thread-safe; you can then guarantee thread safety using a synchronized block. One last word: by default, always use private fields. Always.

final class Contract {
    private final List<Clause> clauses;   // private, always

    Contract(List<Clause> cs) {
        this.clauses = List.copyOf(cs);   // defensive copy
    }
    public List<Clause> clauses() {       // controlled access
        return clauses;                   // already immutable
    }
    private volatile boolean active;      // the reference is visible...
    public synchronized void activate() { active = true; } // state in sync
}
156

What is the difference between an interface and an abstract class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Well, it's not the same thing.

Less short answer

There are two major differences. First, an abstract class has a constructor, which is called to construct your final object — it is not the case for an interface. And second, an abstract class can carry your mutable state — which, again, is not the case for an interface. This question may sound like a trick question, because you can have static and instance methods in both abstract classes and interfaces — a feature that was added in Java 8, in 2014. One last word: now you may be wondering — when should one use abstract classes or interfaces? By default, prefer interfaces. Why? Well, that will be for another time.

abstract class Animal {               // constructor + STATE
    private int energy;               // an interface has none of this
    Animal() { energy = 100; }
    abstract String sound();
}

interface Speaker {                   // a pure contract
    default String speak() { return "..."; }  // default: no state
}
// several interfaces per class; only ONE abstract superclass
157

How can you create a directory with Java I/O?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

The method is called Files.createDirectory(path) — a factory method from the Files class, that takes a path as a parameter; the pattern is very simple. You can also pass file attributes that can set the permissions you want for this directory — here is a classical example for a public directory. One last word: there is also a Files.createDirectories(path) method, that takes the same parameters, but that can create any intermediate directory on the path to the directory you want to create.

Files.createDirectory(Path.of("logs"));            // the parent must exist
Files.createDirectory(Path.of("pub"),
        PosixFilePermissions.asFileAttribute(
            PosixFilePermissions.fromString("rwxr-xr-x")));

Files.createDirectories(Path.of("a/b/c"));         // creates a, b AND c
158

How is the Map.values() method working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Map.values() is a mutable view on the values of a map.

Less short answer

Map.values returns the collection of the values present in your map — a view on the content of your map that reflects its modifications. This view is also mutable: it supports remove — from the collection itself and from an iterator — removeAll and retainAll. It does not support the addition of elements. Removing a value removes the corresponding key-value pair. One last word: if a value is present several times in your map, then one of them is removed — the first that is found when your implementation iterates over them... which may not be the first that you added to your map.

Map<String, Integer> m = new LinkedHashMap<>();
m.put("a", 1); m.put("b", 1); m.put("c", 2);

Collection<Integer> values = m.values();
values.remove(1);          // removes "a"=1 (the first one found)
m.size();                  // 2
values.add(3);             // UnsupportedOperationException
159

What is the first thing a constructor does? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It calls another constructor.

Less short answer

This other constructor can be a constructor from the same class — called by this(...) — or from a superclass, called by super(...). If you don't write any such code explicitly, then the compiler will add one for you. This series of super calls lands in Object. In the case of records, any constructor other than the canonical constructor must call another constructor — and in the end, this chain of calls must call the canonical constructor. One last word: you cannot add any instruction before this call to this or super — being able to execute code before this call is actually being discussed and should become a feature (JEP 513). We will talk more about this later.

class Base { Base() { } }

class Child extends Base {
    Child() { super(); }        // the compiler inserts it if you don't
    Child(int x) { this(); }    // delegates in the same class
}   // the chain always ends in Object()

record Point(int x, int y) {
    Point() { this(0, 0); }     // record: EVERY path leads to the canonical
}
160

How can you create a stream from an Iterator?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a code pattern for that. (no short answer — straight to the pattern)

Less short answer

First, you need to create a spliterator from your iterator, using the Spliterators factory class — the spliteratorUnknownSize factory method also takes the characteristics of the spliterator you want to create; there are several you can choose from: DISTINCT, NONNULL, SORTED, for instance. Then you need to pass this spliterator to the StreamSupport.stream(...) factory class, telling the API if your stream is parallel — and that's it.

Iterator<String> it = legacySource();

Spliterator<String> sp = Spliterators.spliteratorUnknownSize(
        it, Spliterator.ORDERED | Spliterator.NONNULL);

Stream<String> stream = StreamSupport.stream(sp, /* parallel? */ false);
stream.map(String::trim).forEach(System.out::println);
161

Why should you not use static initializers?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Because you can't prevent this code from running.

Less short answer

You can run your application in two contexts, at least — in production, and to run your tests. Odds are that your production context is not the same as your test context. Suppose you create a logger that logs to a file or a database: then your tests need the same file system, or the same database. You can play with the configuration files for that — but then what your code does is not written in your code anymore. Using dependency injection, for instance, leads to a code that is much easier to understand — and to test. One last word: there are some examples of static initializers in the JDK itself — and that's okay, because that's the same JDK, whether you're using it to test your application or to run it in a production environment.

// bad: runs ALWAYS at class loading, in both contexts
static final Logger LOG = createFileLogger();  // tests: need the file!

// good: an injected dependency, a controlled context
class Service {
    private final Logger log;
    Service(Logger log) { this.log = log; }   // a test passes a fake logger
}
162

What is a read-write lock? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A lock that enables parallel reads and blocks on writes.

Less short answer

Locks are used to prevent race conditions — the race condition occurs when two threads are writing and reading the same field: will thread B see the value written by thread A? Synchronization achieves that by blocking everything. Read-write locks work with two locks: one for the read operations, and another one for the write operations. The write lock is exclusive — no other lock can be taken when it is active; the read lock is not: you can have any number of active read locks. One last word: of course, this is an optimization — it pays off only if your number of write operations is much smaller than the number of read operations.

ReadWriteLock rw = new ReentrantReadWriteLock();

String read() { rw.readLock().lock();                // N readers together
    try { return data; } finally { rw.readLock().unlock(); } }

void write(String v) { rw.writeLock().lock();        // 1, exclusive
    try { data = v; } finally { rw.writeLock().unlock(); } }
163

What is the difference between an Iterator and a Spliterator?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An iterator is used by collections; a spliterator is used by streams.

Less short answer

Spliterator comes from the contraction of iterator and split — which means that besides iterating over the data, it can split it. The iterator is pulling its data from a source, with next; the spliterator, on the other hand, sends a consumer with its tryAdvance method — and lets this source call this consumer; and if there is no more element, this tryAdvance method should return false. So: the iterator works on a pull model, the spliterator on a push model. One last word: you can actually implement your own spliterator to create operations on streams — like shifting, sliding, zipping, or even folding; it's a little tricky, but doable.

// pull (collections): you ask
for (Iterator<String> it = list.iterator(); it.hasNext(); )
    use(it.next());

// push (streams): it delivers to the consumer
spliterator.tryAdvance(elem -> use(elem));    // 1 element
spliterator.forEachRemaining(this::use);      // the rest
Spliterator<String> part = spliterator.trySplit(); // split!
164

How is the Map.keySet() method working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Map.keySet() is a mutable view on the keys of a map.

Less short answer

Map.keySet is a set of the keys of your map — you can't have duplicates among your keys, so that's a set. This view is mutable — but not all operations are supported in it: you can delete keys from the set with remove, removeAll, clear, and retainAll, and you can also delete elements through its iterator; but you cannot add elements to it — if you try to do that, you will get an UnsupportedOperationException. One last word: do not use a key set if what you need is to iterate over the key-value pairs of your map, because it's inefficient — you have another set for that, the one you get with map.entrySet().

Map<String, Integer> m = new LinkedHashMap<>(Map.of("a", 1, "b", 2));
Set<String> keys = m.keySet();

keys.remove("a");        // removes the ENTRY from the map!
keys.add("c");           // UnsupportedOperationException
m.containsKey("a");      // false — the entry is gone

// iterating over the pairs? entrySet (not keySet + get: inefficient)
for (var e : m.entrySet()) use(e.getKey(), e.getValue());
165

What is a garbage collector?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A wonderful piece of technology that does incredible things for you.

Less short answer

A garbage collector is an element of the Java virtual machine that works under the hood and takes care of your memory for you. There are actually several garbage collectors available, which you can choose from — you decide which one you want to use when you launch your application. It is the responsibility of your garbage collector to give you the memory you need to create your objects, and to reclaim this memory when you are not using it anymore. One last word: garbage collectors are actually critical to get good performance for your application, and there is a lot of investment in this piece of technology — the two latest ones, ZGC and Generational ZGC, are doing wonders. (Another video.)

java -XX:+UseG1GC App        # default: G1 (balanced)
java -XX:+UseZGC App         # sub-millisecond pauses
java -XX:+UseParallelGC App  # throughput
java -XX:+UseSerialGC App    # small applications/containers
166

What is a final class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class that you cannot extend.

Less short answer

final is a keyword that you can add on many elements of your source code. If you add it on the declaration of your class, and someone tries to extend this class, then the compiler will generate an error. There are elements that you can create in Java that are compiled as final classes: this is the case for enums, compiled as a final class that extends java.lang.Enum — and the case for records too, also compiled as a final class that extends java.lang.Record. One last word: instead of forbidding the extension of your class, you can also control it by making it sealed and publishing the list of classes that can extend it — it works really well, especially in conjunction with the switch on types. (Another video.)

final class Util { }                 // nobody extends it
class Child extends Util { }         // compile-time ERROR

enum Color { RED }          // compiles as: final class Color extends Enum
record Point(int x, int y) { } // final class Point extends Record

public sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square { } // control
167

How can you join the elements of a collection of strings?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

You can use the String.join(delimiter, iterable) method of the String class — it can also take a vararg. If you don't need a delimiter, you can still pass an empty string to this method. You should really use this pattern: it has been carefully optimized to make sure that it is as fast as possible — much faster than any StringBuilder-based pattern that you may be using (you can check the source code to get a hint at it). One last word: there are other patterns that you may want to check: one is based on the use of the StringJoiner class, that can take a prefix and a suffix as well; and there is the Collectors.joining collector, which can be useful if you need to map your elements before joining them.

String.join(", ", List.of("a", "b", "c"));   // "a, b, c"
String.join("", "a", "b");                   // varargs: "ab"

new StringJoiner(", ", "[", "]").add("a").add("b").toString(); // "[a, b]"

names.stream().map(String::toUpperCase)
     .collect(Collectors.joining("|"));      // map + join
168

What does volatile mean? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It has to do with concurrency and race conditions.

Less short answer

volatile is a keyword you can use when declaring a field — and you should use it when there is a possibility of a race condition on this field; that is, when several threads can read and modify this field. Declaring a field volatile ensures that the thread reads the last value written to this field — even if this write occurred in another thread. The exact rule is the following: a synchronized or volatile read always returns the last value written by a synchronized or volatile write. One last word: this definition is actually the definition of the happens-before link between read and write operations. (Another video.)

private volatile boolean running = true;

// thread A:             thread B:
// running = false;      while (running) { ... }
//    ^--- without volatile, B may loop FOREVER on the stale value
169

How can you generate random numbers?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is an interface for that.

Less short answer

If what you need is just a simple series of random numbers, you can use the Random class. Starting with JDK 17, random generators implement the RandomGenerator interface, that models what a random generator can do: basically, you can generate series of any primitive type, evenly distributed, or following a Gaussian distribution; you can ask for one element at a time with the next... methods, or you can generate streams of elements. One last word: if you are working in cryptography, or if you need a specific random number generator, then you need to check the documentation of the java.util.random package — it gives you all the details of the algorithms available in the JDK.

RandomGenerator rng = Random.from(RandomGeneratorFactory
        .getDefault().create(42));          // seedable, for tests
rng.nextInt(100); rng.nextGaussian();

rng.ints(1, 7).limit(5);                    // streams of randoms
ThreadLocalRandom.current().nextInt();      // concurrency
SecureRandom.getInstanceStrong();           // cryptography
170

What is a Supplier?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A functional interface.

Less short answer

The Supplier interface models lambdas that do not take any parameter and return something. These lambdas are very useful when you need to model the construction of an object. Suppose that you need to log a message, or to throw an exception — but this message or this exception is expensive to create. If you pass it as an argument, then it will be created when you call the method — even if this method decides not to use it; this can be the case if your log level is not the right one, or if there is no need to throw this exception. In that case, you can pass a supplier: if the method needs to create this message or this exception, then it can call the get() method of this supplier. One last word: suppliers are very useful — you should use them wherever you can.

// expensive: the string is built ALWAYS, even with debug off
log.debug("details: " + generateWholeReport());

// with a supplier: built only if the level is active
log.debug(() -> "details: " + generateWholeReport());

Supplier<List<String>> factory = ArrayList::new;   // lazy by nature
Objects.requireNonNullElseGet(x, ArrayList::new);  // a lazy default
171

How can you join the elements of a stream of strings?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a collector for that.

Less short answer

You can call collect(Collectors.joining(...)) on your stream. This joining factory method has several overloads: the first one doesn't take any parameter and joins all the strings of your stream; the second one takes a separator — a string that is inserted between each element; and the third one takes a separator, a prefix and a suffix. And yes: these last two handle empty streams or single elements properly. One last word: be careful — this collector works only on streams of strings; if you pass it to a stream of objects, for instance, you will get a compiler error.

Stream.of("a", "b", "c")
      .collect(Collectors.joining());               // "abc"
Stream.of("a", "b", "c")
      .collect(Collectors.joining(", "));           // "a, b, c"
Stream.of("a", "b", "c")
      .collect(Collectors.joining(", ", "[", "]")); // "[a, b, c]"
Stream.of().collect(Collectors.joining(", ", "[", "]")); // "[]" — ok!
172

What does transient mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is used to prevent a field from being serialized.

Less short answer

Serialization consists in saving a root object, along with all the graph of objects it points to, to a binary stream — that can be saved to a file or sent over the network; you can then recreate all this graph on another machine. Sometimes your object has references to elements that are sensitive or system-dependent — like files or network connections — and you don't want these elements to be serialized. Well, that's where you can use the transient keyword: add it on the field, and this field will not be serialized. One last word: serialization used to be a great idea when Java was created — but it brings many issues, including security issues. Nowadays: stay away from it.

class Session implements Serializable {
    String user;                    // serialized
    transient char[] password;      // NOT serialized (sensitive!)
    transient Socket connection;    // NOT serialized (system-dependent)
}
173

How does the forEach() method work?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It iterates over the elements of an Iterable and passes them to a Consumer.

Less short answer

There are actually three forEach methods: one on the Iterable interface and another one on the Stream interface — that both take a Consumer — and a third one on the Map interface, that takes a BiConsumer. This method iterates over the elements of the iterable, of the stream, or over the key-value pairs of your map, and passes them one by one to the consumer you pass as an argument — in the case of maps, the BiConsumer accepts the key and the value of each of your key-value pairs. One last word: apart from printing the content of a collection or a map, doing side effects in your consumer is, most of the time, a bad idea — try to use streams instead of doing side effects.

list.forEach(System.out::println);           // Iterable
stream.forEach(System.out::println);         // Stream
map.forEach((key, value) ->                  // Map: a BiConsumer
        System.out.println(key + "=" + value));
174

What is the difference between a Lock and a Semaphore?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Lock is an interface; Semaphore is a class. (...but that's not the point)

Less short answer

Locks and semaphores are doing the same kind of thing. Locks can hold only one permit, and semaphores can hold as many as you need — and it's still not the point. The biggest difference is that a lock may enforce that the thread that releases the lock is the same as the thread that acquired it — and it is the case for the ReentrantLock implementation — whereas the semaphore explicitly states that the thread that releases a permit can be a different thread than the one that acquired it. One last word: this feature is very useful — it may allow you to unlock systems from the outside, just by releasing permits from any other thread.

Lock lock = new ReentrantLock();
lock.lock();
lock.unlock();   // rule: whoever locks, unlocks (ownership)

Semaphore sem = new Semaphore(3);
sem.acquire();           // thread A takes a permit
// ... in another thread:
sem.release();           // thread B releases! no ownership at all
175

What is the difference between Enum and Enumeration?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Enumeration is an interface; Enum is a class. The only common element between both is the first four letters of their name. (the presenter's joke)

Less short answer

Enumeration is old stuff that you should not be using anymore — it's an interface that has been superseded by Iterator when the Collections framework was added to the JDK — that was Java 2, in 1998. Enum is a great addition of JDK 5 that allows you to control the instances of a given class: if you have only one instance, it gives you a singleton — and enums are the preferred pattern to create singletons. One last word: enums are great — they can have methods, state, and even custom constructors. Enumerations? Well, just forget about them.

// Enumeration (1998): legacy
for (Enumeration<String> e = vector.elements(); e.hasMoreElements(); )
    System.out.println(e.nextElement());

// Enum (JDK 5): a closed type, with methods, fields and a free singleton
enum Planet {
    MERCURY(3.3e23), EARTH(5.97e24);   // a custom constructor
    final double mass;
    Planet(double m) { mass = m; }
    double relativeWeight() { return mass / EARTH.mass; }
}
176

What is a preview feature?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A way to test upcoming features of the JDK before they become final.

Less short answer

It's not just about testing — it's also about providing feedback: so if you spot a bug, or think about an improvement, then you can go to the mailing list and talk about it — you can even get answers from the people who actually wrote the feature. You can find the right mailing list in the JEP that describes the feature. Preview features need to be activated to be used — so no risk of using them by accident; usually you will activate them in your IDE. One last word: be aware that a preview feature may change before becoming final — it can even be dropped. So avoid using them in your application, unless you really know what you're doing.

# activate at 2 levels (compilation and execution):
javac --enable-preview --release 25 App.java
java  --enable-preview App
# a preview can CHANGE or DISAPPEAR before becoming final!
177

How can you reduce a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are several methods for that.

Less short answer

Reducing a stream means combining all the elements produced by this stream into one final element — that can be of a different type. There are three reduce methods on the Stream interface, and two collect methods that reduce in a mutable container. LongStream and DoubleStream have only two reduce and one collect — but they have a sum, a min/max, and also a nifty summaryStatistics() method, that does the max, the min, the count and the average — all in one pass over your data. One last word: some reduce methods return an Optional — and there is a very good reason for that. (Another video.)

Optional<Integer> r1 = stream.reduce(Integer::max);
Integer r2 = stream.reduce(0, Integer::sum, Integer::sum);

DoubleSummaryStatistics st = DoubleStream.of(1.5, 2.5, 3.0)
        .summaryStatistics();   // count=3, avg=2.333..., max=3.0
178

How is HashSet working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It wraps a HashMap.

Less short answer

HashSet needs to store objects that are different, and refuses to add an object that it already contains — it uses an internal HashMap for that. It adds your object to this HashMap, putting them as the keys of the map, associated to a dumb value called PRESENT in the source code. One consequence is that, just like map keys, you should not mutate the state of an object once you have added it to a HashSet — if you do that (and the hashCode changes), your set will not work properly. One last word: if you have a lot of objects to add to a set, try to create it with the right size upfront — there is a factory method for that (new HashSet<>(capacity)).

Set<String> s = new HashSet<>(1000);  // avoids rehashing while populating
s.add("a");    // internal: map.put("a", PRESENT)
s.add("a");    // put returns PRESENT != null -> add returns false

// DON'T: mutating an object after insertion (hashCode changes = it "vanishes")
179

What is JShell?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A REPL.

Less short answer

REPL stands for Read, Evaluate, Print, Loop. It's actually a command-line tool that you can use to quickly write snippets of code and test them. You can launch it with the jshell command — that can take a bunch of options, by the way; you can see them by typing jshell -h. Then you can create variables, call methods, create records, and experiment with any feature — you can even activate the preview features in JShell, so you can experiment with them too. One last word: you can even write your Java code on several lines with JShell — very handy to create complex records, for instance.

$ jshell
jshell> record Point(int x, int y) { }
jshell> var p = new Point(1, 2);
p ==> Point[x=1, y=2]
jshell> p.x() + p.y()
$3 ==> 3
jshell> /exit
180

What is the difference between Comparator and Comparable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A Comparator can compare two objects; a Comparable can compare itself to another object.

Less short answer

Both are interfaces. Comparable defines a method that receives another object from the same class — so a comparable object can compare itself to some other object. Comparator is also an interface, for the same type of objects: these objects can be both comparable, but a comparator can implement a different comparison. One last word: unless you really need it, don't implement your comparators yourself — check the Comparator interface first: there are plenty of factory and default methods that you can use for that.

record Person(String name, int age) implements Comparable<Person> {
    public int compareTo(Person o) { return name.compareTo(o.name); }
}

Comparator<Person> byAge = Comparator.comparingInt(Person::age);
Comparator<Person> full = byAge.thenComparing(Person::name).reversed();
// compareTo = natural order; Comparator = external strategies
181

What is the difference between the reduce() and collect() methods?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

They don't work the same way.

Less short answer

These methods are methods from the Stream API. reduce consists in applying a reduction operation — an immutable one — on the elements consumed by your stream. This operation is modeled by a BiFunction, that should be associative. collect reduces your elements in a mutable container — typically a collection or a map. Stream<T> has a collect method that takes a Collector as a parameter, whereas specialized streams (IntStream and the like) do not — that's for type reasons: there is no specialized collector. One last word: some reduce methods return an Optional, and sometimes the corresponding collector does the same — but not always: that's the case for the summarizing collectors, that return the statistics object. (Another video.)

// reduce: immutable, associative
Optional<Integer> sum = stream.reduce(Integer::sum);
Integer sum0 = stream.reduce(0, Integer::sum);

// collect: a mutable reduction, in a container
List<Integer> list = stream.collect(Collectors.toList());

IntStream.of(1, 2).reduce(Integer::sum);   // OK
// IntStream has no collect(Collector): no specialized collectors
IntSummaryStatistics st = IntStream.of(1, 2).summaryStatistics();
182

What is a vararg?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An array.

Less short answer

A vararg is a feature of the language that allows you to pass zero or more elements to a method: you declare the parameter with the three dots, and then you can call it with any number of Strings. There are two constraints with varargs. First, you can only use one vararg per method; and second, this vararg parameter should be the last of your parameters — no parameter is allowed after a vararg; your compiler will tell you that. In your method, this vararg is an array; and by the way, you can also pass an array directly to a method that declares a vararg. One last word: you can also use varargs with constructors — and because you can pass an array, you should still use a defensive copy with varargs.

static int sum(int... numbers) {   // it's an int[] inside
    int t = 0;
    for (int n : numbers) t += n;
    return t;
}
sum();                 // 0 elements: OK
sum(1, 2, 3);          // any count
sum(new int[]{1, 2});  // a plain array works too!
// int sum(int... a, int b)  // ERROR: the vararg must be last
183

How should you close an IO resource?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You should call its close method.

Less short answer

The only safe way, that guarantees that this method is called in all circumstances, is to open your IO resources in a try-with-resources statement. In that way, even if an exception is interrupting your normal process, your close method is called when your program exits the try block. One last word: you can use any object that implements AutoCloseable with a try-with-resources statement — and to implement this interface, you just need to create a close method. So creating your own AutoCloseable objects is actually very simple — you should definitely use these patterns in your applications.

try (var reader = Files.newBufferedReader(Path.of("a.txt"));
     var writer = Files.newBufferedWriter(Path.of("b.txt"))) {
    writer.write(reader.readLine());   // even with an exception...
}   // ...both close() run, in the REVERSE order of the declaration
184

What is the difference between a Fork/Join pool and an ExecutorService? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

ExecutorService is an interface; the Fork/Join pool is a class. (an implementation of the former)

Less short answer

There are many implementations of ExecutorService — and ForkJoinPool, by the way, is one of them. There is one point worth mentioning: with the cached thread pools and fixed thread pools available from the Executors factory class, your tasks are stored in a single waitlist from which the threads can take them. In a ForkJoinPool, there is one waitlist per thread, and the pool implements the work stealing pattern — so a thread can steal a task from another waitlist in case its own is empty. One last word: as of now, there are two ForkJoinPools used by the JVM: one to run your parallel streams, and another one to run your virtual threads.

ExecutorService fixed = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(8);  // 1 global queue
ForkJoinPool fjp = (ForkJoinPool) Executors.newWorkStealingPool();
// one queue per thread + work stealing from the opposite ends
185

What is a spliterator? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

This interface models the way you can connect a stream to a source of data. The name comes from iterator and split — splitting is the feature used by parallel streams. Implementing this interface is not so easy: you have four methods to implement — trySplit, estimateSize, characteristics and tryAdvance, which is the one called to get the elements one by one — and four more default methods that you can redefine: getExactSizeIfKnown, forEachRemaining, hasCharacteristics and getComparator. One last word: understanding how all this is working can be very interesting for your application. (Another video.)

Spliterator<Integer> sp = Spliterators.spliterator(
        new int[]{1, 2, 3}, Spliterator.ORDERED | Spliterator.SIZED);

sp.tryAdvance(n -> System.out.println(n));  // element by element
Spliterator<Integer> half = sp.trySplit();  // split for parallelism
186

How can you get an instance of a Class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are actually four ways to do that, at least — depending on what you have.

Less short answer

If what you have is an object, then you can call getClass() on it — which is also working for arrays. Second, you can use the Type.class syntax, that works for object types and primitive types. If what you have is the name of a class, you can call Class.forName(...) — it also works with arrays. To get the class instance that represents a primitive type, you can also use the TYPE constant defined in all the wrapper types — and starting with JDK 22, you can get the class that represents a primitive type with the Class.forPrimitiveName("int") method. One last word: you can also get classes from other classes, with getSuperclass() or getInterfaces().

String s = "a";
s.getClass();                         // 1) from the object
String.class;                         // 2) the literal (object or primitive)
Class.forName("java.lang.String");    // 3) by name
Integer.TYPE;  int.class;             // 4) TYPE / forPrimitiveName (JDK 22)
Class.forName("[Ljava.lang.String;"); // arrays too!
187

What does it mean to capture a variable? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It means that you have a local class that references a variable from its enclosing method.

Less short answer

Starting with JDK 8, local classes can capture final variables — this variable can also be effectively final, which means that the compiler adds the final keyword for you; it is only possible if you do not modify this variable in your code. Odds are that you will do that with lambda expressions — and in that case, this lambda is called a capturing lambda. One last word: there is a very good reason why lambda expressions can only capture final variables — that is, values, not modifiable variables — and it has to do with concurrency. (Another video.)

Function<Integer, Integer> addWith(int base) {
    int adjust = base * 2;             // effectively final
    return x -> x + adjust;            // a capturing lambda
}
// int adjust2 = ...; adjust2++; -> a lambda over adjust2 would not compile
188

What is serialization?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A way to save graphs of objects in files — or to send them over the network.

Less short answer

Serialization used to be one of the killer features of the Java platform. It turns out that now the situation has changed: there are several security problems with it. For instance, deserializing an object does not call any of its constructors — thus bypassing any validation rules; and so it can be used by some malicious code to inject corrupted objects in your application. One last word: there are several ways to protect your application against that. First, you can use records — deserialization calls the canonical constructor of records. And then you can use deserialization filters at the JVM level. (Another video.)

class Order implements Serializable { ... }  // a marker interface

// protection 1: records validate on the way back
record Ssn(String value) {
    Ssn { if (!value.matches("\\d{9}")) throw new IllegalArgumentException(); }
}
// protection 2: an ObjectInputFilter (JEP 290) on deserialization
189

What does happen-before mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It has to do with concurrency.

Less short answer

There are several references to happens-before links in the JavaDoc — for instance, the Future interface tells you that the actions taken by the asynchronous computation happen before the actions following the corresponding future.get(). The problem may occur if you modify your variable, and this variable is read by another thread: each thread operates on its own CPU cache, and you need a special operation to make sure that the modification you made is visible from all the other CPU caches — and this special operation is triggered by the fact that the happens-before link exists between the write operation and the read operation. One last word: your only source of information should be Chapter 17 of the Java Language Specification (the Java Memory Model).

// without happens-before: the write may NEVER be seen
boolean ready = false;
// thread A: data = 42; ready = true;
// thread B: while (!ready) {}; use(data);  // data may be 0 (!)

// with happens-before (volatile write -> volatile read):
volatile boolean readyV;
// thread A: data = 42; readyV = true;   // written BEFORE (hb)
// thread B: if (readyV) use(data);      // read AFTER: data=42 guaranteed
190

When can you use finalize()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Never.

Less short answer

Never. Any code you put in finalize is, most of the time, buggy, useless — or both. Don't try to help the garbage collector by clearing your collections or your maps: first, the garbage collector is smart enough to do it itself; and second, you may have race conditions in this code, that may lead to NullPointerExceptions, among other nasty bugs. Don't close your resources in finalize — they may never be closed; prefer the try-with-resources pattern, which is much safer. One last word: finalize is now deprecated for removal — maybe it will stay there for a while, but at some point the garbage collector will not call it anymore. So stop overriding this method.

class Bad {
    @Deprecated(forRemoval = true)
    @Override protected void finalize() {   // NEVER
        map.clear();     // potential race condition (GC thread!)
        connection.close(); // may never run -> leak
    }
}
// right: try-with-resources + (if needed) a Cleaner
191

How can you skip elements in a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

You can call skip(n) on a stream, passing the number of elements you need to skip as an argument. That being said, there are several caveats you need to be aware of: for instance, skipping elements only makes sense for ordered streams. If your stream lists all the lines of a text file, then calling skip(2) skips the first two lines of your text. Skipping elements on a non-ordered stream leads to unpredictable results. One last word: skipping may be costly to call on parallel streams — you may need inter-thread communication to drop the first elements you want to skip; there is another overhead in doing that.

Files.lines(path)                  // ordered (the lines of the file)
     .skip(2)                      // the first two lines are gone
     .forEach(System.out::println);

Set.of(1, 2, 3).stream().skip(2);  // not ordered: unpredictable!
192

What is an anonymous class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class that does not have a name.

Less short answer

The syntax allows you to create the class and its instance at the same time. It's useful when you need to quickly implement an interface or extend a class right in your code. There are cases where an anonymous class can be written as a lambda — but not always: a lambda implements a functional interface; an anonymous class can extend anything — and you cannot create a constructor in an anonymous class (you can use an instance block if you need to do some initialization). One last word: you can use anonymous classes in conjunction with the var keyword — which gives you access to the associated non-denotable type. (Another video.)

Runnable task = new Runnable() {      // the class + the instance at once
    @Override public void run() { System.out.println("I ran"); }
};

var box = new Object() {              // anonymous + var: access to the type
    int value = 0;                    // non-denotable (JEP 286)
    void add() { value++; }
};
box.add(); box.value++;               // members of the type itself!
193

How can you read a GZIP compressed file?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a class for that.

Less short answer

This class is the GZIPInputStream, and it takes another input stream as a constructor argument — so it's actually a decoration on another input stream. Along with the GZIPOutputStream, this class allows you to read and write data compressed in the gzip format. Note that the gzip algorithm supports streaming — meaning that you can get the uncompressed data while you are reading the compressed stream, with the different read methods available. One last word: Java I/O also supports the zip file format, which allows you to compress directory trees — but that will be for another time.

try (var gz = new GZIPInputStream(
             Files.newInputStream(Path.of("log.txt.gz")));
     var reader = new BufferedReader(
             new InputStreamReader(gz, StandardCharsets.UTF_8))) {
    reader.lines().forEach(System.out::println);  // decompresses while reading
}
194

What is a LinkedHashSet?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A set with linked elements.

Less short answer

This set maintains links between its elements so that it maintains the iteration order. If you add "C, A, B, B" in a HashSet, the second B is not added — and if you iterate over the elements of this set, you will get "A, B, C", because the HashSet does not maintain the order of its elements. If you do the same with a LinkedHashSet, you will get "C, A, B" — the LinkedHashSet does what is needed to maintain this order. One last word: starting with JDK 21, LinkedHashSet implements SequencedSet, which brings new methods — like direct access to the first and last element of the set, and the reversed() method.

Set<String> hs = new HashSet<>(List.of("C", "A", "B", "B"));
hs.stream().forEach(System.out::print);   // A B C (any order)

LinkedHashSet<String> lhs = new LinkedHashSet<>(List.of("C", "A", "B", "B"));
lhs.forEach(System.out::print);           // C A B (insertion order)
lhs.getFirst();   // "C"  (JDK 21+, SequencedSet)
lhs.reversed();   // a B A C view
195

What is an intermediate collector?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A collector.

Less short answer

The Collectors factory class gives you a bunch of pre-made, ready-to-use collectors — and some of the factory methods model stream intermediate operations, like mapping, filtering, or flatMapping. These are called intermediate collectors. But then there is a problem: a collector models the terminal stream operation — so these factory methods also take another collector, to compose the mapping operation, for instance, with this other collector. You can then create complex collector compositions, ending with the terminal collector — like toList() or counting(). One last word: the Collector API is quite complex, but also very powerful — you should definitely check it out; we will talk more about it in the future.

// mapping = an intermediate collector: it needs a downstream
Map<String, List<String>> byCountry = cities.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
                City::country,                                 // terminal
                Collectors.mapping(City::name,                  // intermediate
                        Collectors.filtering(n -> !n.isBlank(), // intermediate
                                Collectors.toList()))));        // terminal
196

What is a Hashtable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something you should not be using anymore.

Less short answer

Hashtable was created in the early days of the JDK, before the introduction of the Collections framework. The implementation is very similar to HashMap — apart from the handling of hash collisions, which is not as efficient, and from the fact that all the map methods are synchronized. So if you do not need synchronized methods, you can use the HashMap — in general, HashMap is always a better choice; and if you do need synchronization, then ConcurrentHashMap is also a better choice. One last word: be careful — Hashtable does not support null values or null keys, and if you try to put a null key, you will get a NullPointerException. HashMap supports null keys and null values — even if it may not be the greatest idea.

// legacy (JDK 1.0): everything synchronized, no nulls
Hashtable<String, String> ht = new Hashtable<>();

// modern:
Map<String, String> hm = new HashMap<>();           // no sync, with nulls
Map<String, String> chm = new ConcurrentHashMap<>(); // real concurrency
197

What is a Consumer?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A functional interface.

Less short answer

This functional interface models a lambda that takes a single parameter and does not return anything. The Consumer is what you pass to the forEach method, that you have on the streams and the collections. You can compose consumers thanks to an andThen default method on the Consumer interface — so you can create one consumer from two others, just by calling print.andThen(log), for instance. One last word: the java.util.function package also gives you a BiConsumer, that takes two parameters instead of one, and specialized consumers: IntConsumer, LongConsumer and DoubleConsumer.

Consumer<String> print = s -> System.out.println(s);
Consumer<String> log   = s -> logger.info(s);

list.forEach(print.andThen(log));   // composition: prints AND logs

BiConsumer<String, Integer> put = Map::put;   // two parameters
IntConsumer printInt = System.out::println;   // primitive: no boxing
198

How can you read chars from a binary stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a class for that.

Less short answer

The class is InputStreamReader, which extends Reader — so it can read characters — and that takes an InputStream as a constructor argument: InputStreamReader decorates an existing input stream, reading its binary content, and exposing it as characters. By default, the characters you read are encoded using the default charset — and starting with JDK 18, this charset is UTF-8. One last word: you also have an OutputStreamWriter class, that extends Writer, so that it can write characters and strings on the output stream it is built on.

try (var reader = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(
        Files.newInputStream(Path.of("data.bin")), StandardCharsets.UTF_8))) {
    reader.lines().forEach(System.out::println);
}
// InputStreamReader decorates: bytes -> chars (UTF-8 default since JDK 18)
199

Can you build an object without calling its constructor?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

No. (...actually, yes — see the less short answer.)

Less short answer

The Java language is designed in such a way that calling a constructor is the only way to create an object. Of course, you can create factory methods that you call from your application code, so that this constructor code does not appear in this application code — but this factory method does call the constructor. There is, however, one exception to that, which is serialization: when you deserialize an object, the deserialization mechanism does not call the constructor of that object — it bypasses it. Which is actually a security issue: if you have some validation rules in your constructor, they will be bypassed by the deserialization mechanism — allowing corrupted objects to live in your application. One last word: records were created after serialization was implemented — and they are an exception to the exception: deserialization does call your record constructors and their validation rules; there is no way you can create a record without calling its canonical constructor. One more reason to use them wherever you can.

// Deserializing a regular class: the constructor is NOT called (validation bypassed)
ObjectInputStream in = new ObjectInputStream(new FileInputStream("order.ser"));
Order o = (Order) in.readObject(); // the constructor's rules are skipped!

// Records: always go through the canonical constructor, even on deserialization
record Ssn(String value) {
    Ssn {                          // validation guaranteed on EVERY path
        if (!value.matches("\\d{9}")) throw new IllegalArgumentException();
    }
}
200

What is the heap memory?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The memory managed by your garbage collector.

Less short answer

The memory used by the JVM is made of several parts; the heap memory is the one in which your objects are stored. When you call new ArrayList(), for instance, what you get is a reference to some piece of memory that lives in the heap memory. Then, when you do not need this ArrayList anymore, the garbage collector will detect it, and will reclaim it, to use it for other objects. There are several strategies, implemented by the different garbage collectors, depending on what you need for your application. One last word: there is also an off-heap memory, not managed by your garbage collector — the JDK gives you two APIs to access it: the ByteBuffer API and the Memory API. (Another video.)

ArrayList<String> l = new ArrayList<>(); // the object on the HEAP...
l.add("a");                              // ...the reference on the thread stack
l = null;   // unreachable -> the GC reclaims the heap memory

// outside the GC: off-heap (a direct ByteBuffer / MemorySegment+Arena)
201

How is the reduce() method working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It reduces the elements of the stream.

Less short answer

There are two reduce methods on the Stream interface. The first one takes only the reduction operation, in the form of a lambda expression: the first and the second element of the stream are passed to this lambda; then the result and the third element; then the result and the fourth — until all the elements of the stream. The second overload takes the identity element of your reduction operation. This identity element is then used as the first element of the reduction; and if your stream is empty and you gave an identity element, then this element is returned. If not, then the reduction is undefined — the reason why this reduction returns an empty Optional. One last word: that's why you need Optionals in Java — to have correct reductions.

OptionalInt sum1 = IntStream.of(1, 2, 3, 4).reduce(Integer::sum);
// 1+2=3; 3+3=6; 6+4=10 -> OptionalInt[10]

int sum2 = IntStream.of(1, 2, 3, 4).reduce(0, Integer::sum);
// identity 0 -> 10 (an empty stream would return 0)

int sum3 = IntStream.empty().reduce(100, Integer::sum); // 100
IntStream.empty().reduce(Integer::sum);   // OptionalInt.empty
202

What are the primitive types in Java?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are 8 of them.

Less short answer

There are 4 integer types — byte, short, int and long, of length 8, 16, 32 and 64 bits — then 2 floating point numbers, float and double, of length 32 and 64 bits; char for characters, and boolean. Primitive types are values, not references — so a primitive type can never be null. They are all associated with a wrapper type with the same name, apart from int → Integer and char → Character. One last word: operating on primitive types is super fast — no indirection, no pointer chasing. This is what you need to get the best performance — and this is what the Valhalla project aims to bring for object types. (Another video.)

byte   b = 127;           // 8 bits
short  s = 32_767;        // 16
int    i = 2_000_000_000; // 32
long   l = 9_000_000_000L; // 64
float  f = 1.5f;          // 32
double d = 1.5;           // 64
char   c = 'J';           // 16 (UTF-16)
boolean ok = true;

Integer wi = i;        // wrapper: can be null, has a header...
int added = i + 1;     // ...primitive: a pure value, it flies
203

What is an Optional? *(where it comes from)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A wrapper type that can be empty.

Less short answer

Optionals are there to correctly handle reduction in the case of empty streams. In that case, you would need to return the identity element of your reduction operation — you need that because, if you combine this result with the reduction of another non-empty stream, you still need the correct result. The problem is that many reductions do not have an identity element — that's the case for max, min or average. Returning an empty Optional is a way to solve this issue. One last word: even if you never produce empty streams on purpose, you may still have to deal with them because of filter operations or parallel streams — and both can produce empty streams without you realizing it.

Optional<Integer> max = Stream.<Integer>of().max(Integer::compareTo);
// max() has no identity (the max of nothing does not exist) -> Optional

Stream.of(1, 2).filter(n -> n > 99)   // empty WITHOUT you realizing
      .max(Integer::compareTo);       // Optional.empty
204

What is an expression?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something that produces a value.

Less short answer

An expression is a combination of any operand, operators or method calls that, in the end, produces a single value. There are language constructs in Java that are not expressions — that's the case for the if/else or the while statements. The switch used to be a statement, but is now also an expression as of JDK 14 — and it is suddenly much more useful. You can switch on integer types (not long!), enumerations, strings and types. One last word: creating switch expressions on sealed types is part of the data oriented programming paradigm currently being implemented in Java — super interesting. (Another video.)

int x = 1 + f(a) * 2;              // an expression: produces a value

// if is NOT an expression (it's a statement):
if (x > 0) { sign = 1; } else { sign = -1; }

// a switch expression (JDK 14+): produces a value!
String name = switch (code) {
    case 1 -> "one";
    case 2 -> "two";
    default -> "many";
};
205

What is a ByteBuffer?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class from Java NIO.

Less short answer

NIO stands for New Input Output — that was new in 2002; it's now called Non-Blocking IO. A ByteBuffer can be used to allocate memory on the off-heap and to map files in memory — with several caveats. First, they are indexed with ints; and second, they can't be closed explicitly — meaning that you cannot deallocate the off-heap memory. This class is now superseded by the MemorySegment class, indexed with long — memory segments are created by Arenas, that you can close to deallocate them. One last word: memory segments and arenas are part of JEP 454. (Another video.)

// NIO (2002): int, no close
ByteBuffer buf = ByteBuffer.allocateDirect(1_000_000); // off-heap...
// ...no explicit deallocation (waits for the GC)

// FFM (JEP 454): long, with an arena
try (Arena a = Arena.ofConfined()) {
    MemorySegment s = a.allocate(1_000_000);
}   // deallocated here, deterministically
206

What is a non-denotable type?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A type inferred by the compiler.

Less short answer

A non-denotable type is created by the compiler in several places — one of them is when you create an anonymous class; they are also used for intersection types (you can check JEP 286 for more details and examples). You can use the inferred type of an anonymous class with var: in that case, you have access to the fields and methods of the anonymous type — you can use this feature to create efficient mutable boxes, for instance, much more efficient than using an atomic variable. One last word: atomic variables are costly performance-wise, and they are there to handle concurrency — you should not use them outside of this context.

// a mutable box with an anonymous class + var (non-denotable type)
var counter = new Object() { int value = 0; };
counter.value++;         // a field of the anonymous type, accessible via var!
counter.value++;

// AtomicInteger outside of concurrency = cost with no need
207

What is the difference between a Zip and a Gzip stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Gzip can compress a single file; Zip can compress a full directory tree.

Less short answer

Both are built on the same compression algorithm, called DEFLATE, and both are modeled by their own input and output stream classes. But Zip is a little hairy to use: you first need to create Zip entries for directories and files; then, after adding a file entry, you need to add the content of that file. Reading this content mirrors this procedure: you first need to discover the entries, then read the files. One last word: you can actually create a file system for that — and then you can just create directories and copy files to this file system, normally. Much simpler.

// Gzip: a single stream
try (var gz = new GZIPInputStream(Files.newInputStream(Path.of("log.gz")))) {
    gz.readAllBytes();
}

// Zip: a file system, and done (much simpler than manual ZipEntry)
try (FileSystem zip = FileSystems.newFileSystem(Path.of("data.zip"))) {
    Files.copy(Path.of("photo.png"), zip.getPath("/photo.png"),
               StandardCopyOption.REPLACE_EXISTING);
}
208

What is a SequencedMap?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

It is a nice addition to the Collections framework in JDK 21: it models maps that have a defined encounter order. Because this order exists, you can get the first entry (firstEntry) or the first key, the last entry (lastEntry)... and it gives you other methods, like reversed(), that you can use to get a reversed view on the map — no copy is made. Implementation-wise, TreeMap and LinkedHashMap are both sequenced maps. One last word: SequencedCollection and SequencedSet have also been added along with the SequencedMap interface — that's JEP 431, that you can check for all the details, design decisions and examples.

SequencedMap<String, Integer> m = new LinkedHashMap<>();
m.putFirst("a", 1);      // a new JDK 21 API
m.putLast("z", 26);
m.firstEntry();          // a=1
m.lastEntry();           // z=26
m.reversed().firstKey(); // "z" — a view, no copy
// TreeMap (by comparison) and LinkedHashMap (by insertion) implement it
209

What is the Void type?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(no short answer in the video)

Less short answer

This class is used in the Reflection API, which gives you access to the return type of methods — including the ones that can be void. Because getReturnType() returns a Class, this Void type was added to model this return. You could call that a dirty hack. You cannot create an instance of this type, and you cannot get anything of this type — so apart from this use in the Reflection API (and in the MethodHandle API, by the way), there is not much you can do with it. One last word: the compiler lets you use void.class in an instanceof or in a switch on types — but that's pretty useless, because the result will always be false. Don't do it.

// in reflection: models the void return
Method m = Service.class.getMethod("start");
m.getReturnType() == void.class;     // true (Class<Void>)

// in generics: "a result that does not exist"
Callable<Void> task = () -> { run(); return null; };
CompletableFuture<Void> cf = CompletableFuture.runAsync(...);

// Void.TYPE == void.class; an instance of Void: impossible
210

Why are Vector and Hashtable not deprecated?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(no short answer — straight to the point) They stay for backward compatibility.

Less short answer

Removing them would break some old code. Deprecating something creates warnings at compile time, telling you "hey, this isn't safe to use" — and removing them could then be harmful. So they stay as they are: frozen. One last word: maybe in the future there will be a way to advertise that there is a better pattern (in your IDE, for instance) without generating compiler warnings. That would be great.

// frozen legacy — it works, but don't use it in new code:
Vector<String> v = new Vector<>();       // synchronized method by method
Hashtable<String, String> h = new Hashtable<>();

// modern:
List<String> l = new ArrayList<>();      // or truly concurrent:
Map<String, String> m = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
211

What does late binding mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It has to do with knowing what method should be called only at runtime.

Less short answer

When you call an interface method in your code — or any method that can be overridden — the compiler cannot tell which method would be called at runtime, since it depends on the concrete type you have. Hence the name late binding: it means that your method call is bound to an implementation only at runtime. Early binding means that this binding can be resolved at compile time — that's the case when you call a particular overload of a method. One last word: late binding occurs when you call a method that can be overridden; it has a cost, but can be optimized in some cases.

List<String> l = new ArrayList<>(...);
l.size();   // late: ArrayList.size? LinkedList.size? runtime decides

Math.max(1, 2);        // early: the overload resolved at compile time
Math.max(1L, 2L);      // (another overload, already decided)
212

How is Stream.distinct() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It removes the duplicates from the stream.

Less short answer

Internally, distinct adds the elements of the stream in a hash set. When you add an element to a hash set, the add method returns a boolean: if this boolean is false, it means that the element was not added because it was already in the hash set — and in that case, distinct does not push this element to the downstream. If it is true (it was not there), this element is immediately pushed downstream. So internally, distinct materializes all the different elements of your stream. One last word: if you call distinct on an ordered stream, then a LinkedHashSet is used instead of a regular hash set — to preserve the order of your stream.

Stream.of("a", "b", "a", "c", "b")
      .distinct()                     // a LinkedHashSet internally
      .forEach(System.out::print);    // abc

// under the hood: seen.add(x) ? push it downstream : drop it
213

What does capturing a variable mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

"Capturing" is when the lambda refers to a field or to a local variable.

Less short answer

A lambda expression can refer to the fields of a class it is declared in, and to the local variables of the method it is declared in. In case your local variable is not declared final, the compiler will try to make it final for you — and if it can, the variable becomes effectively final, and you can capture it. If it can't, then you get a compiler error. One last word: returning or capturing lambdas can expose the captured state to race conditions — local variables are never subject to race conditions in Java, and that is exactly why you cannot capture them if they are not final.

class Service {
    private int calls;                    // a field: captured through this

    Runnable counter() {
        int local = 42;                   // effectively final: OK
        return () -> {
            use(local);
            // local++ is illegal: only final values can be captured
            calls++;                      // a field: always "sees" changes
        };
    }
}
214

How can you create temporary files?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a flag for that.

Less short answer

The factory methods from the Files factory class can take StandardOpenOptions as parameters — one of them is called DELETE_ON_CLOSE. If you pass it, the JVM will try to delete the corresponding file when you close this writer or this output stream; and if you don't close it — which is a mistake, you should close it — then it will try to delete it when you exit your application. One last word: you should use this option with caution — you can't be sure that some other outside process did not open your file; so deleting it may be either impossible, or dangerous, or bug-inducing.

Path tmp = Files.createTempFile("processing", ".tmp");

try (var out = Files.newOutputStream(tmp,
        StandardOpenOption.DELETE_ON_CLOSE)) {   // deletes on close
    out.write(data);
}   // closed = the temporary file is removed
// without closing: the removal is only attempted when the JVM exits
215

How is Collection.retainAll() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It computes the intersection of two collections.

Less short answer

retainAll only keeps the elements of this collection that are also in the collection you pass as an argument — so, to be kept, an element needs to be present in both collections. retainAll returns a boolean: true if this collection was modified. Internally, retainAll calls contains on the collection you pass for each element of this collection — so if you want this call to be efficient, you should pass HashSets to retainAll. One last word: there are three methods to deal with sets in the Collections framework: addAll for the union, removeAll for the set difference, and retainAll for the intersection.

Collection<String> letters = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "B", "c"));
Set<String> vowels = Set.of("a", "e", "c");   // a HashSet: contains O(1)

boolean changed = letters.retainAll(vowels);
// [a, c]; changed == true
216

What does tearing mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It has to do with concurrency, and 32-bits versus 64-bits machines.

Less short answer

As you know, some types are stored on 64 bits. On a 64-bits machine, writing a long or a double in memory is done in one operation; but on a 32-bits machine, you need two write operations. If you're not cautious in a multi-thread environment, the thread during the write operations can be interrupted — it means that some other thread could see an intermediate state, where only the first 32 bits have been updated, but not the last. This is what tearing is. One last word: you can prevent that using synchronization — but it has a cost.

long timestamp;   // 64 bits

// a 32-bits JVM: two 32-bits writes
// thread A in the middle of the write || thread B reads:
// -> half old + half new = a torn value (torn read)

// protection: volatile (the JMM guarantees atomicity for volatile long/double)
private volatile long safe;
217

What is a stream builder?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A builder for streams. (literally)

Less short answer

You can build a stream by using Stream.builder(). The pattern is the following: first, you create this builder with the builder() factory method; second, you can add objects to it with chained add() calls (or accept()); once you're done adding elements, you can call build() — you can only call build once — and then you can use this stream as any other. Note that this stream is ordered and can accept any value, including nulls. One last word: this is useful when you can only generate your objects one by one — you do not need to put them in an array or a collection; you can rely on the internal spine buffer of this builder.

Stream<String> s = Stream.<String>builder()
        .add("a")
        .add("b")
        .add(null)          // nulls are accepted!
        .build();           // build() only once

// useful when the elements are born one by one:
// while (hasNext()) builder.add(next());
218

How can you stream the content of a text file?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(straight to the two patterns)

Less short answer

There are two patterns, depending on what you have. If what you have is a Path, then you can call the lines() method of the Files factory class, passing this path as an argument — simple. And if what you have is a BufferedReader, then you can call lines() directly on it (note that this lines method does not exist on regular readers). What you get is a stream of the lines of your text file. The nice thing is that the Stream interface is itself AutoCloseable — so you can create this stream in a try-with-resources statement: it will close the stream, which in turn calls the close() method of the underlying reader. One last word: you also have a lines() method on the String class, that does the same thing.

try (Stream<String> lines = Files.lines(Path.of("app.log"))) {
    lines.filter(l -> l.contains("ERROR"))
         .findFirst();
}   // closes the stream AND the file

new BufferedReader(new FileReader(f)).lines();  // the variant
text.lines();                                   // String.lines()
219

What is a Read-Write Lock?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

This has to do with concurrent programming. The Read-Write Lock is useful in situations where you want to allow concurrent reads, but you need to have exclusive write operations. It works better when you have many reads and very few writes. The pattern is the following: you create an instance with the ReentrantReadWriteLock implementation; then acquire the read or the write lock, use it, and release it in a finally block — releasing it there is important, because it will be executed even if you have an exception along the way. One last word: you can impose fairness on this implementation, giving the priority to the longest-waiting thread — but it has a cost.

ReadWriteLock lock = new ReentrantReadWriteLock();

String read() {
    lock.readLock().lock();              // many readers together
    try { return data; }
    finally { lock.readLock().unlock(); }
}
void write(String next) {
    lock.writeLock().lock();             // 1, exclusive with everyone
    try { data = next; }
    finally { lock.writeLock().unlock(); }
}
220

How is Collection.removeIf() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It removes the elements of a collection that match the predicate passed as an argument.

Less short answer

The implementation of removeIf varies depending on the collection you have. The default behavior is implemented in the Collection interface: it iterates over the elements of the collection, and removes them if they match the predicate — it works for a HashSet or a LinkedList, for instance. Then this method is overridden in ArrayList: an ArrayList scans and marks all the elements to be removed, then removes them in a second pass. One last word: be careful — your predicate should not read the collection it works on, nor throw any exception, because then the behavior could be different on an ArrayList and on other implementations.

List<Integer> l = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5));
l.removeIf(n -> n % 2 == 0);   // [1, 3, 5]
