// ArrayList: marks in one pass, compacts in the other (O(n))
// a predicate reading 'l' = unspecified behavior!
221

What is a weak reference?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A reference that is managed by the garbage collector in a certain way.

Less short answer

You can create an instance of WeakReference by passing a reference to any object — what you're doing is actually telling the garbage collector: "hey, if you need some space, you can reclaim this object". If some other object has a regular (strong) reference to your object, then calling get() will always return your object; but if at some point no regular reference exists anymore, then the garbage collector is allowed to set the object your weak reference is pointing to, to null, and reclaim this object — calling get() will then return null. One last word: weak references are used in a WeakHashMap — a map that can be really useful to design caches. (Another video.)

Config big = new Config();                  // a strong reference
WeakReference<Config> weak = new WeakReference<>(big);

weak.get();     // != null as long as 'big' exists
big = null;     // nobody holds it anymore...
System.gc();
weak.get();     // probably null: the GC reclaimed it
222

What is an IdentityHashMap?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A map.

Less short answer

When you look for a key in a map — whether it is to put a new key-value pair, or because you are looking for a key — your first compute a hash code for that key; and because two keys that are different can still have the same hash code, you compare them. In a regular HashMap, you compare them using their equals method; in an IdentityHashMap, you use == — that is, reference equality. So if you run this code, you will end up with one key-value pair in your map... and if you run this other one, you end up with two. One last word: IdentityHashMap can work as long as your keys can be referenced — so don't use value objects in them, because it will fail when Valhalla is there.

record Key(String id) { }
Map<Key, Integer> m = new IdentityHashMap<>();
m.put(new Key("a"), 1);
m.put(new Key("a"), 2);   // different == -> TWO entries!
m.size();                 // 2 (with a HashMap it would be 1)
223

How can you limit a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

Technically speaking, you are not really "limiting" a stream — what you're saying is that, once this stream has processed the given number of elements, it should stop. And you need to be careful, because at each stage, what you get is a new stream object. If what you need is to skip the first two and the last two elements, then writing the "obvious" skip(2).limit(...) does not give you what you expect — the stream on which you call limit has consumed only 4 elements, not 6; the correct code takes that into account. One last word: skipping/limiting only makes sense on ordered streams — and both manage an internal mutable counter, so don't use them in parallel streams.

Stream<Integer> s = Stream.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6);

// skip the first 2 and the last 2:
s.skip(2).limit(6 - 2 - 2)   // limit = total - skipStart - end (not limit(4)!)
 .forEach(System.out::print); // 3 4
224

What is a latch?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A closed door that will open after you knocked on it a certain amount of times.

Less short answer

A CountDownLatch is a class used in concurrent programming. You create an instance of it by giving it a counter; some threads will call latch.await(), and some other threads can also call the latch.countDown() method, which will decrease the internal count. Once this counter reaches zero, then the door opens, and all the threads blocked on the await calls are free to run. Once a latch opens, it cannot be closed again — you can use a latch, for instance, to start an application where you need to initialize resources concurrently. One last word: you can check the CyclicBarrier class if what you need is something that you can close again.

CountDownLatch ready = new CountDownLatch(3);

for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
    new Thread(() -> { initResource(i); ready.countDown(); }).start();
}
ready.await();            // waits for the 3 to knock on the door
System.out.println("all ready");   // the door is open forever
225

What is a try with resources statement?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A great language feature that can close your resources for you.

Less short answer

The try-with-resources statement works with any class that implements AutoCloseable — which defines only one method, close(). So you can use it with all your classes that have a close() method that returns void. You create your instances as the argument of the try; and then, when your program exits the following block of code — whether it is with an exception or not — all the close() methods are called in the right order (the reverse one). One last word: it's important to open all your resources within the try — because if you don't, you may have some buffer that will not be properly flushed before the close is called, leading to some loss of data.

try (var input = Files.newBufferedReader(in);
     var output = new BufferedWriter(Files.newWriter(out))) {  // order!
    // output closes first, then input (reverse of the declaration)
    output.write(input.readLine());
}   // close() always runs, even with an exception (close exceptions: suppressed)
226

What is a Gatherer?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

You can see a gatherer as the equivalent of a collector, but for intermediate operations. The Gatherer API gives you three elements: the Gatherer interface; a gather method on the Stream interface; and a Gatherers factory class with some ready-to-use gatherers. A gatherer can model intermediate operations that do not exist on the Stream API. Suppose that you want to remove the duplicates from a stream of strings of characters, but ignoring case differences — you can't do it with the Stream API, but you can design a gatherer to do that. One last word: gatherers can do many things — they can push as many elements as you need to the stream, they can manage an internal mutable state, and they can decide to interrupt the stream (or not).

// the collector equivalent, for INTERMEDIATE operations (JDK 22+)
Stream<String> folded = words.gather(Gatherers.fold(...));

// the cited example: distinct ignoring case — the API doesn't have it;
// a gatherer does:
Gatherer<String, ?, String> distinctIgnoreCase = /* your gatherer */;
Stream.of("Java", "JAVA", "java").gather(distinctIgnoreCase);
227

What is type inference?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A way to deduce the type of a variable or an argument.

Less short answer

There are many places where you do not declare the type of the variable you use, and where the compiler needs to make sure that this type is correct. For instance, when you pass a lambda to a method, the compiler checks if this lambda matches the type of the parameter. In JDK 10, a var keyword was added to the language to extend the scope of type inference. One last word: this var keyword can be used only for local variables — not fields, nor method parameters — and these variables need to have an initializer.

var map = new HashMap<String, List<Integer>>();  // JDK 10+: local with init

// does NOT compile:
// var field;            (a field)
// void m(var x) { }     (a parameter)
// var empty;            (no initializer)

Function<String, Integer> f = s -> s.length();    // a lambda: inferred type
228

What is putIfAbsent()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A method from the Map interface.

Less short answer

putIfAbsent takes a key and a value, and binds this value to this key under a condition that may be slightly different from what you expect. If you check the source code of putIfAbsent, you can see that it does not call containsKey — it calls get: if get returns null (the key is not there, or it is bound to null — why would you do that?), then it remaps it with the new value. So it is a good reason to use map implementations that do not accept null values or keys. One last word: putIfAbsent returns the value that was previously bound to this key — which may be null; be careful with that.

Map<String, Integer> m = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
m.putIfAbsent("count", 1);       // returns null (there was none)
m.putIfAbsent("count", 99);      // returns 1 — and does NOT overwrite
m.get("count");                  // 1

Map<String, Integer> hm = new HashMap<>();
hm.put("x", null);
hm.putIfAbsent("x", 5);          // get returned null -> WRITES 5!
229

What is a PrintWriter?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(the presenter skips it — straight to the explanation)

Less short answer

This type is important because it is the type of System.out, the one on which you call println to pass "hello world" to it. It is an extension of the Writer class, and it's the class that carries the println and printf methods — this one takes that nifty, horrible format as a parameter. This format is coming from C and C++, and it allows you to format your strings of characters to properly display them on text terminals (yes, that was some time ago). By the way, this format is documented in the java.util.Formatter class, in case you need it. One last word: there are several formatting classes in the JDK — another one is based on the Format abstract class, extended by MessageFormat or SimpleDateFormat. (Another video.)

PrintWriter out = new PrintWriter(Files.newBufferedWriter(
        Path.of("log.txt"), StandardCharsets.UTF_8));
out.println("start of processing");
out.printf("used: %,d (%.1f%%)%n", 32_000, 12.5);
out.flush();   // PrintWriter swallows exceptions: check checkError()!
230

What is a ThreadLocal variable? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Old stuff from Java 2.

Less short answer

ThreadLocal variables were added to allow passing arguments from one servlet to another without having to rely on method parameters — that was very useful in the early days of J2EE, because a servlet was actually your callback. Now, some of the design decisions made at that time do not fit very easily with modern frameworks — and especially with virtual threads. The reason why the Loom Project — that delivered virtual threads in JDK 21 — is developing ScopedValues: scoped values do the same thing as ThreadLocals, but without the drawbacks. One last word: ThreadLocals can be the source of security vulnerabilities in your application — so do not forget to call remove() on them once you're done with them.

// the classic use: a request context without parameters
private static final ThreadLocal<Context> CTX = new ThreadLocal<>();
CTX.set(ctx);
try { chainOfServices(); }     // any servlet/service reads CTX.get()
finally { CTX.remove(); }      // in a thread POOL: MANDATORY
231

What is a final field?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A field with the final modifier on its declaration.

Less short answer

A final field can be static or not. If it is a static field, then it must have an initializer — and once it has been initialized, it cannot be modified anymore; Math.PI is a public static final field defined that way in the Math class. If your field is an instance field, then it needs to be initialized before your object is fully constructed — and once you give a value to it, you cannot change it, not even from within another constructor. One last word: final also means that the value of your fields is visible from other threads — so you never need to declare a final field volatile.

class Converter {
    static final double FACTOR = 1.609;    // static: initializer required
    private final BigDecimal rate;         // instance: set in the constructor

    Converter(BigDecimal rate) {           // the only chance to write it
        this.rate = rate;
    }
    // this.rate = otherValue;  // ERROR anywhere else
}   // final: visible to other threads without volatile
232

How can you sort a collection?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Well... you can't.

Less short answer

It does not make sense to sort a Collection, because a collection doesn't have any order — the elements you will see if you iterate over them could change from one iteration to the other. You may be thinking that sorting only makes sense for lists, since lists are ordered — and indeed, there is a sort method. But you can also sort some sets — sorted sets, or navigable sets — and this is captured by the sequenced collections added in JDK 21: SequencedSet extends SequencedCollection, and there is also a SequencedMap for maps. One last word: ordering and sorting are two different notions — you need ordering to sort, but you can have order without sorting.

List<Integer> l = new ArrayList<>(List.of(3, 1, 2));
l.sort(Comparator.naturalOrder());          // [1, 2, 3]

Set<Integer> sortedSet = new TreeSet<>(Set.of(3, 1, 2));
// TreeSet: always sorted (it's not a "sort", it's order maintenance)

// LinkedHashSet: ORDERED (insertion) but not SORTED
233

How can you create an Iterable from a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(straight to the pattern)

Less short answer

The idea is to avoid putting all the elements produced by the stream in a collection or an array — to save on memory. So what you can do is get the iterator of the stream and use a factory method of the Spliterators class... (the idea in short: an Iterable is basically a supplier of iterators; you pass the construction of this iterator to build your Iterable). This iterable produces the elements as they are pulled by the underlying stream, without buffering them. One last word: be careful — a stream can produce an unbounded number of elements, so you probably need to protect your application against that.

Stream<String> stream = lines();           // can be HUGE

Iterable<String> asIterable = stream::iterator;  // a supplier of iterators

for (String s : asIterable) {             // pulled on demand, no buffering
    if (s.startsWith("END")) break;       // protect yourself from infinity!
}
234

What is a VarHandle?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object to access fields.

Less short answer

You may be thinking that VarHandle is the equivalent of MethodHandles, but to access fields — it's actually not the case: you can access fields with MethodHandles too. The VarHandle was added in JDK 9 — whereas the MethodHandle was added in JDK 7; so during these six years, MethodHandles were used to access fields. What the VarHandle gives you is access to fields, but with the concurrent semantics — like volatile or atomic. Accessing a field using this mode mimics the fact that the field was defined as a volatile field, for instance. One last word: be careful — accessing a field value using a VarHandle ignores the possible volatile declaration that this field may have.

class Counter {
    private int value;                       // a plain field
    private static final VarHandle VALUE;
    static {
        try { VALUE = MethodHandles.lookup()
                    .findVarHandle(Counter.class, "value", int.class); }
        catch (Exception e) { throw new RuntimeException(e); }
    }
    void increment() {
        VALUE.getAndAdd(this, 1);   // atomic, without volatile in the declaration
    }
}

What is a MessageFormat?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class.

Less short answer

MessageFormat works with a pattern — and this pattern can be used to format messages from a given object, or to parse strings of characters to extract the information they contain. There is one major trap in the MessageFormat class: you may be tempted to reuse your MessageFormats and cache them in public static variables... well, don't do that — MessageFormat is a mutable object, and it is not thread-safe: sharing such an instance between threads will produce race conditions. One last word: date formatting has been superseded by the Date and Time API in JDK 8 — so you may want to check the DateTimeFormatter class. (Another video.)

String msg = MessageFormat.format(
        "Hi {0}, you have {1,choice,0#no messages|1#one message|1<{1,number,integer} messages}",
        "Ana", 3);

// do NOT cache: mutable and not thread-safe
// public static final MessageFormat FMT = ...  <- race condition!
236

What is a collector built on?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Three elements plus one.

Less short answer

A collector collects the elements of the stream in a mutable container. For that, it needs two elements: one used to build the mutable container — in the form of a Supplier — and another one to add an element to this container — a BiConsumer: that's the accumulator. Then, as you can use the collector in parallel — meaning that several instances of the container may be built in different threads — at some point you will need to combine them: that's the combiner, a BinaryOperator. And the last one is there to make your container, for instance, non-modifiable — that's the finisher, a function. One last word: a collector also has characteristics. (Another video.)

Collector<Integer, ?, List<Integer>> toList = Collector.of(
        ArrayList::new,               // 1. supplier: creates the container
        List::add,                    // 2. accumulator: adds an element
        (l1, l2) -> { l1.addAll(l2);  // 3. combiner: merges (parallel)
                      return l1; },
        Collections::unmodifiableList // 4. finisher: transforms the result
);
237

What does escape analysis mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It means performance.

Less short answer

There are cases in your code where you declare variables that are objects, and you already know that they will be destroyed very soon. Objects are a little costly to create — and then they need to be garbage-collected. And sometimes they are just not referenced by your code: an iterator in the for-each pattern, for example; or they do not escape the method you're in. In these cases, the JVM may decide not to create these objects: it uses their content where it's needed, but does not create them. One last word: on a stack, this is called scalarization; and on the heap, flattening — something Valhalla already does very well.

int totalLength(List<String> l) {
    // iterator wrappers, counters... the JIT removes the allocations
    int total = 0;
    for (String s : l) {          // the iterators do not escape
        var box = s.length();     // the boxing does not escape
        total += box;             // -> scalarized code, no objects
    }
    return total;
}
238

What is getOrDefault()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A method from the Map interface.

Less short answer

getOrDefault takes two parameters: a key, and a default value. You may be thinking that, if the key is not in the map, then it returns the default value — well, the detail is that getOrDefault does not check if the key is in the map with containsKey: it checks if get(key) returns null. In a map that does not accept null values — like a ConcurrentHashMap — then yes: an absent key means getOrDefault returns the default value you provide. But if your map accepts null values (that's the case for HashMap), then it also returns the default value in the case of a null value bound to that key. One last word: getOrDefault is aligned with what putIfAbsent does — we already talked about that.

Map<String, Integer> stock = new HashMap<>();
stock.getOrDefault("pen", 0);        // 0 (absent)

stock.put("pencil", null);          // HashMap accepts null!
stock.getOrDefault("pencil", 0);    // 0 (!) — get returned null

Map<String, Integer> conc = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
conc.getOrDefault("pencil", 0);     // 0 (there is never a real null)
239

What is this?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Come on. (the presenter's ironic short answer)

Less short answer

this is a keyword of the language — a reference on the object you are in. Because it is a reference on your instance, you cannot use it in static members of your class, for obvious reasons. One fun fact: you can write your methods in such a way, with this as a first parameter — this is actually the trick the compiler is using to give you a this reference in your method... and that's a very good way of writing unreadable code, and of tricking people. One last word: this(...) is also a way to call another constructor from the constructor — the old restrictions on the calling of constructors within constructors have been lifted (for Valhalla to have immutable objects). (Another video.)

class Point {
    private final int x, y;
    Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }  // this.x = the field
    Point() { this(0, 0); }   // this(...) calls another constructor

    // the unreadable-code trick (this "as a parameter" — the compiler's)
    // static int x(Point this, int delta) { return this.x + delta; }
}
240

How expensive is a thread?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A thread is quite expensive.

Less short answer

It actually depends on your operating system, because an instance of the Thread class is a wrapper on a thread provided by your OS. A kernel thread holds several megabytes of memory, and may take several milliseconds to create. So yes — it is an expensive resource; the reason why applications create them when they start, try to keep them alive, and reuse them (pools). This led to the callback-based asynchronous programming model, that can work with very few threads. One last word: all this is valid for kernel threads; virtual threads, on the other hand, are like regular objects — you can create them on demand, and have as many as you need in your application. (Another video.)

// kernel thread: ~1 MB of stack, creation in ms -> a POOL
ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(20);

// virtual thread: KBs, creation in µs -> one per task, by the thousands
Thread.ofVirtual().name("worker-", 0).start(task);
241

What is pattern matching?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An old feature from the 70s that is making its way to the Java language.

Less short answer

Pattern matching was defined by the functional programming people about 50 years ago. So far there are two patterns in Java: the type pattern and the record pattern. When you write o instanceof String s, what you're really asking is: "could this variable o be declared as String s?" — and if it is the case, then s, called the pattern variable, is created, and you can use it where it makes sense. With the record pattern, you can deconstruct records and create pattern variables for their components. One last word: there are more patterns to come — primitive type patterns and named patterns. (Another video.)

// type pattern: the question + the variable creation in one step
if (o instanceof String s && !s.isEmpty()) {
    System.out.println(s.length());   // no cast!
}

// record pattern: deconstruction
if (o instanceof Point(int x, int y)) {
    System.out.println(x + "," + y);  // the components, directly
}
242

Why can't you map filter reduce a collection?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Because you don't want to map filter reduce a collection.

Less short answer

There are cases where the reduce operation does not need to process all the elements you have to produce a result. If you're looking for the first element, having to map and then filter all the rest of your elements would be a terrible overhead. So mapping everything, then filtering everything before reducing would be a terrible way to implement this algorithm. What you want to do is to take every element through your entire processing pipeline, one step at a time — and this is what Streams are doing. One last word: Eclipse Collections implemented the concept of lazy collections — so if this is what you need, please use it.

// wrong (eager on a collection): maps EVERYTHING, filters EVERYTHING, then seeks
huge.map(...).filter(...).findFirst();

// Streams: each element flows through the whole pipeline, one at a time
huge.stream().map(...).filter(...).findFirst();  // short-circuit!
243

Can you explain the parameter types of a collector?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are three of them.

Less short answer

But you only need to understand the first one and the last one. The first one is the type of elements this collector can consume; the third type is the type of the element that this collector can produce. You can leave the second type anonymous — it represents the internal container your collector implementation may use. One last word: actually, collectors do not depend on the Stream API — you can use their supplier, accumulator and finisher independently. (Another video.)

Collector<String, ?, Long> counting = Collectors.counting();

Collector<String, StringBuilder, String> joining =
        Collector.of(StringBuilder::new,      // A: the container (the middle)
                     StringBuilder::append,   // T: consumes Strings
                     (a, b) -> a.append(b),
                     StringBuilder::toString);// R: produces a String
// you write Collector<String, ?, String> — the middle stays anonymous
244

What does volatile mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It has to do with the visibility of fields in a concurrent environment.

Less short answer

volatile is a keyword you can put on a field. It means that the read and write operations on this field are volatile reads and volatile writes — and this has to do with the existence of happens-before links between your read operation and your write operation. The specification says that a happens-before link exists between a synchronized/volatile write and the corresponding synchronized/volatile read. One last word: a volatile reference on a mutable container does not protect the content of this container against race conditions — so be careful with that.

private volatile boolean running = true;   // visible write/read

// writer: running = false  ->  reader: while (running) ... SEES the change

private volatile List<Integer> list;      // the reference is visible... the content is NOT!
list.add(1);   // race condition: volatile does not protect the inside
245

How can you compute the median value of an array?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

By using the right algorithm.

Less short answer

The lazy way of doing it would be to sort the array, then read the middle index — but it's inefficient. The first step of the quicksort algorithm is to choose a pivot and move all the smaller elements to the left — meaning that if, after doing this, you have split your array in two equal parts, it's done: you don't need to go any further. The problem is that you may not get the middle index in one guess — so you need to repeat, but only on the largest part that you have. On average, you will not have to sort everything, so you will go faster. One last word: you can use better strategies if what you need is just a value that is close enough — that can be useful, especially if you have a lot of data.

// QuickSelect: the quicksort partition repeated only on the side that matters
int quickSelect(int[] a, int k) {
    int lo = 0, hi = a.length - 1;
    while (true) {
        int p = partition(a, lo, hi);      // the pivot in its right place
        if (p == k) return a[p];
        else if (p < k) lo = p + 1;        // only the right side!
        else hi = p - 1;                   // only the left side!
    }
}
int median = quickSelect(vals, vals.length / 2);  // ~O(n) on average
246

How is the toArray() method working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are several toArray methods — on Collection, on streams, and on specialized streams.

Less short answer

On specialized streams (IntStream...), toArray builds an array with your primitives — plain and simple. On a Stream<T>, toArray builds an array of objects, but you can also pass a supplier of the given type to produce an array of values of that type. On Collection, toArray does the same — but it can also take an array of the given type. One last word: for collections, passing an empty array may actually be a better idea than passing an array of the right size — it has to do with the fact that an array must be filled with zeros; but as usual, when it comes to performance: measure, don't guess.

int[] p = IntStream.of(1, 2, 3).toArray();       // primitives: direct

String[] a1 = stream.toArray(String[]::new);     // a typed Stream<T>
Object[] a2 = stream.toArray();                  // Object[]

String[] a3 = list.toArray(new String[0]);       // collection: an empty array
String[] a4 = list.toArray(new String[list.size()]); // the exact size
247

What is an unnamed variable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A variable that has no name.

Less short answer

What could be the use of such a variable? Well, with record patterns, you create pattern variables when you deconstruct a record — and creating such a variable calls the accessor of your record, which may be costly. So, if you don't need them, you can declare them as unnamed (with _), and they will not be created. You can also use these unnamed variables in lambda expressions, or when you catch exceptions. One last word: for record deconstruction, using unnamed variables may also hide a change in your model — as does the use of the var keyword, by the way. So be aware of that before using this feature.

// an unnamed type pattern: only the type matters, nothing is created
if (obj instanceof Circle _) { processAsCircle(); }

// a record pattern with an ignored component (the accessor is not even called):
if (obj instanceof Circle(double radius, Point _)) {
    System.out.println("radius=" + radius);   // 'center' never becomes a variable
}

for (var _ : list) counter++;                                     // ignores the element
try { parse(v); } catch (NumberFormatException _) { return 0; }   // unnamed
248

What does the reversed() method do?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It reverses a list.

Less short answer

It is actually one of the methods brought to you by the Sequenced Collections API (Java 21): this method returns you a view on your sequenced collection. Since it is a view, if the underlying sequenced collection is modified, you will see this modification — and note that for an ArrayList, this reversed view is also modifiable: modifying it modifies the underlying list. If what you need is a copy and not a view, you can pass it to List.copyOf(), that will happily copy it for you. One last word: be careful though, because List.copyOf does not accept null values. (Why would you put null values in your collections, anyway?)

List<Integer> l = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
List<Integer> view = l.reversed();   // a view: [3, 2, 1]
view.set(0, 99);                     // modifies l: [1, 2, 99]
List.copyOf(l.reversed());           // an independent copy (no nulls!)
249

Why does Java not allow variable capturing?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Because it would have changed the concurrent programming model.

Less short answer

A lambda can only capture a final variable — that is, a value. It cannot capture a modifiable local variable. If it had been allowed — since this lambda can escape the method it is declared in — different threads would have been exposed to these local variables, and then to race conditions; something that does not exist in Java (for locals, in the Java model). So the decision was: you can only capture values, never modifiable variables. One last word: there are JVM-based languages that allow the capturing of local variables in lambdas — but when it comes to race conditions, you're on your own.

int counter = 0;                    // effectively final: OK
Runnable r = () -> use(counter);    // captures the VALUE (a copy)

// counter++; above would make the capture illegal — by design:
// a Runnable in another thread mutating a local = a free race condition

// if you need shared mutable state:
AtomicInteger n = new AtomicInteger();
Runnable r2 = n::incrementAndGet;   // explicit and safe
250

What is a blocking call?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something you want to avoid.

Less short answer

How much time does it take to process a network request-response cycle? Creating a request and analyzing a response takes in the order of microseconds; waiting for a network response takes in the order of milliseconds — a thousand times slower. "Blocking" means that during this time, the thread that is running your request is waiting, doing nothing: it could handle about a thousand request constructions or response analyses, but it does not. One last word: the existing solution to overcome this problem is to use reactive programming, avoiding the writing of blocking code; the future is to write blocking code — but in virtual threads. (Another video.)

// blocking: an OS thread stopped for milliseconds...
Response r = client.send(request);

// with virtual threads, blocking is cheap — the carrier is released:
Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> client.send(request));
251

What is a Predicate?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A functional interface.

Less short answer

This interface defines a test method, that takes an object and returns a boolean. It is used in several places in the Stream API and the Collections framework — for instance, in the filter method, that can filter a stream, or in the Collection.removeIf method, that will remove elements from a collection. This interface defines a default method to combine a predicate with and / or; you can also negate a predicate with the negate method, or with the not factory method, that takes another predicate. One last word: it is one of the four fundamental functional interfaces that you need to know: Consumer, Supplier, Function and Predicate.

Predicate<String> empty = String::isEmpty;
Predicate<String> short = s -> s.length() < 5;

empty.or(short).test("abc");            // true
empty.and(short).negate().test("xy");   // false
Predicate.not(empty).test("java");      // true (a static factory)
252

What is a WeakHashMap?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A map.

Less short answer

The keys of a WeakHashMap are weak references, that can be garbage-collected if they are not used by anything else in your application — meaning that the size of this map may decrease over time, without your application ever calling a remove. This is very useful to create caches, for instance: put key-value pairs in a WeakHashMap — and if at some point your application is running low on memory, the garbage collector can just remove the ones that are not used. One last word (an important one): the values in a WeakHashMap are stored using strong references — so if your value has a strong reference on its key, your WeakHashMap will just behave as a regular HashMap. Don't do that.

Map<Config, CachedData> cache = new WeakHashMap<>();
cache.put(config, data);     // config is weak: without external refs, the entry goes

// TRAP: a value referencing the key = an immortal entry
// class CachedData { final Config key; }  <- DON'T!
253

How can you use a collector outside of the Stream API?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that.

Less short answer

A collector is built on four elements: a supplier, an accumulator, a combiner and a finisher. You can apply these elements on a list, for instance: first, you need to create the mutable container with the supplier; second, you can accumulate the elements of this list in it, using the accumulator; and once this is done, you need to call the finisher on the result, if you have one — and you're done. One last word: collectors do not depend on the Stream API — you can use them independently, and it's a very powerful API that can model complex processing. The same is true for gatherers. (Another video.)

Collector<String, ?, Long> counting = Collectors.counting();

// driving the collector manually (without any stream):
var box = counting.supplier().get();              // creates the container
counting.accumulator().accept(box, "a");          // accumulates...
counting.accumulator().accept(box, "b");
Long total = counting.finisher().apply(box);      // finishes: 2
254

What is a StampedLock?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A lock with an optimistic read mode, among other things.

Less short answer

A StampedLock has three modes: exclusive write and non-exclusive read — the two modes of read-write locks — plus a third one: the optimistic read. The stamp you get when you call one of the lock methods must be provided to unlock this StampedLock. Optimistic reads are interesting, but also fragile: you need to acquire the stamp, check that it is non-zero, read what you need, and then validate what you just read, to make sure no write was done from another thread. One last word: locks obtained from a StampedLock can also be converted — from a read lock to a write lock, for instance. (Another video.)

StampedLock lock = new StampedLock();

double read() {
    long stamp = lock.tryOptimisticRead();   // without blocking!
    double x = data;
    if (!lock.validate(stamp)) {             // a write happened meanwhile?
        stamp = lock.readLock();             // fallback: really locks
        try { x = data; } finally { lock.unlockRead(stamp); }
    }
    return x;
}
255

How can you create multi-dimensional arrays?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that.

Less short answer

There is a syntax to create arrays with multiple dimensions — but under the hood, what you get is an array of references to other arrays, that can be stored anywhere in the memory of your application. It is actually working in the following way: first, you create an array of references; then, for each of them, you create another array of the right size — and by the way, this size can vary from one array to the other (jagged arrays). One last word: there is no way you can automatically map a large contiguous region of memory to a multi-dimensional array, as you can do in C — if this is what you need, you will have to write some code (a linearized index).

int[][] grid = new int[3][4];        // an array of 3 references...
grid[0] = new int[10];               // ...each row can have its own size

int[][] jagged = { {1}, {1, 2}, {1, 2, 3} };  // jagged: legal!

// truly contiguous? Do it yourself:
var flat = new int[3 * 4];
flat[row * 4 + column] = 1;
256

How can you stream the content of a directory? *(with DirectoryStream)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

The method is Files.newDirectoryStream(dir), declared on the Files factory class. It returns a DirectoryStream object — and this object is not a stream from the Stream API: it is an Iterable, from which you can get an iterator. This iterator gives you the guarantee that a call to next() that follows a call to hasNext() that returned true will not fail — which, when you think about the concurrent nature of file systems, is not obvious. Note that the directory you stream is not frozen while you iterate on its content; and what you get is the content of this directory — sub-directories are not explored. One last word: you can also pass a filter (a glob) to the construction of this object, to list only the files of a certain type, for instance.

try (DirectoryStream<Path> ds = Files.newDirectoryStream(
        Path.of("."), "*.{java,txt}")) {          // a glob filter
    for (Path p : ds) {                           // it's an Iterable
        System.out.println(p);                    // one level only
    }
}
257

What is a stack trace?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An array.

Less short answer

All the exception classes extend Throwable — and when you create an instance of such an object, an array is created, that contains all the methods that called the line of code on which this exception was created. Note that this array is filled when the exception is created — the reason why, if you create an exception to report an error and throw it, you'd better call throw new ..., so that the stack trace you get takes you to the right place in your application. One last word: sometimes the stack trace does not contain all the stack frames — in extreme cases, you may even have no stack frame at all; that may happen if the VM has no stack trace information.

try { process(); }
catch (IOException e) {
    // the trace points HERE (where the exception was created):
    throw new IntegrationException("integration failure", e);
}
e.getStackTrace();   // StackTraceElement[] — frames, newest first
258

How can you read a ZIP file with the FileSystem API?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that.

Less short answer

Java NIO gives you the FileSystem abstract class to model file systems — and one of them can access data compressed in a ZIP/JAR, used in Java applications. You can create such a file system on a zip file, and then use it to create paths — you can do the same to read them: it's exactly as if you were writing or reading from a regular disk file system. One last word: as you know, Path objects are actually bound to a specific file system — so when using this pattern, you need to use path objects (or Files) created from the zip file system, not paths from the default file system.

try (FileSystem zip = FileSystems.newFileSystem(Path.of("data.zip"))) {
    Path inside = zip.getPath("reports/q3.csv");
    Files.readString(inside);                       // reads as if it were a disk!
    try (Stream<Path> s = Files.walk(zip.getPath("/"))) {
        s.forEach(p -> System.out.println(p));      // browses the whole zip
    }
}
259

Is the String class an immutable class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

That's actually a trick question — and the correct answer is no.

Less short answer

You probably think that the String class is non-modifiable, which is partially true. What is true: once you fix the value of a string of characters, you cannot change it; all the methods of the String class — like toUpperCase or substring — actually return another String object, wrapping a different array. But there is a mutable field in the String class: the one that holds the hash code of that String object — computed on the first call, and cached in this field. One last word: this mutable field is what makes it impossible for String to become a value object in Valhalla, unless the class is changed. (Another video.)

public final class String {
    private final byte[] value;  // immutable (final, defensively copied)
    private int hash;            // MUTABLE: computed and cached later!
    // -> that's why String is not 100% immutable, nor value-based
}
260

What is a virtual thread?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class. That is not public. (a joke — it is much more than that)

Less short answer

Virtual threads are an amazing addition of JDK 21. A virtual thread is a wrapper on a task that you want to submit to a regular thread — this task can be modeled by a Runnable or a Callable. Formally, virtual threads can be mounted and unmounted on regular threads, so that they can execute the task that they wrap; and when your task blocks — like when it is waiting for data from the network — the NIO API unmounts the virtual thread, so that the regular thread is not blocked. Being able to unmount a task from a thread was not possible in Java before virtual threads — and that's a game changer. One last word: virtual threads are made to wrap blocking tasks. Forget about using them for anything else.

// 1,000,000 blocking tasks? No problem:
try (var executor = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
    for (var url : urls) {
        executor.submit(() -> download(url));  // blocks? unmounts. Cheap.
    }
}
261

How can you represent time?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a class for that. (the problem is that there are two of them)

Less short answer

The legacy java.sql.Time (that extends java.util.Date) and LocalTime — which is the one you should favor. A LocalTime models an hour, a minute and a second, with nanosecond precision, and no time zone information — and it's an immutable class. You have factory methods to create a LocalTime, accessors to read the time it carries, methods to create another LocalTime from the one you have, and even with* methods. Neat. One last word: LocalTime is now a value-based class — the Valhalla notion; among other restrictions, you should not synchronize on it. (Another video.)

LocalTime now = LocalTime.of(14, 30, 15);
now.getHour();        // 14
now.plusMinutes(90);  // 16:00:15 (a new object)
now.withHour(9);      // 09:30:15
now.isBefore(LocalTime.NOON);
// legacy: java.sql.Time — avoid
262

What is mapMulti()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A method from the Stream interface.

Less short answer

mapMulti allows you to flatMap without having to create intermediate Stream objects — which can be another overhead in your data processing pipeline. You need to call mapMulti with a lambda that takes two arguments: the element of the stream you need to process, and the "downstream" object. When you want to pass an object to the downstream, you need to call downstream.accept() — you can call accept any number of times, or not call it at all. One last word: you need to specify the type of the object you are pushing to the downstream when you call mapMulti — if you don't, this method will return a Stream<Object>. Usually, it's not what you want.

record Token(String text) { }

List<Token> tokens = Stream.of("a b", "c")
        .<Token>mapMulti((phrase, downstream) -> {   // the explicit type!
            for (String p : phrase.split(" "))
                downstream.accept(new Token(p));     // 0..N per element
        })
        .toList();
263

What is a ConcurrentMap?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

The ConcurrentMap interface has a double role: defining thread safety and the atomicity of some operations on maps. It has two implementations in the JDK: ConcurrentHashMap and ConcurrentSkipListMap. Each method defined in this interface can be specified to be atomic — that's the case for putIfAbsent, for instance, which cannot be interrupted between the checking of the absence of the key and the binding of this key to the value you provide. Atomicity may also apply to some methods of the collections you can get from this map — values(), keySet() or entrySet(). One last word: be careful, because some ConcurrentMaps do not accept null keys. (Why would you put null keys in, anyway?)

ConcurrentMap<String, Integer> map = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();

// putIfAbsent is ATOMIC: check + put are indivisible
map.putIfAbsent("count", 0);
map.compute("count", (k, v) -> v + 1);   // also atomic
// map.put(null, 1);  // NullPointerException (no nulls!)
265

How can you access the off-heap memory?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a whole API for that. (two, in fact)

Less short answer

The first API is really old — from Java 4, in 2002: the ByteBuffer class. The Panama Project brought new patterns to access the off-heap memory, based on the use of an Arena object: from an arena, you can get memory segments, and then read and write data from them — directly with an offset, or with an index. You can also map them to files, just like you could with byte buffers. One last word: byte buffers are indexed with integers, and memory segments with longs; and you can also define layouts on them, to access their content in a structured way. (Another video — "see you in the next one".)

try (Arena arena = Arena.ofConfined()) {
    MemorySegment seg = arena.allocate(1_000);
    seg.set(ValueLayout.JAVA_DOUBLE, 0, Math.PI);  // by offset
    seg.getAtIndex(ValueLayout.JAVA_LONG, 3);       // by index (the 3rd long)
}
// ByteBuffer (legacy, Java 4): int indexes, no arena
ByteBuffer b = ByteBuffer.allocateDirect(1_000);
266

What is a deserialization filter?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a filter that you can declare to prevent the deserialization of specific classes.

Less short answer

Deserializing an unknown stream of data is an inherently dangerous activity. A deserialization filter can prevent instances of certain classes, and graphs of objects that are too deep or too complex, from being deserialized. You need to implement the ObjectInputFilter.checkInput() method, with the information you need on the data stream; you can then return a status that can be ALLOWED, REJECTED, or even UNDECIDED. One last word: serialization is the source of many security issues — using records can prevent some of them, so use them wherever you can.

ObjectInputFilter filter = info ->
        info.serialClass() == Order.class ? ObjectInputFilter.Status.ALLOWED
        : info.serialClass() != null      ? ObjectInputFilter.Status.REJECTED
        : info.depth() > 5                ? ObjectInputFilter.Status.REJECTED
                                          : ObjectInputFilter.Status.UNDECIDED;

var in = new ObjectInputStream(inputStream);
in.setObjectInputFilter(filter);   // JEP 290: protection against gadgets
267

How can you get the class of a primitive type?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(straight to the method)

Less short answer

Primitive types are associated with classes, that you need when you want to get a method from a class using the Reflection API. You can use the int.class syntax, that gives you a Class instance — but JDK 22 added a new method on the Class class: Class.forPrimitiveName("int"), that takes the primitive type as a string of characters, and that returns you the same object. One last word: so far, primitive types are different from object types; this may change in the future, once the value types from Valhalla make it to the JDK — you will even be able to define your own primitive types, like a 16-bits float. (Another video.)

Class<Integer> c1 = int.class;                  // the literal
Class<?> c2 = Integer.TYPE;                     // the wrapper constant
Class<?> c3 = Class.forPrimitiveName("int");    // JDK 22+
c1 == c2 && c2 == c3;                           // true: the same object

// a typical use: reflection of methods with primitive parameters
String.class.getMethod("charAt", int.class);
268

Why do you need Optional?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

To tell that a method cannot return anything.

Less short answer

There are cases where you need that — and returning void or null does not tell that you cannot return anything: it's a way of returning nothing, which is different. Where does it come from? Because a stream can be computed in parallel, the reduction operation has to be associative — otherwise, the result of your operation would depend on the order in which it has been computed. But then, a stream can be empty: what could be the result of the reduction of an empty stream? It would have to be the identity element of your reduction operation... but what can you do for min or max, or any reduction that does not have an identity element? The choice was to return an Optional — that can be empty. One last word: using Optionals in any other context is usually a bad idea. Don't do that.

Optional<Integer> max = Stream.<Integer>empty().max(Integer::compare);
max.isEmpty();   // true — a reduction without identity returns an Optional

// Optional was born in the Stream API: use it there (and for possibly-absent returns)
// Optional as a field/parameter: a terrible idea
269

What is a Function?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A functional interface.

Less short answer

A Function maps an object of a given type to an object of another type — it is used by the map method of the Stream interface. The Function interface also defines methods to chain and compose functions (andThen, compose), and a factory method that returns the identity function (Function.identity()). There are also specialized functions to avoid the boxing of primitive types when you want to process numbers (IntFunction, ToIntFunction...), and the UnaryOperator — a function that does not change the type. One last word: Function is part of the four fundamental functional interfaces of the JDK — Consumer, Supplier, Predicate and Function; and yes, it's okay to add Runnable to the lot, even if Runnable was there long before Java 8.

Function<String, Integer> length = String::length;
Function<Integer, Integer> twice = n -> n * 2;

length.andThen(twice).apply("java");  // 8
twice.compose(length).apply("java");  // 8 (the reverse order)
Function.identity();                  // x -> x
UnaryOperator<Integer> triple = n -> 3 * n; // the same type in and out
270

What is a Spliterator?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

A spliterator is the object on which a stream is built. It has a number of methods to traverse the elements of the source, to partition them, and to get information on the source. Writing your own spliterator is not an easy task — you need to be careful about the behavior of each method. Fortunately, there are many examples in the JDK itself to help you: you can check, for instance, the ArrayListSpliterator, or the HashMap.KeySpliterator — which is the spliterator of the HashSet. One last word: a spliterator can produce an unbounded number of elements, and it can produce them lazily — you can check the Random.ints() spliterator, or the MatcherIterator, used in the Pattern.splitAsStream() method.

// iterate -> an unbounded, lazy spliterator
Spliterator<Integer> sp = Stream.iterate(1, n -> n * 2)
        .spliterator();   // good for building your own pipeline
Stream<Integer> s = StreamSupport.stream(
        List.of(1, 2, 3).spliterator(), /* parallel */ false);
271

What is a value-based class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class that wraps a value.

Less short answer

There are several examples of value-based classes in the JDK: Integer, Double, and all the classes that wrap primitive types, are all value-based classes. All the classes of the Date and Time API are also value-based classes, as well as others — like Optional. A value-based class can only carry some non-modifiable information — thus its name. And since its instances should behave as values, you should not use anything related to their identity — that is, their address in memory: things like ==, System.identityHashCode, or synchronization, should not be used — and will fail at some point. One last word: String is not a value-based class — first, it is not fully unmodifiable (it carries this hash field that is not final); and second, it may wrap a very large array of bytes, that does not fit well with this notion of value.

Optional<Integer> o = Optional.of(1);  // value-based
LocalDate d = LocalDate.now();         // value-based

if (o == another) { }          // forbidden: identity
synchronized (d) { }           // forbidden: synchronizing on a value
System.identityHashCode(o);    // avoid
272

What is a non-null stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The stream with the non-null characteristic.

Less short answer

NONNULL is a characteristic that a stream may have, if you have the guarantee that this stream is built on a source that cannot produce any null element. You can check this characteristic through the spliterator your stream is built on, by calling its hasCharacteristics() method, for instance. You can rely on this characteristic when you need to collect your elements in a container that does not accept null values — which is the case for most concurrent collections, or for the ConcurrentHashMap. One last word: avoiding null values in your data processing pipelines is something you should aim for — it will make your life so much easier.

Spliterator<String> sp = List.of("a", "b").spliterator();
sp.hasCharacteristics(Spliterator.NONNULL);   // true for List.of

// useful: collecting into a container that does not accept nulls
ConcurrentHashMap.newKeySet().addAll(List.of("a")); // a null would blow up
273

What is a fail-safe iterator?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something that does not exist in Java. (the presenter's trick question)

Less short answer

"Fail-safe" would describe a specific behavior when you iterate on a collection that is modified while you iterate over it. In Java, modifying the collection outside of the iterator itself results in a ConcurrentModificationException being thrown — this is called the fail-fast behavior. Concurrent collections usually have a different behavior, called weakly consistent. And the CopyOnWriteArrayList has yet another behavior, called snapshot: each modification creates a copy, so you can keep iterating on your own version of this copy, without any problem. One last word: be careful — the ConcurrentModificationException you get has, in fact, nothing to do with concurrency: everything usually happens in the same thread.

List<String> l = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b"));
for (String s : l) {
    if (s.equals("a")) l.remove(s);   // CME — same thread, no concurrency!
}

List<String> cow = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b"));
for (String s : cow) {
    if (s.equals("a")) cow.remove(s); // OK: iterating over the snapshot
}
274

How can you create a sealed type without permits?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that.

Less short answer

A sealed type needs to know its permitted extensions at compile time, and all these extensions need to live in the same compilation unit — that is, in the same package, or in the same module if you're using the Java module system. Instead of declaring your extending types in separate class files, you can declare them as member types — and in that way, the permits clause becomes optional: the compiler assumes that all the member types that extend your sealed type are permitted. A very convenient way of organizing your codebase, especially if you need to conduct a refactoring at some point. One last word: sealed interfaces implemented by records are a very powerful feature, used in data oriented programming. (Another video.)

public sealed interface Shape {          // no permits!
    record Circle(double radius) implements Shape {}
    record Square(double side) implements Shape {}
    // members in the SAME file = permitted automatically
}
275

What is heap pollution?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A problem you can easily avoid.

Less short answer

Heap pollution has to do with generics and raw types. If you declare a list with a raw type — that is, you omit to give a value to the type parameter — you may get an exception, usually a ClassCastException; so don't do that. And even if you declare a List<String>, you can still add Integers to it by using raw types — this is, of course, silly, and the compiler gives you hints about that by issuing warnings. But, because of backward compatibility, raw types are legal, so you can still use them. One last word: sometimes it may look easier to use raw types because generics are complex — that's always a mistake. Don't do that.

List<String> names = new ArrayList();   // raw: an unchecked warning
List unsafe = names;                    // a raw type, again
unsafe.add(42);                         // "works"... for now
String s = names.get(0);                // ClassCastException (!)
276

What is an Arena?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

An arena is the object you need to use to create memory segments in the memory part of the Foreign Function & Memory API. An arena is responsible for allocating these memory segments, and controls how your application is using them: it is AutoCloseable, and when closed, it deallocates all the memory segments it created; it also controls whether your read and write operations are within the boundaries of your segments, and which thread is accessing them. One last word: there are four different arenas in the JDK, all with different behaviors — your preferred choice should be Arena.ofConfined(), or Arena.ofShared() in a multi-threaded environment; and you can even create your own arena, in case you need it.

try (Arena arena = Arena.ofConfined()) {
    MemorySegment seg = arena.allocate(128);   // a controlled allocation
    seg.set(ValueLayout.JAVA_INT, 0, 7);
}  // close: deallocates EVERYTHING, checks bounds and thread on each access
277

What is thread pinning?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something you want to avoid.

Less short answer

Thread pinning is an issue that came with virtual threads. Virtual threads are executed by regular threads, called carrier threads in this context; and when a virtual thread executes a piece of blocking code, it is unmounted from its carrier thread — and when the data it is waiting for is available, this virtual thread is mounted again, possibly on a different carrier thread. Now here is the trick: C or C++ code has access to addresses on the stack, so a virtual thread cannot be unmounted if it is running native code — those addresses would just be corrupted. This is what pinning is: the thing that prevents the unmounting. One last word: in the first version, from JDK 21, virtual threads were also pinned if they were executing a synchronized block — this one has been fixed in JDK 24.

Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> {
    callNativeLibrary();  // pinning: it does not unmount during JNI/FFM
});

// JDK 21: synchronized also pinned; JDK 24 (JEP 491): not anymore
synchronized (lock) { io(); } // OK on JDK 24+
// detect it with: -Djdk.tracePinnedThreads=full
278

What is a skip list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A nifty structure to create lists.

Less short answer

One of the bad things of linked lists is that accessing an element by its index has a big O(n) complexity. A skip list adds an internal structure that reduces this complexity to big O(log n) — which is much faster: the log of 1000 is roughly 10, in base 2. This structure relies on the fact that the elements you store are comparable, and it creates fast lanes to quickly move to the element you need. When you add or remove elements from a skip list, this structure is updated, so that it is always balanced — well, as much as possible. In the JDK, the skip list has been used to implement the ConcurrentSkipListSet — a set, and even a navigable set — and the ConcurrentSkipListMap, a map, and even a navigable map. One last word: both structures are concurrent and built on the use of atomic references — you can go and check the source code; it's interesting.

// a skip list search: starts on the upper "fast lane" and goes down
// expected O(log n), without balancing rotations
ConcurrentSkipListMap<String, Integer> map = new ConcurrentSkipListMap<>();
map.put("ana", 1); map.put("bob", 2);
map.firstKey();  // "ana" — navigable and thread-safe
279

What is scalarization?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An optimization that consists in not creating the object declared in your code.

Less short answer

Take the example of joining strings of characters: running this code in a naive way would lead to the creation of a bunch of intermediate strings that are actually not used by your code. Well, the same could happen with your own objects: you end up creating a bunch of Population objects that you don't need. Since these objects are not escaping the method they are created in, the simple optimization is to avoid creating them, and just use the values they carry — this is what is called scalarization: you replace your object with the numbers you need to conduct your computations. One last word: believe it or not, this is what Valhalla is already doing — just make this Population record a value type, and you will get scalarization for free.

int total(Country a, Country b) {
    // the JIT (escape analysis) can remove the allocation:
    Population sum = new Population(a.pop() + b.pop());
    return sum.value();
    // scalarized -> becomes: return a.pop() + b.pop();
}
280

How is the Collectors.joining() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(the presenter skips the short answer)

Less short answer

First, it creates the internal mutable container — a StringBuilder. Second, it accumulates in it the elements produced by your stream; if you passed a separator, a prefix or a suffix, then they are added to this StringBuilder too. And third, once all the elements of the stream have been consumed, it calls toString() on its internal StringBuilder, and returns this result. If you want to write your own collectors, taking a look at how this one is working is interesting: it exhibits the three elements every collector has — an initializer, an accumulator, and a finisher (it also has a combiner, "but I'm not sure you want to use this joining collector in a parallel stream"). And its set of characteristics is empty (CH_NOID — none). One last word: joining works only on a stream of strings — your compiler will tell you if you try to collect a stream of the wrong type.

String r = Stream.of("java", "kotlin", "scala")
        .collect(Collectors.joining(", ", "[", "]"));
// "[java, kotlin, scala]"

// the internal design: supplier=StringBuilder::new,
// accumulator=sb.append, finisher=StringBuilder::toString, characteristics={}
281

How can you split a string of characters?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are several patterns for that.

Less short answer

Choosing the right pattern depends on how you want to split your string — and on whether you think you're going to use all the elements, or if you think you will be needing just the first ones. In case you need all the elements: the good old String.split() pattern creates an array with what you need in it. And in case you're interested only in the first ones — or you're running in an environment where memory is tight — then you could prefer a pattern that splits your string lazily, like Pattern.splitAsStream(). One last word: depending on what you want to do, you may find analyzing your string with regular expressions and named groups useful; it's a little tricky to set up — regular expressions are not easy — but super powerful. (Another video.)

String csv = "id;name;age";

String[] all = csv.split(";");            // the full array

try (var parts = Pattern.compile(";")
        .splitAsStream(csv)               // lazy: splits only what is read
        .limit(2)) {
    // uses only the first 2, without materializing the rest
}
282

What is a parallel stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A stream that is processed in parallel.

Less short answer

Creating a parallel stream is super simple: you just need to call .parallel() on your stream, and you're done. The internal algorithm will then split your data in two chunks, and process each of them in its own thread; then each chunk may be split again — and again and again — until the algorithm decides that the chunk it has is small enough to be processed entirely. All these mechanics are managed by the Stream API: you do not need to worry about anything — neither race conditions, nor visibility issues, and the like — as long as you're not doing any side effects in your stream pipeline. (You know that side effects in a stream pipeline are a bad idea, don't you?) One last word: there are good use cases for parallel streams, and bad use cases — if your processing is not a good use case, then you may hurt your performance instead of improving it. As always, when it comes to performance: measure, don't guess.

long sum = LongStream.range(0, 100_000_000)
        .parallel()                     // automatic divide and conquer
        .filter(n -> n % 3 == 0)
        .sum();                         // no side effects = safe
283

What set operations are supported by the collection framework?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Actually, all of them: union, intersection and set difference.

Less short answer

These operations are defined in the Collection interface: addAll is the union, removeAll is the set difference, and retainAll is the intersection. These methods take another collection as a parameter, mutate the collection you are invoking the method on, and return a boolean that is true if the collection was mutated. If you plan to use these operations, you need to choose your implementation wisely, as they do not perform the same: removeAll removes all the elements of your other collection, which is not efficient on an ArrayList — it is much better on a HashSet; and the same goes for retainAll, which calls contains instead of remove — again, ArrayList is not very good at that, HashSet is much faster. One last word: for this kind of operations, HashSet is most of the time your best choice — as long as you're okay with its distinct nature.

Set<Integer> a = new HashSet<>(Set.of(1, 2, 3, 4));
Set<Integer> b = new HashSet<>(Set.of(3, 4, 5));

a.addAll(b);      // union:        [1..5]  (mutates a!)
a.removeAll(b);   // set difference: [1, 2]
a.retainAll(b);   // intersection:   [3, 4]
// boolean return: true if anything changed
284

Why is finalization being deprecated?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Because it was buggy from the beginning.

Less short answer

Finalization consists in calling the finalize() method on an object that is about to be garbage — this method is defined on the Object class, and you can override it. There are two main problems with this mechanism. First, the thread that is calling the finalize method was never specified — and it was never a thread from your application: it is a thread from the garbage collector. So if you call list.clear() or map.clear() in there, for instance, on an ArrayList or a HashMap, then you have a nice race condition in clean code — which, if you're unlucky, can lead to a nice NullPointerException. Second, because you are in the object about to be garbage, you can actually revive it — by storing this in a static field, or a static container, for instance — which prevents some optimizations at the garbage collector level. One last word: finalization has been deprecated and will be removed at some point; you can use the Cleaner API instead, as a replacement — which is safer and fixes the two issues we just mentioned. (Another video.)

@Deprecated(since = "9", forRemoval = true)
class Bad {
    @Override protected void finalize() {   // the GC thread calls this!
        list.clear();                       // a potential race condition
        STATIC_REGISTRY.add(this);          // revives the object (!)
    }
}
285

Should you favor streaming or iterating?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It really depends on your use case.

Less short answer

What is important is that you understand the differences between both approaches. You can stream or iterate over your data in a lazy way — as long as you write your own iterator. Depending on the processing you need to conduct, streaming may be more readable: you pass the data, then you pass your operations, and then they are applied to your data by the API; if you iterate yourself, then you need to write all this code explicitly. Streaming also allows you to go parallel easily, if this is what you need. But there is a caveat: creating a stream is not free — it has a cost, memory-wise and performance-wise. One last word: you should favor the readability of your code in all cases — writing readable code makes the long-term maintenance of your application less expensive; favoring performance is a short-term view, and in many cases it is just longer.

// streaming: declarative
var active = clients.stream()
        .filter(Client::active).map(Client::email).toList();

// iterating: explicit, no overhead
var active2 = new ArrayList<String>();
for (Client c : clients)
    if (c.active()) active2.add(c.email());
// choose whichever is more READABLE in the context
286

What is a MemorySegment?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An interface.

Less short answer

MemorySegment models the contiguous zone of memory that lives outside of the heap memory of your application. So this MemorySegment object lives on the heap, as any Java object, and has an off-heap reference to this piece of memory. A memory segment is created by an Arena object, that controls several things — among them, whether you are reading and writing within the boundaries of your segment, and from the right thread; arenas are also responsible for closing your memory segments. A memory segment is indexed by a long, so it can be really large — as of now, probably larger than what your machine can give you. One last word: you can write structured data in a memory segment, that you can then stream — which is amazing; all this using the Memory Layout API. We will talk more about it later.

try (Arena arena = Arena.ofConfined()) {
    MemorySegment seg = arena.allocate(1L << 30);   // 1 GB, long-indexed
    seg.set(ValueLayout.JAVA_LONG, 0, 42L);          // bounds + thread check
}  // closed by the arena — no use-after-free
287

What thread does a CompletableFuture use?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The default behavior is to use a thread from the common ForkJoin pool.

Less short answer

The CompletableFuture API allows you to define asynchronous pipelines of operations that are triggered by the API itself: you define this pipeline — passing your operations, modeled by lambda expressions — and the API processes the data when it is available. It has been designed to be non-blocking, and applies the callback-based reactive approach. The thread that is executing your lambda expression is taken from the common ForkJoin pool — but the API accepts Executor Services for specific operations; in that case, your threads will be used. One last word: this pool of threads is the same as the one used to run your parallel streams — so if you're using — hmm, using both APIs in your application, you will have a competition on the use of these threads, which may slow down both your streams and your CompletableFutures. You may want to avoid this situation.

CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(this::load)      // common pool...
        .thenApply(this::transform)
        .thenAccept(this::save);

CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(this::load, myExecutor)  // YOUR threads
        .thenApplyAsync(this::transform, myExecutor);
288

When is a stack trace created?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

When the exception is created.

Less short answer

There is no exception to this rule — as all exceptions extend Throwable, directly or indirectly, and the stack trace is created when the Throwable is created. This is why, as a rule of thumb, you should always create your exception where you throw it, with the throw new MyApplicationException(...) pattern; in that way, the stack trace takes you directly where the error was noticed in your application. This is one of the drawbacks of the callback-based reactive frameworks: since the element that calls your lambda is the framework itself, the stack trace takes you into this framework — and not into your application — making the bug much harder to find. When you re-throw an existing API or language exception wrapped in one of your application exceptions, don't forget to pass the first exception as the cause of your application exception: in that case, you will have both stack traces, making the debug much easier.

try {
    process();
} catch (IOException e) {
    // the cause preserved = 2 chained stack traces
    throw new ProcessingException("failure in batch 7", e);
}
// throw new ProcessingException(msg);  // WITHOUT the cause: harder debug
289

What is wrong with ThreadLocal? *(version 2)*

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Everything.

Less short answer

In a nutshell: ThreadLocal is an API that was added to the JDK in Java 2, 1998 — that is, six years before the ExecutorService pattern was introduced in Java 5, 2004 — and the interaction between both patterns does not work so great. A ThreadLocal variable is actually stored in a hash map attached to a thread — so it shares the life cycle of this thread: if you do not remove your ThreadLocal variables by hand, then they will stay forever, and can be read by any code running in this thread — any code; that could lead to serious security breaches. Don't do that. A ThreadLocal variable is mutable, so it prevents the JVM from optimizing it, making them quite costly in your application. And last but not least: the binding is local to a thread — so if you move from one thread to the other, well, you need to tell that you want to inherit your ThreadLocal variables. But the fact is: you need thread locals — and if you think you don't, your favorite application framework does, and uses them heavily. One last word: starting with JDK 25, you can use ScopedValues instead of ThreadLocal variables — that fixes all these issues. We will talk more about that in the future.

// the thread's hash map holds the value; without remove, it outlives the task:
static final ThreadLocal<Context> CTX = new ThreadLocal<>();
CTX.set(requestContext());
try { serve(); } finally { CTX.remove(); }  // ALWAYS (seriously!)
290

What is a stateful operation?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A special type of intermediate operation on the Stream API.

Less short answer

Stateful means that there is a state that needs to be carried over from the processing of one element to the other. Being stateful means that the result of this operation depends on the element being processed, and on some mutable state — the state that lives inside or outside of your stream. For instance, distinct is a stateful operation, as it stores all the elements it sees in an internal hash set — so passing an element depends on this element and on the content of this hash set; and the same goes for limit, that needs to count the number of elements it has passed so far. One last word: this mutable state needs to be shared among the different threads processing your stream, in the case of a parallel stream — in other words, stateful operations don't play well with parallel streams. You've been warned.

stream.distinct();  // stateful: an internal HashSet
stream.limit(10);   // stateful: a shared counter
stream.map(fn);     // stateless: element by element, free
stream.filter(p);   // stateless
291

How can you format a floating point number?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that.

Less short answer

Java actually supports the same kind of ugly formatting thingy that you can use in C/C++ — you know this format with sharp characters, and plus, and dot, and other things and numbers, that you never know how it works; yes, that's the one. To access this formatting thingy, you need to call printf instead of println: this printf method works the same as the printf from C — where the F means format. It takes two parameters: the format, and a variety of arguments to print — which can be empty, by the way. The format itself is implemented in a class called Formatter, where you have all the documentation and the syntax you can use — this is the class you need to check for the documentation. You can use a Formatter directly to format a string, or you can use the String.format() factory method, which creates and calls a Formatter for you. One last word: you can also format elements with one of the extensions of the Format abstract class from java.text — extended by MessageFormat, NumberFormat, DateFormat, and some others. (Another video.)

System.out.printf("PI ~ %.2f%n", Math.PI);  // PI ~ 3.14
String s = String.format("%,.3f", 12345.6789);
NumberFormat currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance(Locale.US);
currency.format(3199.99);                   // $3,199.99
292

What is a parallel friendly stream source?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A source of data that is easily splittable.

Less short answer

A parallel stream splits its source in two chunks, again and again, until each chunk reaches a certain size where the algorithm decides to process it. For this way of splitting your source to work properly, your source needs to have two properties. First, the parallel stream needs to know how many elements this source will produce — if it's not known, then dividing this source in two parts of the same size is not going to work very well. Then, the size of each chunk also needs to be known, for the algorithm to carry on.
