You may be thinking that, if the size of the source is known, then it's also the case when it is split — well, it's not always the case: for instance, if you split the internal array of a hash set, you cannot know in advance how many elements you will have in each subarray. One last word: in a nutshell, the best source is an array; other in-memory collections — like hash sets — are not so good; and IO sources are all bad. Pattern.splitAsStream, which is a non-collection, in-memory structure, is also very bad.

int[] data = ...;
Arrays.stream(data).parallel().map(this::heavy);  // great: SIZED + SUBSIZED

hashSet.stream().parallel()...   // bad: unbalanced split
Files.lines(path).parallel()...  // terrible: an IO source
293

What is a Cleaner?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An API.

Less short answer

The Cleaner API is some kind of replacement of finalization that fixes the problems this mechanism has. It allows you to execute a task when an object is about to be garbage — this task is usually a lambda; in its simple form, it's just a Runnable. Of course, if this lambda holds a reference to the object you want to clean, this reference will prevent your object from ever being garbage — and your cleaner will never be called. Be careful if you implement a cleaner with an anonymous class, because anonymous classes have a sneaky reference on this, which will prevent your object from being garbage. Your cleaners can be called by different ways, so you need to be careful with race conditions and the like. One last word: the nice thing is that a cleaner cannot revive an object, as finalization could. Great.

private static final Cleaner CLEANER = Cleaner.create();

class Resource implements AutoCloseable {
    private final Cleaner.Cleanable cleanable;
    Resource() {
        cleanable = CLEANER.register(this, () -> releaseNative(id()));
        // the lambda must NOT capture 'this'!
    }
    @Override public void close() { cleanable.clean(); } // safety net
}
294

What is operator overloading?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something Java has — but not you.

Less short answer

Operator overloading means that a given operator, like plus, can operate on different types of operands. For instance, in Java, you can apply plus on numbers, and on strings: plus can add two numbers, and can concatenate strings — this is an example of overloading. Technically, adding ints and floats is also overloading: the bytecode instruction that "plus" compiles to is not the same, and the addition is not the same either — adding ints is associative; adding floats or doubles is not. What you cannot do in Java is define your own overloading: for instance, you could define a Population record, and could define the addition of Population objects — that could make sense — but Java does not allow this; you cannot define your own overloading of operators. One last word: once Valhalla brings value types to the Java platform, your Population record will become a value record — and maybe adding operator overloading for value types could make sense. But that will be for later.

1 + 2;          // iadd
1f + 2f;        // fadd (a different instruction, a different semantics!)
"a" + "b";      // StringBuilder.append (an overload for String)

record Population(int value) { }
// Population p3 = p1 + p2;   // ERROR: no user-defined overloading
Population p3 = new Population(p1.value() + p2.value()); // a method, as usual
295

How can you stream the content of a directory?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

The method is defined on the Files factory class, and is called walk. It needs a path where the walking starts; and you can give it a depth, to avoid exploring super deep hierarchies; and you can even pass an argument to tell if you want to follow symbolic links or not. This algorithm is said to be depth-first, meaning that it will explore directories before moving to the next element in a given directory. The result you get is a Stream<Path> that is lazily evaluated — so if you're looking for a specific element, you can use a findFirst or findAny terminal operation, and the stream will interrupt the exploration once it is found. One last word: don't even think about going parallel on these streams — you can only produce them one at a time, so it will not work very well.

try (Stream<Path> s = Files.walk(Path.of("./src"), 3)) {
    Optional<Path> readme = s.filter(p -> p.endsWith("README.md"))
                             .findFirst();     // lazy: stops when found
}
296

How can you map a file to memory?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that — actually, two patterns.

Less short answer

In both cases, you start by creating a FileChannel, by providing a path and some open options. Then you have two very similar patterns. The first one consists in calling map(), passing another option — the mode — a position in the file you want to map, and the size of the region to be mapped: this is the old-fashioned code, that returns a ByteBuffer. The second pattern consists in passing another argument, which is an Arena — then what you get is a MemorySegment, and this pattern is much better than the first one for many reasons: closing your arena — usually in a try-with-resources statement — invalidates your memory segment, and flushes your modifications to the disk if needed. One last word: be careful, because closing your FileChannel does not invalidate your ByteBuffer — and if you continue writing data to it, this data may still be written to the disk; probably not what you will expect.

// the new pattern (FFM): the arena invalidates and synchronizes
try (Arena arena = Arena.ofMap()) {
    try (FileChannel ch = FileChannel.open(Path.of("data.bin"),
            StandardOpenOption.READ, StandardOpenOption.WRITE)) {
        MemorySegment seg = ch.map(FileChannel.MapMode.READ_WRITE, 0, ch.size(), arena);
        seg.set(ValueLayout.JAVA_INT, 0, 42);   // write-through
    }
}   // closing the arena: safely invalidates the segment

// legacy: closing the channel does NOT invalidate the buffer!
MappedByteBuffer buf = ch.map(FileChannel.MapMode.READ_WRITE, 0, size);
ch.close();  // buf is still alive and writable... a trap
297

How can you check if a character is a letter?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

For all these kinds of things, you need to check the factory methods of the Character class — there are about 50 of them, to check many things about your character. But you still need to be careful, because a character — the one that you write — may actually be encoded in two Java chars: that is, 32 bits, and not 16. So if your application needs to deal with non-trivial characters, the answer to this question may be much more complex than what it seems. One last word: it also has an impact on the value returned by the length method of the String class — length returns the number of Unicode code units, so it may actually be larger than what is printed on your screen.

Character.isLetter('a');      // true
Character.isLetter('á');      // true (Unicode, not just ASCII!)
Character.isLetterOrDigit('7');
Character.isLetter('😀');     // false — and it takes 2 chars (a surrogate pair)
"😀".length();                // 2 — more than the "1 character" you see
298

Are static members inherited?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

No. But yes. (a trick question)

Less short answer

This one is actually a trick question. Inheritance is defined on instances, and not for static members — so the answer is no. But there is a trick in Java, which is the following: if you have a B class that extends the A class, and A defines a public static field or method, then you can get a reference on these through B — you can write B.method(), and the compiler will be happy. But if the class B defines its own public static method with the same name, then this method will hide the method defined on the class A. One last word: this is confusing, but it is not inheritance, nor overriding — inheritance is for instances only, not for static members. Calling an instance method that can be overridden is a virtual call, that is resolved at runtime; static code is resolved at compile time.

class A { public static String who() { return "A"; } }
class B extends A { }

B.who();   // "A" — accessible through B, but... inheritance? NO: just access
// if B declares its own who(), it HIDES A's — static resolution
299

What is a cache miss?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something you want to avoid.

Less short answer

A cache miss has to do with how our CPUs are working. All the data you are processing lives in the memory of your machine — accessing this memory is slow, so your data is first moved to the different levels of caches you have on your CPU, so that it can be accessed quickly. This transfer is made by chunks of memories: more than one piece of the data you need is transferred in one go — think about an array, for instance: a single chunk may hold like eight elements of it, that are transferred in one go; for your application, that's a win. If at some point your CPU checks its cache and sees that the data it needs is not there, then it needs to go back to the main memory to get it — this is what a cache miss is. As this round trip is costly, your CPU may decide to switch to another computation, and remove yours from the CPU until the data is there — and that may be a significant performance hit. One last word: this is why you should always favor array-based, in-memory structures or collections, over reference-based structures — because cache misses can really kill your performance.

int[] data = new int[1_000_000];       // contiguous: 1 cache line ~ 8+ ints
for (int x : data) sum += x;           // very few cache misses

List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(); // references scattered on the heap
for (int x : list) sum += x;            // miss after miss (and boxing!)
300

What is a distinct stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A stream where all the elements are distinct.

Less short answer

DISTINCT is actually one of the characteristics that are defined in the spliterator of your stream — and that you can check. The stream created on a Set, for instance, has this characteristic set: it means that if you call distinct() on it, nothing is done — all the elements are passed directly. If the stream is created on an ArrayList, then calling distinct will store them in a hash set before pushing them — or not — to the downstream. Since calling distinct returns a distinct stream, calling distinct again will not trigger any computation — which is what you want. One last word: unfortunately, you cannot check these characteristics from the lambdas you pass to the intermediate or terminal methods of your stream — and yes, that includes forEach and collect.

Set<String> set = Set.of("a", "b");
set.stream().distinct();   // a no-op: the spliterator is already DISTINCT

List<String> l = List.of("a", "a", "b");
l.stream().distinct();     // goes through an internal HashSet
// .distinct().distinct()  // the 2nd is a no-op by construction
301

What is dependency injection?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A mechanism to inject dependencies into an object.

Less short answer

Especially in large applications, the initialization of some objects can be really complex, with complex dependency graphs — like: object A needs object B to work, that itself needs B1 and B2, etc. — and this initialization requires to be made in a precise order to work properly. To organize this initialization, your best bet is: for each object to declare what it needs to work, and let some external element initialize everything. It could be some third-party framework, or some internal tool that you've developed yourself — in simple cases, it could even be a simple main method. The easiest and safest way to declare that an object A needs objects A1, A2 and A3 to work, is to create a constructor in A that takes instances of A1, A2 and A3. By using Reflection, a third-party framework can discover that, and do the wiring of all your objects for you — this is called constructor injection, and this is the way you should favor. One last word: the type of your dependencies should always be interfaces — it allows you to easily create dummy implementations for testing, and you can even use frameworks for that.

class OrderService {                          // constructor injection
    private final Repository repo;            // a dependency by INTERFACE
    private final Notifier notifier;
    OrderService(Repository repo, Notifier notifier) {
        this.repo = repo; this.notifier = notifier;
    }
}
// the "external element" (framework/main) does the wiring
new OrderService(new PostgresRepo(), new EmailNotifier());
302

What is a monitor?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something that has to do with synchronization.

Less short answer

Objects have been designed in Java so that each of them can act as a monitor for synchronization. You can use any object — even just a simple new Object() — to synchronize a block of code; this is due to the fact that every object carries a monitor, which is stored in the header of any instance. To be able to enter a synchronized block of code, a thread needs to hold this monitor: it enters the block, takes this monitor, and gives it back when it exits. Then, if another thread tries to enter the same block at the same time, it will discover that the monitor is absent, so it will have to wait until it is available again. One last word: having a monitor for each object has two consequences: first, it consumes memory — not much, but it is still an overhead; and second, it prevents some optimizations, like inlining or flattening objects — the reason why value types will not have any monitor. (Another video.)

private final Object lock = new Object();  // any object will do

synchronized (lock) {        // the thread holds the header's monitor
    counter++;              // mutual exclusion guaranteed
}                           // the monitor is released here
303

What is the epoch?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A special date in operating systems and APIs.

Less short answer

Measuring time is actually harder than it seems — especially when all you have are 16-bits integers. So at some point, when the first operating systems were created, people decided that the time would be measured in seconds, starting on January 1st, 1970; it could be different from one OS to the other, so this definition may vary. Of course, this used to have limitations, and could generate bugs — especially if you were working with a language whose definition of the epoch was different from the one in your operating system. But now things have been sorted out... well, mostly. One last word: if you need it in your application, you have two constants in the Java API to model this epoch: LocalDate.EPOCH and Instant.EPOCH.

Instant.EPOCH;                  // 1970-01-01T00:00:00Z
LocalDate.EPOCH;                // 1970-01-01
Instant.now().getEpochSecond(); // seconds since the epoch
304

How is switch on String working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Switching on a string is actually switching on an integer.

Less short answer

The possibility to switch on strings was added in JDK 7, in 2011 — and it uses a trick: it actually computes the hash code of your selector variable, along with the hash codes of your case labels, and switches on integers. The hashCode method that is used is the one of the String class — which has an unfortunate consequence: it comes carved in marble. Changing it would require to recompile all the existing Java code, since the output of this method is hard-coded in the bytecode of your switch statements on strings. One last word: you can now switch on types — this is one of the cornerstones of data oriented programming — and switching on primitive types may also come in the future.

// compiles to: switch (s.hashCode()) { case 104827: if (s.equals("run"))... }
switch (command) {
    case "start" -> start();
    case "stop"  -> stop();
    default      -> help();
}
// this is why String.hashCode() can NEVER change
305

What is a design pattern?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A recipe to write clean code.

Less short answer

It is actually a term that refers to a book published in 1994, called Design Patterns: Elements of Reusable Object-Oriented Software — also called the Gang of Four, due to its four authors. Its influence on programming is still immense, even if some of the proposed implementations are a little dated — the book is written in C++, because it was published before Java was released, in 1995. There are 23 design patterns in it, each one to solve a particular problem in programming. Some are still very well known — like Iterator, Factory, Singleton, or Strategy; some less used, like Memento. The GoF also gives you two principles in programming: favor object composition over class inheritance, and program to an interface, not to an implementation. One last word: the authors created more design patterns in other books — but I don't think any of them could reach the popularity and influence of the Gang of Four.

// two GoF principles in action:
List<String> list = new ArrayList<>();  // to the INTERFACE (List)
class Car { private final Engine engine; // COMPOSITION, not inheritance
    Car(Engine e) { this.engine = e; } }
306

What are bounded type parameters?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Some ugly stuff you never know how it is working.

Less short answer

You know this bizarre question-mark-super-T syntax, or question mark extends R? Yes, this is what we're talking about. This syntax was added with Generics, to manage type inheritance. On the one hand, we need it; and on the other hand, we never know what to put, and end up trying stuff until the compiler is happy. So, in a nutshell: ? super T represents all the types that are supertypes of T. For instance, the method Collection.forEach takes a Consumer<? super T>, so that when you write strings.forEach(...), you can pass System.out::println — that takes instances of Object, which is indeed a supertype of the String type. On the other hand, Stream.map takes a function that returns ? extends R, and produces a Stream<R> — meaning that this function can return any subtype of R, so that it can be cast into R. One piece of advice: if you're struggling with all this, you can check the signatures of the methods of the Stream API — there are plenty of examples that will help you understand all that. One last word: this is probably the most tricky part of Generics — and if you check the methods of Comparator, you may even come across multiple bounded type parameters. (Another video.)

// ? super T (a consumer): accepts supertypes
List<String> names = List.of("a");
names.forEach(System.out::println);   // println accepts Object (super String)

// ? extends R (a producer): returns subtypes
Stream<Number> nums = Stream.of(1, 2L).map(n -> n);  // Integer -> Number

// multiple bounds:
static <T extends Object & Comparable<T>> T max(List<T> l) { ... }
307

What is a statement?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Almost anything is a statement.

Less short answer

A statement is a construct that represents a complete unit of execution. An expression can be made a statement if you add the semicolon after it: assignments, method invocations, incrementations or decrementations, object creations — these are called the expression statements. There are also control flow statements: if-then-else, switch, for, while, even return — all these are control flow statements. One last word: statements are grouped into blocks, that are delimited by curly braces; such blocks can be used wherever a single statement can be used — and sometimes making a block, even for a single statement, can make your code more readable.

int x = 0;                 // assignment (an expression statement)
method();                  // invocation
x++;                       // increment
if (x > 0) {               // control flow + a block
    log("positive");       // a readable block, even with 1 statement
}
308

What is a thread scheduler?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An object that schedules threads.

Less short answer

A thread is a piece of code that is executed independently of other threads — and while doing so, it uses some CPU resource. At some point, if there are many threads running at the same time, you need to be able to suspend a thread so that the others get a chance to run. The suspension should happen if the thread is blocked — for instance, waiting for some data coming from the disk or the network, or waiting for some monitor that is preventing it from executing a synchronized piece of code. That is the role of the thread scheduler: making sure that the CPU resource is evenly shared among threads, and that the active threads are indeed using the CPU resource — and not waiting on the CPU doing nothing. This capacity of suspending a thread is called preemptiveness: the thread scheduler can be preemptive, or non-preemptive. One last word: virtual threads are using a specific virtual thread scheduler, that is not preemptive — the reason why you should never conduct long-running, in-memory computations in your virtual threads; if you do, you could block your entire virtual thread system.

// OK: blocking on IO -> the virtual thread unmounts, the carrier is released
Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> downloadFile(url));

// BAD: a long computation monopolizes the carrier (non-preemptive scheduler)
Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> computePi(10_000_000)); // use a platform thread!
309

What is a final method?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A method with the final keyword on its declaration.

Less short answer

The use of the final keyword there prevents any kind of overriding or hiding from the extending classes. For static methods: you cannot create a static method with the same signature in any extending class — that would hide this static method, and it is forbidden; so a final static method cannot be hidden in any extending class. For instance methods, it's the same: it means that this method cannot be overridden in any extending class. In both cases, it is possible to have a method with the same name and different parameters, in the same class or in any extending class. One last word (no pun intended): if you have a final static method in a class A, it also prevents a non-static method with the same name in any extending class — and the reverse is also true: a final instance method in A prevents any static method with the same name in its extending classes.

class A {
    final void process() { }        // cannot be overridden
    final static void helper() { }  // cannot be hidden
}
class B extends A {
    // void process() { }           // ERROR: overriding a final method
    // static void helper() { }     // ERROR: hiding a final method
    void process(String s) { }      // OK: overloading (other params)
}
310

What is a LocalDateTime?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class from the Date and Time API.

Less short answer

The Date and Time API has several classes to represent dates and times — all final — and LocalDateTime is one of them. Local means that the date it represents is not bound to any time zone; LocalDateTime carries both a date and a time. There are several factory methods to create instances — that take a year, month and day of the month, plus an hour, minute, second, and even nanoseconds. There are two methods to bind this date to a time zone — atOffset and atZone — and plenty of others to add temporal amounts, like days or hours. LocalDateTime objects are non-modifiable: all these methods return new objects. One last word: all these objects from the Date and Time API are good candidates to become value types once Valhalla makes it as a final feature — as such, they are already annotated with @ValueBased. (Another video.)

LocalDateTime dt = LocalDateTime.of(2026, 10, 4, 15, 30);
dt.plusDays(1);                              // a new object (immutable)
dt.atZone(ZoneId.of("America/Sao_Paulo"));   // binds to a zone
dt.toLocalDate();                            // the date only
dt.toLocalTime();                            // the time only
311

When does a switch check for exhaustiveness?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Not always. (the presenter's short answer for this one, which is trickier than it seems)

Less short answer

The switch changed over time. What does it mean for a switch to check for exhaustiveness? It means that if, for some values of your selector variable, you can traverse your switch block without matching anything, you get an error. In its initial version, the switch was a statement — and statements do not check for exhaustiveness, and they still don't, for backward compatibility reasons. But the switch expression — the one that returns a value — requires exhaustiveness: either you cover all the cases, or you provide a default — without that, a compilation error. With sealed types and pattern matching, the compiler knows the closed set of subtypes and can prove the coverage — that is where the check shines.

int days(Weekday d) {           // a statement: no requirement
    switch (d) {
        case MON -> return 1;   // missing the other cases: it compiles!
        default -> return 0;
    }
}

int code(Weekday d) {           // an expression: EXHAUSTIVE or it doesn't compile
    return switch (d) {
        case MON -> 1;
        case TUE -> 2;
        // ... covering all of them (or a default) is mandatory
    };
}
312

What is wrong with ThreadLocal variables?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Almost everything.

Less short answer

ThreadLocal is a class from Java 2, 1998 — a time when there was no ExecutorService, which is a feature of Java 5, 2004 — so that's six years later. If you carefully check the API, you will see that in Java 5, a remove() method was added to the ThreadLocal class. Because before the ExecutorService pattern, you would create a thread on demand and let it die — and its ThreadLocal variables would go long with it. The ExecutorService pattern put an end to this golden era: now you reuse your platform threads — and you need to take care of the removal of your ThreadLocal variables. If you don't, everything you put in them can be reused by any other code running in the thread you are reusing — probably not what you want. One last word: ThreadLocal variables are fully supported by virtual threads, but starting with Java 25, you can use ScopedValues instead — they work much better, they can be optimized by the JVM, and you don't need to remove them.

private static final ThreadLocal<User> CTX = new ThreadLocal<>();

CTX.set(user);            // in a thread pool...
process();                // ...another task on the SAME thread
CTX.remove();             // ...without this, it sees your user!

// Java 25+: no remove, bound to the scope
private static final ScopedValue<User> CTX2 = ScopedValue.newInstance();
ScopedValue.where(CTX2, user).run(this::process);
313

What is a guarded pattern?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A pattern with a guard.

Less short answer

A guarded pattern is something you can use in a case label. Such a label starts with a type pattern or a record pattern, and is followed by your check — the when clause — on the type variables that you created. For instance, you can create a case where you only select the circles that are blue, another one for the circles that are green, and a last one for all the other circles. Two elements you need to be aware of. First: a case label with a guarded pattern never counts when it comes to checking if your switch is exhaustive or not. And second: a guarded pattern can never dominate any other pattern — so most of the time, you will need a case where you check the type or the record with no guard, for your switch to compile. One last word: there is an exception to all this, for the guarded pattern when true — but please avoid doing that in your code.

switch (shape) {
    case Circle c when c.color() == BLUE  -> paintSea(c);
    case Circle c when c.color() == GREEN -> paintField(c);
    case Circle c                         -> paintNeutral(c); // no guard!
    default                               -> ignore();
}
314

What does work stealing mean?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Probably not what you think.

Less short answer

It is a pattern that is used in a ForkJoinPool, where you have several waitlists that can accept tasks; each waitlist is bound to a specific thread, that picks tasks from this list. Now imagine a time where this list — and the end of this list — is empty, while the other lists in the pool are not: in that case, the starving thread can steal a task from another list — and this is called work stealing. This pattern is used in parallel streams — where the main thread creates the first tasks, which are stolen by other threads from the common pool — and it's also used in virtual threads, where a virtual thread that is coming back to the carrier it was unmounted from can also be taken by another kernel thread, in case the first one is busy. One last word: all this is transparent for you, as an application developer — which is a good thing, because it comes with issues when it comes to race conditions and visibility, that are all taken care of by the API.

// ForkJoinPool: a deque per thread + stealing from the opposite end
var pool = ForkJoinPool.commonPool();
list.parallelStream().map(this::heavy)  // tasks are divided;
    .collect(toList());                 // idle threads steal work
315

How can you get the substring from a string?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

There are actually two overloads of this method. The first one takes only the starting index, and gives you all the rest of the string; and the second one gives you the string between a starting index and an end index — excluded. You will get a StringIndexOutOfBoundsException if you provide bad indexes. One last word: there was a time, back in the days, where the string that was returned would share the same char array as the original string — and this was seen as a memory optimization. First, strings are now built on byte arrays, not char arrays anymore; and second, this was removed a long time ago — just because if you select only two characters from a very large string that you don't need anymore, keeping this original array alive was not really a memory optimization.

String s = "Cracking the Java Coding Interview";
s.substring(13);          // "Java Coding Interview"
s.substring(13, 17);      // "Java" (the end is exclusive)
// s.substring(-1);       // StringIndexOutOfBoundsException
316

How is the Collectors.toMap() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

This collector is used to build maps from keys and values.

Less short answer

It takes two parameters — both are functions that take the element of the stream: the first one is used to build a key, and the second one to build the value. If you are sure that no duplicate will be generated from your keys, then you can stop there — each element will generate a key-value pair in the final map. If you think that you may have duplicates — or you're sure you will — then you can provide the third argument, which is a merging function: it is called by the implementation with the two values that are bound to the same key, and you should return a merged value. If you don't, you will have an exception. One last word: if merging means adding the elements to a list, then you may consider using Collectors.groupingBy instead of toMap — which can create this list for you.

Map<String, Integer> byName = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.toMap(Person::name, Person::age));

Map<String, Integer> withMerge = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.toMap(Person::city, Person::age, Integer::sum));

// merging into lists? groupingBy is better:
Map<String, List<Person>> byCity = people.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Person::city));
317

What is an object header?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A header that the JVM needs to manage your objects in memory.

Less short answer

Each object in Java has a header that carries information that the JVM needs — the size of this header can be as big as 16 bytes: something significant for small objects, like an instance of Integer. Several things are stored in this header: the monitor used for locking, that takes 2 bits; 4 bits are used by the garbage collector; 31 bits for the identity hash code; and 32 bits for a pointer to the class of the object — and some of the bits are not used. One last word: this is a lot of memory, and not all of it is actually used — some objects never get an identity hash code, and most of them are never used for locking. That's the goal of the Lilliput project: to compress things and reduce the size of this header. (Another video.)

Header (64 bits, compressed klass pointer):
| monitor 2b | GC 4b | identity hash 31b | unused | klass ptr 32b |
318

What is data alignment?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something that has to do with storing elements at specific addresses in memory, to read them efficiently.

Less short answer

Most CPUs require your data to be aligned in a specific way in memory. Your shorts — two bytes — should be aligned on addresses that are multiples of two; your ints — four bytes — on addresses that are multiples of four; and so on. Any short, int or long that does not comply is said to be not properly aligned, or misaligned. It does not mean that you cannot read it anymore, of course — it only means that you may need two CPU cycles instead of one to read it. One last word: it depends on where your CPU communicates with your memory; a safe approach would be to make sure — to always align your data properly. But dealing with misaligned data is always possible — this can be configured if you use the Memory API to read this data. (Another video.)

an aligned int    @ address 100 (a multiple of 4): 1 read cycle
a misaligned int  @ address 102: spans 2 words -> 2 cycles
319

What is a reference?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

That one is actually a trick question.

Less short answer

Every variable in your Java code that is not of a primitive type is a reference variable: a reference to an object. It is implemented by a pointer that contains an address — but it is not itself an address. If we had a sizeof method in Java, the size of any reference variable would be... the memory needed to store the pointer (4 or 8 bytes depending on your machine) — not the size of the object this reference is pointing to. We do not have pointers in Java — only references — and, of course, no possibility to add anything to a pointer, as is the case in C or C++. One last word: as you know, if you compare two reference variables with ==, what you're comparing are the values of these references, not the objects that these references are pointing to — so the pointers are still there, just under the surface. This may change with value types and Valhalla. (Another video.)

String a = new String("x");
String b = new String("x");
a == b;        // false: compares the REFERENCES (2 different addresses)
a.equals(b);   // true: compares the objects
// does not exist in Java: a + 1 (pointer arithmetic)
320

What is a wrapper class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A class that wraps a primitive type.

Less short answer

Six of them are extensions of the Number abstract class — Byte, Short, Integer, Long, and then Float and Double; the two others are not — Character and Boolean. They are all very convenient, as they carry utility methods — for instance, to parse strings of characters, or to check for some properties of the wrapped value — and they also define constants: MAX_VALUE and MIN_VALUE for the integer types; TRUE and FALSE for Boolean; and the floating point numbers also have NaN — which stands for not a number — POSITIVE_INFINITY, NEGATIVE_INFINITY, and some others. They also have a TYPE constant, that returns the Class of the primitive type — used in the Reflection API. One last word: there is no plan to add any more primitive types to the Java language — but with Valhalla and value types, there will be more wrappable types, like a Float16, that is already there as a preview feature.

Integer.parseInt("42");         // parsing utilities
Integer.MAX_VALUE;              // constants
Double.isNaN(x);                // properties
Character.isLetter('a');
Integer.TYPE == int.class;      // for reflection
Float16.valueOf(1.5f);          // preview (Valhalla)
321

How can you insert a list into another list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You can use subList() for that.

Less short answer

subList is a method from the List interface, which gives you a sublist from a list — it takes two arguments: a beginning index and an ending index. Another point: this sublist is a view on the first list, and this view is modifiable. Calling addAll on this sublist will insert the collection you pass into the main list, right at the position of the end of the view. If you're using an ArrayList — as you should — you should also favor a single addAll over several add calls: each of them triggers a System.arraycopy on the internal array. One last word: this pattern also works with clear(), that can delete a range of elements in a list.

List<String> l = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "d"));
l.subList(1, 1).addAll(List.of("b", "c")); // inserts at index 1
System.out.println(l);                     // [a, b, c, d]

l.subList(0, 2).clear();                   // erases the range [0, 2)
System.out.println(l);                     // [c, d]
322

What is polymorphism?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

One of the principles of object-oriented programming.

Less short answer

Inheritance makes it so that, when you call an instance method on an object, the real method that your program is going to use can only be known at runtime, and not at compile time. Why? Because at runtime, you can pass an extension of the declared class — with new code that overrides the method you are calling, and that is not known by the compiler. This overriding method is the one that should be called; so the method that is executed when you run your application can only be resolved at runtime. This is obvious when you think about interfaces, because there you may not have an implementation — but it's also the case for regular classes. One last word: this is for instance methods; a static method can also hide another static method, but it is a different mechanism, that is resolved at compile time.

Shape s = new Circle(2);   // declared Shape, really a Circle
s.area();                  // which area()? only at RUNTIME -> Circle.area()

static class A { static String m() { return "A"; } }
static class B extends A { static String m() { return "B"; } }
A x = new B();
x.m();                     // "A"! static: resolved at COMPILE time
323

What is a module?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A notion that is very useful — and that you probably don't use.

Less short answer

Modules were added in Java 9, in 2016. A module is a set of classes, and other things, that can live in a jar once compiled, and that has a descriptor — in the form of a module-info.java file. This descriptor defines, among other things, the modules this module depends on, and which classes it exposes to the outside world. This point is very interesting, because it adds a level of protection for your classes: you can choose the ones you need to make public, and the ones you prefer to keep private. So if you're a framework developer, you can change the internals without breaking your users' application — they just can't see these classes; super convenient. One last word: the nice thing with this system is that it's completely optional to use: you can stick to the classical class path, and continue writing your application as if it was not there. Backward compatibility is preserved, as it should be.

module com.acme.payments {
    requires java.net.http;            // explicit dependencies
    exports com.acme.payments.api;    // only this is visible outside
    // com.acme.payments.internal -> invisible to the others!
}
324

What is a ServiceLoader?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A service that can tell you what implementation was loaded for any given interface. (in practice: the implementation-discovery mechanism)

Less short answer

If you build a jar with an implementation of an interface — like your JDBC driver, for instance — you can add a declaration in this jar, telling that you're providing this implementation. This declaration is a simple text file, that sits in the META-INF/services directory of your jar file: its name is the full name of the interface you implement, and its content is the name of the class that implements that interface (note that you can put several class names, if you have more than one). Then you can use the ServiceLoader.load(MyInterface.class) method, passing it the interface you want: it returns a ServiceLoader object, that can give you an iterator or a stream, to find the implementations you're looking for. One last word: this mechanism was added in Java 6 — that was a long time ago! You can check the jars as examples, to see how this ServiceLoader is working.

META-INF/services/com.acme.Repository
-------------------------------------
com.acme.PostgresRepository
com.acme.MongoRepository

ServiceLoader<Repository> loaders = ServiceLoader.load(Repository.class);
loaders.stream().forEach(p -> System.out.println(p.type()));
for (Repository r : loaders) { ... }   // the iterator, too
325

What are the different kinds of arenas?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are four of them.

Less short answer

An arena is the object from which you can create memory segments in the Memory API — and you can construct four types of arenas, with the four factory methods on the Arena interface: the global, the auto, the confined, and the shared arenas. The global arena shares the life cycle of your application; the auto arena can be garbage-collected — but as for the global arena, calling close() on it throws an exception. The confined and the shared arenas can be closed by calling close() on them, which closes the memory segments you created with them. And of all these four arenas, the confined arena is the only one that cannot be shared among different threads — meaning that the memory segments you create from it can only be used in the thread that created this arena. One last word: if you're just exploring the API, writing some code you will throw away after that, you can safely use the auto or the global arena; but for your application, you should favor the confined arena — which is the easiest one to close — or the shared arena, if you need the multi-thread access; but in that case, closing it will take longer.

Arena.ofGlobal();   // lives with the JVM; close() throws
Arena.ofAuto();     // released by the GC; close() throws

try (Arena a = Arena.ofConfined()) {  // deterministic close; 1 thread
    MemorySegment s1 = a.allocate(1024);
}
try (Arena a = Arena.ofShared()) {    // multi-thread; slower close
    MemorySegment s2 = a.allocate(1024);
}
326

How can you format a date?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are patterns for that.

Less short answer

There is the SimpleDateFormat in java.text, that extends Format — which is the legacy way of formatting dates. This is the class that carries the JavaDoc for these date and time patterns, based on the use of y, M, d, and other letters that you never know how they work. Be careful, because this class carries a mutable state — so trying to put an instance of it as a constant in a class, so that you can reuse it, is a mistake that will give you race conditions. The Date and Time API from Java 8 has its own class to format and parse dates and times, called DateTimeFormatter: it is non-modifiable, so it can be safely shared between threads. One last word: it also uses these "horrible" y-M-d letter-based formats — but it also provides a bunch of standard formats that follow the ISO recommendations.

// legacy: mutable — do NOT share between threads
var sdf = new SimpleDateFormat("dd/MM/yyyy");
sdf.format(new Date());

// modern: immutable and thread-safe
DateTimeFormatter fmt = DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("dd/MM/yyyy");
LocalDate.now().format(fmt);                    // 04/10/2026
LocalDate.parse("04/10/2026", fmt);
DateTimeFormatter.ISO_LOCAL_DATE;               // 2026-10-04 (ISO standard)
327

How is the Collectors.mapping() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

This is the factory method from the Collectors factory class, that creates a collector that can do a mapping — and that needs to push the result to another collector.

Less short answer

Mapping is an intermediate operation of the Stream API — and a collector is used in a terminal operation; so this factory method needs a downstream collector, which will receive the result of the mapping of the elements pushed by the upstream. Of course, this downstream collector can itself be a composition of more collectors — but you need to be careful there, because composing too many collectors may lead to some code that is hard to read and harder to understand. One last word: collector composition is a very powerful feature of the Stream API — you can use it when you build maps with groupingBy, for instance, to create the exact histogram you need to analyze your data.

// group cities by country, mapping to the name (a composed downstream)
Map<String, List<String>> byCountry = cities.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
                City::country,
                Collectors.mapping(City::name,
                        Collectors.toList())));
328

How can you get the content of a directory?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are several patterns for that.

Less short answer

There is a legacy pattern available on the File class: it consists of several methods — list() or listFiles() — that return arrays of strings (the file names), or arrays of Files. You can also give a FilenameFilter object — which can be a lambda, by the way — that will filter the elements of the file directory before adding them to the result. But if you need to visit sub-directories, you need to do that by hand. The preferred patterns are on the Files factory class: the walkFileTree(dir, visitor) method takes a file visitor as a parameter, that is called for every element visited — and that can visit sub-directories; and the walk(dir) method returns a stream of paths, that contains all the elements of these directories — and which can also be used to visit sub-directories. One last word: you should always prefer the patterns from the Files class over the File class — they are based on the use of the Path interface, that supports different file systems, among other things; the File class is mostly a wrapper on a string of characters, with fewer possibilities than the Path interface.

// legacy
File dir = new File(".");
dir.listFiles((d, name) -> name.endsWith(".txt"));

// preferred: a stream of paths, including sub-directories
try (Stream<Path> s = Files.walk(Path.of("."))) {
    s.filter(p -> p.toString().endsWith(".txt"))
     .forEach(System.out::println);
}
329

What is the difference between a value and an object?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A value is a value; an object is an object. (the presenter's deliberate tautology)

Less short answer

A value is something that is inherently unmodifiable — and, as a consequence, can be defined by a name that fully qualifies it. For instance, one is a value: its name qualifies it — and by the way, this name is not related to the object. If you assign this value to a variable, then you can change the value of this variable to two — it changes the value of your variable, but neither one nor two change. A value can safely be flattened and inlined where you use it. Whereas an object is something that can carry a state — this state can be defined by a set of possible values, and can be modifiable; in that case, if your code needs to check this state, it needs to read a specific piece of memory: no inlining, no flattening, no scalarization. An object can also have a behavior, in the form of methods — as well as a value, by the way: in Java, primitives do not have behavior, but wrappers do. One last word: if an object cannot be modified, then it may define a value that can be inlined, scalarized or flattened — and this is exactly what Valhalla is doing.

int one = 1;              // a value: inlinable, no identity
one = 2;                  // the variable changes; the values 1 and 2, never

var list = new ArrayList<>();  // an object: mutable state, identity,
list.add("x");                 // an address in memory, no inlining
330

How is flatMap() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Very well. But you can do better. (the presenter's joke)

Less short answer

flatMap is an intermediate method from the Stream interface. It works like mapping, except that the mapping function needs to return a stream, instead of any object: so each element of the upstream is mapped to its own stream, by the flat-mapping function — and then all these streams are flattened in the resulting stream. flatMap can be used to concatenate streams, and is actually more efficient than Stream.concat(), that you should use with caution. One last word: you can also implement the flat-mapping feature with the mapMulti() method — in that case, you do not create any intermediate streams (the ones that are flattened); and in some cases, it can be more efficient to use this method.

Stream.of("java rocks", "streams top")
      .flatMap(s -> Arrays.stream(s.split(" ")))
      .forEach(System.out::println);   // java rocks streams top

// the alternative without intermediate streams:
      .mapMulti((String s, Consumer<String> down) -> {
          for (String p : s.split(" ")) down.accept(p);
      })
331

What is a memory layout?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An API from the Memory API.

Less short answer

A memory layout is an API that allows you to describe how your data is organized — in a file, for instance, or in the off-heap memory of your application. Think about the structs you have in C, for instance, where you state that a color is made of opacity, cyan, magenta, yellow, and black — and that a pixel is made of two coordinates and a color. Well, the Memory Layout API gives you the elements you need, so that you can describe this kind of thing in Java. You can create these memory layouts by hand, or use a tool for that, called jextract: jextract can analyze a .h file that contains a C struct, and generate the corresponding memory layout objects for you — plus, it will generate the var handles that you need to read and write these struct-like elements in your memory. One last word: once you have these memory layout objects, you can use them to create var handles — precisely to access this data in files, or in the off-heap memory of your application. (Another video.)

// C struct: struct color { unsigned char opac, cyan, magenta, yellow, black; }
StructLayout COLOR = MemoryLayout.structLayout(
        ValueLayout.JAVA_BYTE.withName("opac"),
        ValueLayout.JAVA_BYTE.withName("cyan"),
        ValueLayout.JAVA_BYTE.withName("magenta"),
        ValueLayout.JAVA_BYTE.withName("yellow"),
        ValueLayout.JAVA_BYTE.withName("black"));

VarHandle opac = COLOR.varHandle(MemoryLayout.PathElement.groupElement("opac"));
opac.set(segment, (byte) 255);
332

How is the string hash code computed?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a polynomial: the coefficients are the characters of the string, the degree is the length of the string, and it is computed for the value 31.

Less short answer

Why 31? Well, because it's a prime number — and because 31 − 2 = 29 is also a prime number ("I'm not kidding", says the presenter). But don't worry: there is an iterative algorithm to compute that — h = 31h + c — and if the string is too long, there is even an implementation that leverages the SIMD of your CPU. In a nutshell, this hash code is quite expensive to compute — fortunately, a string is non-modifiable, so this hash code is computed once, when you first need it, and then cached for later use. One last word: unfortunately, this caching makes the String class "modifiable" — because it has two fields: the hash code, and a hashIsZero flag — and for that, and probably other things, the String class cannot be a value class as it is now.

// s[0]*31^(n-1) + s[1]*31^(n-2) + ... + s[n-1], computed as:
int h = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
    h = 31 * h + s.charAt(i);   // the JIT optimizes to (h << 5) - h
}
// the value is cached in the private hash field (String is immutable)
333

How is the Collectors.partitioningBy() working?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It partitions your data on a predicate, and puts the result in a map.

Less short answer

Collectors.partitioningBy is a factory method of the Collectors factory class, that builds a map with keys that are booleans. It is built on a predicate, that is evaluated for all the elements that your stream has: the element is put in a map where the keys are the two booleans, true and false. So, once your data is processed, you can call map.get(true) or map.get(false), to get the two partitions. This is useful when you need to filter your data, but still need to work with the elements that were rejected by your predicate. One last word: there is no overload of this method that would take a map supplier to create the instance of the map — as you have on other factories from the Collectors factory class, like groupingBy or toMap; for partitioningBy, what you get is an implementation specific to Map<Boolean, List<T>>.

Map<Boolean, List<Integer>> parts = Stream.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6)
        .collect(Collectors.partitioningBy(n -> n % 2 == 0));
parts.get(true);   // [2, 4, 6]
parts.get(false);  // [1, 3, 5]  <- the rejected ones stay reachable
334

What is the identity hash code?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An int, that is the identity of this object.

Less short answer

The System.identityHashCode() static method is a native method — meaning that the implementation is not written in Java. It guarantees that, for a given object, it will always return the same value — meaning that this value can be used in the same method; it cannot be created from the fields of this object, since they can be mutated. The value of this identity hash code has to do with the address where this object is stored in the heap memory of your application — which is a little more complicated than it seems, because the garbage collector would probably like to move it around while doing its job. One last word: with Valhalla, instances of value classes do not have an identity — so this method will throw an exception in that case.

Object o1 = new Object();
Object o2 = new Object();
System.identityHashCode(o1) == System.identityHashCode(o1); // true (stable)
System.identityHashCode(o1) == System.identityHashCode(o2); // almost never
// the basis of IdentityHashMap (compares by identity, not equals)
335

How can you use Stream.peek()?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

With moderation. (the video jokes about it)

Less short answer

Unless you use it to debug your stream processing, stay away from this method — it's even written in the JavaDoc: this method mainly exists to support debugging. It takes a Consumer, and returns the object processed by the stream. If you are doing side effects in this method, and you are on a parallel stream, you are on your own when it comes to synchronizing your data — and if you do that, you will probably lose all the benefits of going parallel. One last word: stick to the documentation — if you want to understand the objects that are flowing through your stream, this method is useful; but don't use it in a production environment.

Stream.of("a", "b", "c")
      .peek(s -> log.debug("before the filter: {}", s))  // debug only!
      .filter(s -> !s.equals("b"))
      .forEach(System.out::println);
336

What is the singleton?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A pattern from the GoF.

Less short answer

In a nutshell, the singleton is implemented by classes that can have only one instance — and implementing a singleton correctly is actually not that simple. There are two constraints on a singleton. First, you want to initialize them lazily — only if they are needed, and when they are needed; why? Because in large applications, you may have many singletons — and if you decide to initialize all of them before your application is available for doing anything, then you may have to wait for some time. And second, you want this initialization to be thread-safe — and if it's not handled correctly, you may end up with several instances instead of one, which could be a problem. So people came up with — same with many — many solutions to implement singletons correctly; but in the end, you need one class per singleton, which is not that great. One last word: implementing a singleton with an enumeration is probably, as of now, your best bet — avoid this horrible double-checked locking pattern; and keep an eye on the lazy constants that are coming to the JDK — that could be the solution you need. (Another video.)

enum ConnectionPool {             // the idiomatic singleton (Effective Java)
    INSTANCE;
    private final Pool pool = Pool.create();
    Pool get() { return pool; }
}
// usage: ConnectionPool.INSTANCE.get()
337

What is double-checked locking?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something that looks like a good idea — but that is not that great.

Less short answer

It is a complex pattern, error-prone — and odds are that you don't need it. This pattern was first created to implement the Singleton pattern: it can initialize your singleton lazily and in a safe way — but the code you need to write for that... it's just horrible; and performance-wise, it's not so great: your field needs to be volatile, meaning that every time you read it, there is a check to make sure that it has not been modified by some other thread — which, by the way, is not supposed to happen. One last word: using an enumeration was the solution proposed by the excellent book Effective Java, written by Joshua Bloch — and it is still the simplest way to create singletons in your application; it is used in the JDK itself — you can check the Comparator.naturalOrder() factory method, for instance.

// double-checked locking: it works, but... (a volatile field is mandatory)
class Config {
    private static volatile Config instance;
    static Config get() {
        if (instance == null) {
            synchronized (Config.class) {
                if (instance == null) instance = new Config();
            }
        }
        return instance;
    }
}

// the Effective Java way: an enum — simple and bulletproof
enum ConfigHolder { INSTANCE;
    static final Config CONFIG = new Config(); // lazy at first class-init
}
338

What is the string pool?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A pool of strings. (the short answer of someone who doesn't want to spoil it)

Less short answer

Strings of characters may take a lot of space in your memory — and since you cannot change the value of a string of characters during its lifetime, it may be an optimization to try to cache them. You can do so by calling the intern() method on the string: it returns a string — calling this method will check if an instance already in the pool has the same value as your string; if it's not the case, then it will add it — and in both cases, it returns the instance that is in the pool. In the end, you may save memory by doing that: no duplication is possible in a pool. Putting all your strings in a pool may sound like something you should be doing — actually, you need to be a little more cautious than that: being a string in a pool is interesting only if you have many references to it; if it is a specific string that could be garbage soon — don't put it in a pool. One last word: you need to be very cautious when it comes to optimization or possible performance gain: whatever you do, you need to carefully observe the effects on your application in a production environment.

String a = "hello";                  // a literal: already goes to the pool
String b = new String("hello");      // a new instance, outside the pool
a == b;                              // false
a == b.intern();                     // true (intern returns the pool instance)
339

What is the off-heap memory?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A portion of memory that is not in the heap memory.

Less short answer

The heap memory is the realm of the garbage collector: the GC can give you pieces of this memory, and then, when you don't need them anymore, it will take them back — in a nutshell, that's what a garbage collector is doing. The off-heap memory is not managed by the garbage collector; to get pieces of it, you need to use the old ByteBuffer API — or better, the Memory API. You can get very large segments in it, that will not be moved around by the garbage collector activity — so it's great to map files in memory, for instance. One last word: the ByteBuffer API is an API from Java 4, that was in 2002, and it can give you access to the off-heap memory — it was superseded in Java 22 by the Memory API, which is safer to use, and can index the memory segments with long instead of int for the ByteBuffer: much better for 64-bits systems.

// off-heap via FFM (Java 22+): a large segment, outside the GC
try (Arena arena = Arena.ofShared()) {
    MemorySegment file = mapInMemory(arena, Path.of("data.bin"));
    long size = file.byteSize();   // long indexes (ByteBuffer: int)
}
340

Why don't stateful operations play well with parallel streams?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Because you need to share the state between the multiple threads.

Less short answer

Some stream operations are said to be stateful — that's the case for limit or skip, for instance. Limit needs to interrupt your stream after a given amount of elements — and if your stream is ordered, you need to be able to get the N first elements of the upstream, not N elements randomly chosen. Even if your stream is not ordered, it still needs an internal counter that is shared among the different threads that are computing your stream in parallel — and this added synchronization can only slow down your computation, something you probably want to avoid. One last word: you need to be careful when you use parallel streams in general — a parallel stream will use all the cores of your CPU to conduct the operations, and may slow down your other processes. When it comes to performance, as usual: measure, don't guess.

IntStream.range(0, 10_000_000).parallel()
         .limit(100)            // stateful: a counter shared between threads
         .toArray();            // synchronization that only slows things down
341

How does dropWhile() work?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It acts like a door that is closed at first, and that opens on a predicate.

Less short answer

dropWhile is an intermediate method of the Stream API; it takes a predicate as a parameter, that is evaluated for the elements of the stream. While the predicate results in false, no element is pushed to the downstream; when the predicate results in true, then the door opens — meaning that all the remaining elements are pushed to the downstream, and the predicate is not evaluated anymore. If your stream is ordered, then dropWhile drops the first elements of your stream; but if your stream is not ordered, then this notion of "first element" is not defined — so the results may vary: the implementation can actually drop any subset of your source. One last word: dropWhile is a stateful operation — meaning that it does not play well with parallel streams, especially if your stream is ordered; actually, using dropWhile on an ordered parallel stream will probably slow down your operation — so it's probably not a good idea.

Stream.of(1, 3, 5, 6, 7, 2, 4)     // ordered
      .dropWhile(n -> n % 2 == 1)   // drops 1, 3, 5 and STOPS testing at 6
      .forEach(System.out::print);  // 6 7 2 4  (the "oddness" of 7 isn't even tested)
342

What is String deduplication?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A feature that may save some memory in your application.

Less short answer

String deduplication consists in detecting if two strings of characters have the same value in your application — and then creating only one instance of their internal arrays. We can do that because strings are immutable: once you have created a string, you cannot change its value. This deduplication feature was added to the G1 garbage collector back in the days of Java 8 — and deduplication actually operates on the byte array of the string, not on the String object itself. And because there is some CPU time involved, deduplication is attempted only on strings that have reached a certain age. One last word: ZGC also supports string deduplication, since Java 18 — you need to add the option -XX:+UseStringDeduplication to activate it.

java -XX:+UseStringDeduplication -XX:+UseG1GC MyApp
# equal strings now share the same internal byte[]
# (typical savings: heaps with many repeated strings)
343

What is the difference between a wrapper class and a value-based class?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

They are not the same thing.

Less short answer

A wrapper class is a value-based class — but not the contrary. A wrapper class is a class that wraps a primitive type (Integer for int...), and they have been in the JDK since the beginning — and that's all there is to it. A value-based class is a notion that is coming from Valhalla, and that appeared in the JDK. Technically speaking, it is an annotation added on some classes — wrapper classes have it, but also some classes from the Date and Time API, for instance, or Optional, and even some non-modifiable collections are annotated with value-based. This annotation triggers some warnings at compile time, if you do things with these classes that will fail once Valhalla is there — like using their instances for synchronization, for instance. One last word: this annotation is internal to the JDK — you can see it if you check the source code, but you cannot use it for your own classes.

Integer n = 42;                        // a wrapper AND value-based
Optional<String> o = Optional.empty(); // value-based
LocalDate d = LocalDate.now();         // value-based

synchronized (n) { ... }   // warning: will break with Valhalla (value types)
if (n == anotherN) { ... } // warning: identity is not reliable
344

How does the Collectors.teeing() work?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The teeing collector is a rather complex collector, added in JDK 12.

Less short answer

It consists in collecting your stream with two different collectors, that will produce two different results — and then merging these results with a merging function. These two collectors are the first two parameters of teeing(), and you need to know your Collector API pretty well to write them. That being said, this collector is very useful when you need to apply two different algorithms on the same stream without going through it twice. One last word: avoiding the streaming of your data twice is especially useful when your streaming can only be done once — like your streaming an IO, for instance — and you need to avoid buffering everything in memory; in that case, the teeing collector may be extremely useful.

// min and max in a single pass (without traversing twice)
var minMax = Stream.of(3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6)
        .collect(Collectors.teeing(
                Collectors.minBy(Integer::compareTo),
                Collectors.maxBy(Integer::compareTo),
                Map::entry));               // merges the two results
minMax.getKey();   // Optional[1]
minMax.getValue(); // Optional[9]
345

How many objects are there in a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(straight to the point) None.

Less short answer

The stream is a pipeline that pulls objects from the source, and pushes them to the downstream — or terminal operation. So the rule is: there is no object in a stream. That being said, some streams are managing an internal mutable state, that may be storing the elements it consumes for processing — that's the case of the distinct operation, that stores the elements of the stream in a set, and the sorted operation, that needs to store them before sorting them. But mapping, filtering, flat mapping — all these regular operations do not store any object. One last word: this feature makes the Stream API very powerful: the stream can consume a reasonable amount of memory, while staying able to process very large amounts of data — much larger than what you could store in the memory of your application.

Files.lines(path)                    // GBs of data...
     .filter(l -> l.contains("ERROR"))
     .map(String::trim)
     .limit(10)
     .forEach(log::error);           // ...with a tiny memory footprint
346

How can you create a pre-filled list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

You can just call List.of(...) and pass the elements you need in that list. Just be aware that there are restrictions on the implementation you get. First, you cannot have null values in this list — why would you put null values in this list? And then, the list you get is non-modifiable. The implementations you get are specific to these factory methods, and optimized in several ways, so that the operations on these lists are fast — and by the way, the implementations you get are also serializable. One last word: this List.of factory method was added in JDK 9, as part of the convenience factory methods for collections feature — so there is an equivalent method for Set, and — even if it's not mentioned in the name of the feature — also for Map.

List<String> colors = List.of("blue", "green", "red");
Set<Integer>  evens = Set.of(2, 4, 6);
Map<String, Integer> stock = Map.of("pen", 12, "pencil", 30);
// colors.add("yellow");  -> UnsupportedOperationException
// nulls are rejected at creation time
347

What is hashcode collision?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something that can happen when two different objects have the same hashcode.

Less short answer

From a technical point of view, a hashcode is an int encoded in 32 bits — and you can have way more different objects than 2³², so there has to be collisions. By the way, that's the reason why the specification says that two objects that are equal need to have the same hashcode — but the opposite is not true; in fact, it cannot be true. Having hash collisions can have unexpected effects on your application, including bad ones: for instance, if you store your objects in a set, they are actually added to an internal map, where the keys are the hashcodes of these objects; in case of many collisions, calling contains, for instance, may not be an O(1) operation anymore — but an O(log n) (after the tree transformation). Not that bad, but still not as fast. One last word: one more reason to avoid parallel streams on sets — if you have too many collisions, your set may not be split properly, and going parallel may hurt your performance instead of improving it.

record Point(int x, int y) { }         // a 32-bits hashcode
// a collision: two different points, same hash -> the same bucket
new Point(1, 2).hashCode() == new Point(2, 1).hashCode(); // possible
// in a HashSet: the bucket becomes a list -> a tree if they pile up (>8)
348

What is dependency inversion?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

"Inversion" means that the dependency between two modules, for instance, at compile time, is the opposite of the dependency at runtime.

Less short answer

You may be wondering: how is this even possible? Well, it's actually very simple — and very common too. If you have a module A that uses some classes of a module B, then A depends on B at compile time, and also at runtime. If you want to make B depend on A at compile time, then what you need to do is: create an interface in A, with what A needs from B — this interface can use records to transport the data from B to A — and then B needs to implement this interface. Now, B depends on A. This is exactly how the JDBC API works, for instance: most of what it contains are interfaces, implemented by drivers that are specific to the database you're using. One last word: the nice thing with this approach is that, when you need to update B for whatever reason, there is no need to recompile A — why? Because A does not even know B. One less pain point in your application.

// Module A: defines the interface (and the transport records)
interface OrderRepository { void save(OrderRecord o); }

// Module B: implements A's interface (the inverted arrow)
class PostgresOrderRepository implements OrderRepository { ... }
// swapping/recompiling B does not affect A
349

What is the first thing a constructor does?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It calls another constructor.

Less short answer

You can issue this call manually — which is handy if you need to call a specific constructor — either from the same class with this(...), or from the superclass with super(...). And if you do not write this code yourself, then a call to super() — the no-argument super constructor — is added for you by the compiler; the reason why, if your superclass does not have a no-arg constructor, you need to call one by hand, or suffer a compiler error. And if you don't write any constructor at all, then the compiler adds one for you, that does not take any argument, and that just calls super. One last word: you cannot prevent the execution of the superclass constructors — and it can be an overhead if, in the end, you decide not to create this object, because the arguments you got were not valid... well, until now: this changes in JDK 25 — you can now call super whenever you want (JEP 513), validating your arguments before. (Another video.)

class Base { Base() { ... } Base(String cfg) { ... } }

class Child extends Base {
    Child() {
        this("default");      // calls a constructor of the SAME class
    }
    Child(String cfg) {
        super(cfg);           // calls a constructor of the SUPERCLASS
    }                         // (unwritten, the compiler inserts super())
}
