What is the JavaDoc?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A very handy tool to write and render the documentation of your code — because you always write some documentation for your code, don't you?

Less short answer

From the beginning, Java thought about documenting all the elements you can write — classes, interfaces, and the like. You can write your JavaDoc in special comments (/** ... */), and use special tags to document what a method is doing, what the parameters it takes are and what their uses are, or what exceptions it may throw. You can add some JavaDoc on classes, methods, fields — but also on packages, using a special file called package-info.java — very handy. All the JDK JavaDoc is written in that way — you can check it out for examples. One last word: back in the days, it was written in HTML — and it was great, because you could read it from a browser; now you can also write it in Markdown — that's a feature from JDK 23. (Another video.)

/**
 * Converts currencies.
 * @param amount  the amount to convert
 * @param currency  the target currency
 * @return the converted amount
 * @throws IllegalArgumentException if the currency is not supported
 */
public BigDecimal convert(BigDecimal amount, String currency) { ... }
351

How can you run a parallel stream in a specific pool of threads?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that — a little hidden.

Less short answer

As you know, a parallel stream runs in a common ForkJoin pool by default — there is only one such pool in your application, and the number of threads is set to the number of cores you have on your CPU. There may be situations where you need to decrease this number of threads, because you want to keep some CPU cycles for other things than running your parallel streams — you can do that with the JVM option java.util.concurrent.ForkJoinPool.common.parallelism: if you set this property to 3, it will create 3 threads in the common ForkJoin pool of your application. But you can do better: if you run a stream in a thread that is part of a ForkJoin pool, and this stream is a parallel stream, then it will run in this ForkJoin pool — and not the common ForkJoin pool. So the trick is to create a Callable that will carry your parallel stream computation, and submit this callable to a ForkJoin pool that you created yourself — and that's it. One last word: is it a good idea? Well, you need to think about it very carefully — because having a gazillion ForkJoin pools in your application, hammering your CPU like crazy, is most certainly a very, very bad idea. When it comes to performance: measure, don't guess.

// a global tuning of the common pool
System.setProperty("java.util.concurrent.ForkJoinPool.common.parallelism", "3");

// the pattern: run the parallel stream inside your own pool
ForkJoinPool pool = new ForkJoinPool(2);
var result = pool.submit(() ->
        list.parallelStream()
            .map(this::transform)
            .collect(Collectors.toList())
).get();
352

What is the difference between a ByteBuffer and a MemorySegment?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Oh, not the same thing.

Less short answer

ByteBuffer is a class from Java 4, designed in 2002, that gives you access to the off-heap memory of your application; MemorySegment is an interface from Java 22, from 2024 — that kind of does the same thing. ByteBuffer has 32-bits indexes; a MemorySegment has 64-bits indexes. You cannot free a ByteBuffer yourself — which may lead to OutOfMemoryExceptions, without you being able to do anything about it; a MemorySegment is obtained from an Arena — that is auto-closable — so you can free them on demand. With MemorySegment, you can control how different threads access your memory segments — you can even prevent any other thread than yours to access them (a confined arena); with ByteBuffer, when it comes to concurrency, you're on your own. One last word — in case it has not been explicit enough: using ByteBuffers in any place other than legacy code is really not a good idea; learn how to use arenas, memory segments, and memory layouts — it will be much better for your application.

// Legacy (Java 4): no explicit release, int indexes
ByteBuffer buf = ByteBuffer.allocateDirect(1_000_000);

// Modern (Java 22): a closable arena, long indexes, controlled access
try (Arena arena = Arena.ofConfined()) {
    MemorySegment seg = arena.allocate(1_000_000);
    seg.set(ValueLayout.JAVA_LONG, 0, 42L); // deterministic release at close
}
353

What is a ScopedValue?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A value that you can pass around in your application, without relying on method arguments.

Less short answer

Passing elements from one method to another method, without using method parameters, is something you need when you write callbacks. ThreadLocal variables were created exactly for that — to pass elements around (and you can also relate to the other one, that was in Java 2, in 1998: the InheritableThreadLocal). ThreadLocal variables have several issues: they are mutable; they are bound to a thread; you need to call remove() on them when you're done with them — and if you don't, the next task this thread will execute will have access to it — something you want to avoid. ScopedValues are a replacement, that is bound to a task — namely, a Runnable or a Callable — and not bound to any thread; you don't need to remove them, because they are not transmitted by the thread — and they cannot escape your method call, unless they were created by a StructuredTaskScope. One last word: the API does not give you thread safety — so if you share a mutable scoped value among different threads, then you need to manage the synchronization yourself; sticking to non-modifiable scoped values is probably your best choice.

private static final ScopedValue<User> USER = ScopedValue.newInstance();

ScopedValue.where(USER, authenticatedUser)
           .run(() -> processRequest());

// inside any callback in the scope:
User u = USER.get();   // no ThreadLocal, no remove()
354

How can you explore a tree of directories?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that. (actually, two)

Less short answer

You can use the Files.list(dir) method to give you access to all the entries of a directory — you can then filter them with a regular expression, and when you find a directory, recursively explore this directory; but using recursion is always a little dangerous — you can get StackOverflowExceptions while doing that, something you usually want to avoid. So there is another method, Files.walkFileTree(dir, visitor), that takes a file visitor as a parameter — it implements the real Visitor pattern: you can give a callback for every entry that is found; it visits the content of a directory, and when it finds a subdirectory, you can tell this method to explore this subdirectory or not. One last word: you need to keep in mind that a file system can be modified while you explore the content of a directory — so you can come across weird errors if it takes too long, competing with other processes accessing your file system; that's another level of concurrency issues.

// 1) manual recursion (careful with StackOverflow)
try (Stream<Path> s = Files.list(dir)) {
    s.filter(Files::isDirectory).forEach(this::explore);
}

// 2) a visitor: fine control, without your own recursion
Files.walkFileTree(Path.of("."), new SimpleFileVisitor<>() {
    @Override public FileVisitResult visitFile(Path f, BasicFileAttributes a) {
        System.out.println(f);
        return FileVisitResult.CONTINUE;
    }
});
355

What is an EnumMap?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A map.

Less short answer

An EnumMap is a map where the keys are the values of a single enum — that allows for a very efficient implementation, both memory-wise and CPU-wise. Since the enumerated values are known before the map is created, and these values have an index — the ordinal — you can simply store the values in an array, which size is known upfront (you will never need to resize this array once it has been created), and using this index makes it so that you do not need to compute any hash code. One last word: this map does not support null keys — why would you put a null key in an array? But it does support null values: trying to add a null key will throw a NullPointerException, but you can still check for the presence, or try to remove a null key, and get false as a result.

enum Season { SUMMER, FALL, WINTER, SPRING }

Map<Season, Double> temp = new EnumMap<>(Season.class);
temp.put(Season.SUMMER, 32.5);
temp.get(Season.SUMMER);       // direct array access, no hashing
temp.containsKey(null);        // false (no exception)
// temp.put(null, 10.0);       // NullPointerException

How can you use markdown in your JavaDoc?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a pattern for that.

Less short answer

Writing JavaDoc may not be your thing — but it is still a very interesting feature of the Java platform. In 1995, people chose to use HTML for the JavaDoc — and it was probably an excellent choice at the time; and somehow, that's still the case: you can easily read your JavaDoc in the browser. But 1995 — that was a long time ago; and nowadays, writing Markdown is probably easier to do, and as efficient. To write some Markdown code in your JavaDoc, all you need to do is add this triple slash /// at the beginning of each line. Several Markdown features are supported, including titling and links — which is great. One last word: you can also include snippets of code in your JavaDoc, so that the examples you show are tested during the building process of your application — another great addition to this old feature. (Another video.)

/// Converts a value to a currency.
///
/// ## Example
/// ```java
/// Money m = Money.of("12,34");
/// ```
///
/// [See more](https://docs.example.com)
public class Money { ... }
357

How should you check if a value is null?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Use Objects.requireNonNull().

Less short answer

We can always write a test with double equal — that will work, of course. But a better pattern is to call Objects.requireNonNull(): it has three advantages. First, this simple factory method throws the NullPointerException for you, explicitly. Second, it can return the object you pass — so you can inline this null check with the use of the variable you want to check (assigning it directly to a field, for instance). And third, it can take a custom error message, if you want to give context or information to debug the problem. One last word: there is also an overload of this method that can return a default value instead of throwing the NullPointerException — you can pass this value as an argument, or — if it's expensive to create — pass a Supplier to build it (requireNonNullElseGet).

this.name = Objects.requireNonNull(name, "name cannot be null");
// throws an NPE with a clear message, and assigns

Config cfg = Objects.requireNonNullElse(currentCfg, Config.DEFAULT);
Config costly = Objects.requireNonNullElseGet(currentCfg, () -> loadFromDisk());
358

Is Collections.unmodifiableList() non-modifiable?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(the video answers it in practice) It should be an obvious yes — and in a way, it is.

Less short answer

Collections.unmodifiableList() returns a list that you cannot modify — calling add, remove, clear, and all these kinds of things. But this non-modifiable list is actually a wrapper on the list you pass as an argument: no defensive copy is made. So if you keep a reference to this list, and modify it, you will see these modifications in the unmodifiable list. One last word: whatever you do, if you need an unmodifiable list built from a regular list, you need to make a defensive copy — unmodifiableList is a very good first choice, but you can also use one of the List.of(...) / List.copyOf(...) patterns.

List<String> original = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b"));
List<String> readOnly = Collections.unmodifiableList(original);
original.add("c");            // the "unmodifiable" list sees the change!
List.copyOf(original);        // a defensive copy: truly immutable
359

How can you use regular expressions?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are several classes for that.

Less short answer

All these classes are in the java.util.regex package. First, you need to create a Pattern with the Pattern class, using the compile() factory method — immutable and reusable. You can use this pattern in two ways. First, you can split a text lazily with the splitAsStream() method, passing the text: what you get is a stream, that you can analyze with the traditional stream patterns. Second, you can call matcher(), and pass a text to match against this pattern: what you get is a Matcher object; you can then call Matcher.find() and then Matcher.group(), to jump from one matching subsequence to the other; calling Matcher.start() and Matcher.end() gives you the position of this subsequence in the text; and you can also call replaceAll() on this Matcher, to replace the matching subsequences with what you need. One last word: the regular expression engine also supports groups and named groups, with methods to export them directly — but that will be for another video.

Pattern p = Pattern.compile("\\w+@(\\w+)\\.com");

// 1) a lazy split into a stream
p.splitAsStream("a,b,,c").forEach(System.out::println);

// 2) the classic matcher
Matcher m = p.matcher("contact: john@acme.com, ana@zupper.com");
while (m.find()) {
    System.out.println(m.group() + " domain=" + m.group(1)
            + " pos=" + m.start() + "-" + m.end());
}
360

How can you gather statistics on a stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a collector for that.

Less short answer

There are actually three, depending on the type of statistics you need — you can get them with the summarizing factory methods from the Collectors class: one for integers, one for longs, and one for doubles. They return a summary statistics object — one for each type — that computes the count, the sum, the min, the max and the average, in one pass over your data. One last word: these summary statistics objects are in fact consumers — all the statistics are computed in the accept method of these consumers; so if you need other statistics than these, copying this pattern is very easy — you just need to create your own summary statistics object, and copy the pattern from one of the factory methods; and it even supports parallelism for free.

IntSummaryStatistics st = Stream.of(2, 7, 1, 9, 4)
        .collect(Collectors.summarizingInt(Integer::intValue));
st.getCount();   // 5
st.getSum();     // 23
st.getMin();     // 1
st.getMax();     // 9
st.getAverage(); // 4.6
361

Should you comment your code?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

No.

Less short answer

If you need comments to explain your code, it means that it is not readable enough — so instead of writing comments, you should work on the readability of your code; that should be your first reflex — and it's much harder to do. Comments are not documentation: documentation is written using the JavaDoc — and JavaDoc is of paramount importance: it should be precise, accurate; it should give you examples of how to use your code, and explain what happens if something goes wrong; your JavaDoc should be proofread during your code reviews, to make sure it has the right level of quality. Comments, on the other hand, live their own life — and your code also lives its own life; but both are not always related: sometimes you fix bugs in your code, or change what it's doing, leaving the comments as they are — and at some point, what the comments tell you about your code is no longer related to what your code is actually doing; so no one reads them anymore, and they just stay there, rotting, polluting your code base. One last word: there are exceptions to this rule — for instance, when you need to give some detail on a complex algorithm your code implements; you can check the ConcurrentHashMap or the ConcurrentSkipListMap classes.

// bad: a comment explaining what the code already says
i = i + 1; // increments i

// good: a readable name; a comment only for the non-obvious WHY
int attemptsLeft = limit - attempts;
// exponential backoff: a requirement of the partner's SLA (contract §4.2)
362

How can you know what thread is running your code?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

The method you need to call is a factory method from the Thread class, called Thread.currentThread(): it gives you a reference on the thread that is running your code. There are several cases where you need to call this method, apart from debugging or analyzing purposes: if you execute a long-running task, and you need to manage the interruption of this task yourself, then you need to monitor the interrupted status of the thread that is executing your task — you can do that by calling Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted(); if this method returns true, then you need to stop your task. One last word: currentThread returns the platform thread, or the virtual thread that is running your task — and in that case, you don't have access to the underlying platform thread.

while (hasWork()) {
    if (Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted()) {  // cooperative cancellation
        cleanup(); return;
    }
    processNext();
}
Thread.currentThread().isVirtual(); // true in a virtual thread
363

How can you generate the JavaDoc?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The JavaDoc tool is a tool that you have in the standard JDK distribution — and most of the time, your IDE uses it for you.

Less short answer

JavaDoc can analyze all your class files, and will take the so-called JavaDoc comments (/** ... */) to generate a set of HTML pages that you can distribute along with your application. And, as all the tools available in your JDK, JavaDoc is also available as a service, that you can use programmatically — you need to check the ToolProvider interface.

javadoc -d docs -sourcepath src/main/java -subpackages com.acme

// calling javadoc programmatically
DocumentationTool tool = ToolProvider.getSystemDocumentationTool();
364

What is the Template Method?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A pattern from the Gang of Four.

Less short answer

The template method pattern is very simple: it is about creating an abstract class with an abstract method — this abstract method is the template method itself; then you need to extend your abstract class, and provide a specific implementation for the template — that is used at runtime by all the other methods of your abstract class. Both ArrayList and LinkedList apply this pattern: they extend AbstractList and AbstractCollection. AbstractCollection has two abstract methods — size() and iterator() — and one method that throws an UnsupportedOperationException: add(element). So implementing your own collection is very simple: you just need to extend AbstractCollection, implement size and iterator — and override add if what you want to make is a mutable implementation. AbstractList extends AbstractCollection, and adds one abstract method — get(index) — and three methods that throw an UnsupportedOperationException: add at index, remove at index, and set at index. Same thing: implementing a list is super simple — just extend AbstractList: you have one method to implement, and up to four to override if you need to make it a mutable implementation. One last word: the same goes for maps — there is one class to extend, AbstractMap, with one abstract method (entrySet), and put(key, value) to override if you need a mutable implementation.

class MyList extends AbstractList<String> {
    private final String[] data = {"a", "b", "c"};
    @Override public String get(int i) { return data[i]; }
    @Override public int size() { return data.length; }
    // the inherited add/set/remove throw UnsupportedOperationException
}
365

How does the calling of super() work?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It calls the super constructor of your class.

Less short answer

Calling another constructor — this is the first thing any constructor does; it can be a constructor from the same class, or a constructor from a superclass. This pattern is actually problematic: as you can access fields from the subclass in a constructor from a superclass — and these fields may not be initialized, even if they are final. So it means that during the construction process of an object, you can observe the transition of a final field from its initial null value, to its final value — which kind of defeats the point of being final. JEP 513 — "statements before super" — changed that: you can now execute some code before the call to the other constructor — meaning that you can, and you should, conduct any initialization of your object before calling the super constructor. There is a restriction on what you can do: you cannot call any instance method before calling this super constructor — eliminating the risk of executing subcode from a subclass. One last word: instead of letting the compiler call the super constructor from the constructor file for you, the good practice is: not to call super explicitly without having initialized your object first — one less place for bugs to hide.

class Base { Base(String config) { ... } }

class Child extends Base {
    private final Config cfg;
    Child(String raw) {
        // JDK 25 (JEP 513): initializing BEFORE super() is possible
        this.cfg = Config.parse(raw);          // was a compile error before
        super(cfg.validatedName());            // super gets ready data
    }
}
366

What is the difference between an ExecutorService and a Fork/Join pool?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A Fork/Join pool is an ExecutorService.

Less short answer

ExecutorService is an interface. The first interface is the Executor interface: it defines the submission of a Runnable; and the ExecutorService is an interface that extends Executor. It supports Callables — returning a Future on a submission of a Callable — and defines two shutdown strategies. Usually, the implementation of the ExecutorService has one waitlist, in which they store the tasks you submit, and a pool of threads: each thread takes a task from the waitlist, and executes it. A ForkJoinPool has one waitlist per thread; and each task is expected to split itself, spawning at least two other tasks that are stored in the same waitlist; then it implements the work-stealing pattern — meaning that if a thread has an empty waitlist, it can steal a task from another waitlist. This has a cost — when it comes to visibility issues and cache misses — but it's necessary to keep all your threads busy. One last word: there are two ForkJoinPools running in your JVM: one common pool, that runs your parallel streams — and another one, that runs your virtual threads; and they don't work exactly in the same way. (Another video.)

ExecutorService ex = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(4); // 1 shared queue
ForkJoinPool fj = (ForkJoinPool) Executors.newWorkStealingPool(4);
// a queue/deque per thread + work stealing -> divide and conquer
367

How does takeWhile() work?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It acts like a door that closes on a predicate.

Less short answer

takeWhile is an intermediate method of the Stream interface, that takes a predicate as a parameter. If your stream is not parallel, then it will evaluate the predicate for all the elements, one by one: as long as the predicate evaluates to true, the elements are pushed to the downstream — but when it becomes false, then the stream is interrupted. If your stream is not ordered, and possibly parallel, then takeWhile may push any subset of elements that match the predicate downstream, and interrupt the stream when it finds an element that does not. If your stream is ordered, then this subset is the longest sequence of matching elements, starting at the front — the first element of your stream. One last word: in the case of an ordered parallel stream, takeWhile has to manage an internal counter, shared among the different threads running your stream — and it will hurt your performance; one more reason to think twice before using parallel streams.

Stream.of(2, 4, 6, 7, 8, 10)          // ordered
      .takeWhile(n -> n % 2 == 0)     // stops at 7
      .forEach(System.out::print);    // 2 4 6   (8 and 10 are never evaluated)
368

What is padding?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Padding is about adding unused bytes in memory.

Less short answer

It has to do with memory alignment. Some CPUs are connected to their memory in a way that makes accessing some words — like 32-bits words or 64-bits words — faster if they are stored at addresses divisible by 4, or by 8. The JVM knows that — and to optimize the read and write performance, it adds bytes when needed, to make sure that all your data is properly aligned in memory. It can also optimize the layout of your objects in memory, to make sure that padding is kept minimal. One last word: when you need to read a file that is not using any padding — compact binary formats — you may come across alignment issues; the reason why you can specifically read unaligned data with the Memory API, from JDK 22. (Another video.)

Object with fields long x; int y; byte z;  (8-byte alignment)
| header (8) | x (8) | y (4) | z (1) | padding (3) |
                                   ^--- 3 bytes of padding to close the 8
369

How can you compare objects for equality?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Your best choice is to use the Objects.equals() utility method.

Less short answer

Of course, the most obvious choice would be to call a.equals(b) — but it can fail if a is null. So if you want your code to be robust, you first need to check if a is null — and then, if b is also null, you get an option: well, then you may want to return true... And suddenly, something that was supposed to be super simple becomes a mess in your application. JDK 7 added this small utility class, Objects, that has an equals method that does all this for you; it's not a revolution — it's just so much simpler. One last word: Objects also has a hashCode(Object) method, that takes an object — and if this object is null, it returns 0. Simple and useful.

a.equals(b);                  // NPE if a == null
Objects.equals(a, b);         // null-safe: true only if both null, or equals
Objects.hashCode(null);       // 0, no NPE
Objects.hash(name, age);      // a hashCode from several fields
370

What are the SOLID principles?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A set of principles for object-oriented programming, that you should know and follow.

Less short answer

The video attributes the acronym to Kent Beck (author of several books on TDD and extreme programming) — historically, it was coined by Michael Feathers, and Robert Martin popularized it. S is for the Single Responsibility principle: one reason to change your class or your method — one stakeholder; two is already too much. O is the Open/Closed principle: open for extension, and closed for modification — you can implement it with composition, which makes it even more powerful. The L principle is the Liskov Substitution principle, named after the scientist Barbara Liskov: it defines what inheritance is — if an instance of B behaves the same as an instance of A, to a point that you cannot tell which one is which, then B extends A. I is for Interface Segregation: in a nutshell, an interface should not have methods that you don't use. And D is for Dependency Inversion: if a module A depends on a module B at runtime, then B should depend on A at compile time — the two dependency arrows are opposite; this is what "inversion" means. One last word: all this may sound like old stuff — and it is: these principles have been around for more than 25 years — but applying them will help you make your legacy code much easier to manage.

// D: OrderService depends on the abstraction; the implementation is injected
class OrderService {
    private final OrderRepository repo;    // the abstraction
    OrderService(OrderRepository repo) { this.repo = repo; } // the inverted arrow
}
371

What is a megamorphic call?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A method call that the JVM has a hard time optimizing.

Less short answer

You write your code against interfaces, right? Like when you need a list, you make it a List, and not an ArrayList. So it means that at runtime, the JVM needs to find the implementation you're calling — because it's not in the bytecode. This is called a virtual call, and it happens every time you call a method that can be overridden somewhere. But then, if the JVM realizes that, at runtime, a particular virtual call always calls the same implementation, then it can optimize things, and make it like a non-virtual call — a regular call, which will be much faster. A megamorphic call is a call that cannot be optimized in that way, because you have too many overriding versions of the method you're calling — and the JVM needs to check which one you are calling. And the bad news is that "too many" starts at three: a megamorphic call is a virtual call with at least three possible implementations. One last word: with sealed types and pattern matching, you can eliminate all the virtual calls of your application. Will it be better performance-wise? Well, it is something worth checking — when it comes to performance: measure, don't guess.

List<String> list = ...;
list.size(); // a virtual call: ArrayList? LinkedList? ...
// monomorphic (1 impl) -> the JIT inlines
// bimorphic  (2 impls) -> the JIT still inlines
// megamorphic (3+ impls) -> virtual dispatch, no inlining

// sealed + switch: static dispatch, no virtual call
sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square {}
double area(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {
        case Circle c  -> Math.PI * c.radius() * c.radius();
        case Square q -> q.side() * q.side();
    };
}
372

How can you read and write unaligned data?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a button for that. (the presenter jokes: the ready-made option exists.)

Less short answer

You need to use the Memory API for that, from the Panama Project. First you create an Arena — and then, create a memory segment from this arena; this memory segment can represent a portion of memory from the off-heap memory of the application, or an array that lives on the heap. You can use the set method to write some data to this memory segment: it takes a memory layout, an offset — and, of course, a value. The memory layout and the offset you pass need to be compatible: if you need to write some unaligned data, then you need to pass an unaligned memory layout (like JAVA_INT_UNALIGNED); if you don't, and you try to write at an offset that is not aligned, then you will get an IllegalArgumentException. One last word: you need to keep in mind that there is another overhead when you read and write unaligned data — so you should use this feature only if you absolutely need it.

try (Arena arena = Arena.ofConfined()) {
    MemorySegment seg = arena.allocate(100);
    // a compact file header: an int field at offset 3 (unaligned!)
    seg.set(ValueLayout.JAVA_INT_UNALIGNED, 3, 42);
    int value = seg.get(ValueLayout.JAVA_INT_UNALIGNED, 3);
    // with JAVA_INT (aligned) at offset 3 -> IllegalArgumentException
}
373

How much memory does an object consume?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It depends.

Less short answer

It really depends — it is even dependent on the machine that is running your application. Each object has a header that is made of one 32-bits word and one 64-bits word on 32-bits machines — or two 64-bits words on 64-bits machines, with your application living in a heap of up to 32 GB (thanks to the compressed oops; above that, more). Then there is a second overhead, which is due to alignment: for instance, an Integer in an application that has a 64-GB heap on a 64-bits machine takes two 64-bits words — the header — then the integer itself, 32 bits, and then 32 more bits of padding to preserve the alignment. That's 6 32-bits words, to carry 1 32-bits word of information. One last word: there are two ways of optimizing that: the first one is the Lilliput project, which consists in drastically reducing the size of the header — and the second one is the Valhalla project, that removes the header completely for the value objects.

An Integer on a 64-bits JVM (heap > 32 GB):
+---------------------------+-----------+-----------+
| header (2 x 64 bits)      | int 32 b  | padding   |
|      128 bits             |  32 bits  |  32 bits  |
+---------------------------+-----------+-----------+
= 24 bytes to carry 4 bytes of data
374

How can you add a snippet of code in your JavaDoc?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are actually several — and one of them is much better.

Less short answer

The first one consists in adding this snippet directly in your JavaDoc comment with {@snippet ...}: it's nice, it works well — but you cannot validate this snippet at runtime, like it was a unit test. So there is a better way, which consists in including a region of a class: you reference that class with the class tag, and then the region you want to include in your JavaDoc. This class needs to live in the snippet-files directory of the package where the class that references the snippet lives. On the one hand, it's convenient — because your IDE can read this class, and render your JavaDoc on the fly; but on the other hand, Maven will have a hard time running all these classes as unit tests — which you can solve with the --snippet-path option, when you run the JavaDoc tool. One last word: all this also works if you write your JavaDoc in Markdown (/// comments).

/**
 * Usage example:
 * {@snippet class="MoneyExamples" region="rounding"}
 */
public class Money { ... }
// in src/main/java/.../snippet-files/MoneyExamples.java
public class MoneyExamples {
    // @start region="rounding"
    Money m = value.setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP);
    // @end region
}
375

What is an EnumSet?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(the video goes straight to the explanation)

Less short answer

An enum set is a set that can hold some values of a single enum. The implementation stores them in a bit vector — which is very efficient, both memory-wise and CPU-wise. There are several factory methods that you can use to create an enum set: allOf(Weekday.class) puts all the enum values in a set; noneOf() gives you an empty enum set for the right type — then you can choose the values you want to add, one by one; and then you have this complementOf(otherSet) method, that takes another set, and that gives you the complement of this other set. One last word: an enum set is modifiable, but you cannot add null elements to it — which is expected, since it can only contain values from enums, and enums cannot have null values. (And why would you add null elements to a set, anyway?)

enum Day { MON, TUE, WED, THU, FRI, SAT, SUN }

Set<Day> weekdays = EnumSet.range(Day.MON, Day.FRI);
Set<Day> all = EnumSet.allOf(Day.class);
Set<Day> weekend = EnumSet.complementOf(weekdays); // [SAT, SUN]
376

What is context switching?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something you want to avoid.

Less short answer

It has to do with concurrency — doing too many context switches in an application is something that will hurt your performance badly; the reason why you need to avoid that. When a thread is running on one of the cores of your CPU, it is there with all the information it needs to run the code — the data, and some other stuff; all this is stored in a cache and in the registers of this core: this is what the context of this thread is. At some point, the operating system may decide to pause this thread, and give the hand to another one — so all the data this thread uses is removed from the core, to be brought back at a later time. Doing this takes in the order of 100 microseconds — which is quite a lot. It will happen, for instance, if this thread is executing some network call, and is waiting for the data to be there, doing nothing — the reason why you should never do that with kernel threads. One last word: using virtual threads helps you preventing context switching from happening, since blocking a virtual thread does not block the kernel thread that is running it — one less bug to take care of in your application.

// a kernel thread blocked on IO = an expensive context switch (~100 µs)
var response = client.send(request); // the OS thread just sits there

// with virtual threads, blocking is cheap — the carrier is released
Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> process(client.send(request)));
377

What is the subsized stream?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A good candidate to be executed in parallel.

Less short answer

Sized means that you know how many elements this stream will process — for instance, a stream opened on any collection is sized, because you just need to call size() on the collection to get this information. Subsized means that if you split this stream in two sub-streams — well, if you split the stream — you still know how many elements each sub-stream will process. Not all collections give you subsized streams: lists do — if you split a list in two, you know how many elements you have in each sub-list; but sets do not — splitting a set consists in splitting its internal array, and you do not know how many elements you have in the two sub-arrays you get: if you are unlucky, you may have zero elements in the first one, and all the elements in the second one. One last word: splitting a source is what parallel streams do — so do not use parallel streams with a source of data that is not easily splittable; and even if it is — think twice before using parallel streams: it may hurt your application instead of improving it.

// SIZED + SUBSIZED: splitting a list preserves the notion of size
List<Integer> list = List.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6);
// HashSet: splitting the internal array guarantees no balance
Spliterator<Integer> it = new HashSet<>(list).spliterator(); // not SUBSIZED
378

How can you get the number of characters in a string?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

You probably think that's an easy answer — well, it's harder than what you think.

Less short answer

It depends on what you call a character. You have a length method on the String class — and if you check the JavaDoc carefully, you will see that it returns the number of Unicode code units in this string class: a code unit is used for 16-bits chars. But some Unicode characters may be encoded on two code units — and even more than that. So if your application is playing with characters encoded on two chars, then length does not return the number of characters of your string — and you may end up with a string that displays only one character on your screen, but with a length of two, or more. One last word: there are two notions: the code unit — which is a char — and the code point, which is an int; and not all values of this int are valid. That will be for another time.

String plane = "✈";                  // a single visible character
plane.length();                      // 2  (a surrogate pair = 2 code units)
plane.codePointCount(0, plane.length()); // 1  (the real code points)
379

How can you limit the number of concurrent accesses to a resource?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

That's the job of the semaphore.

Less short answer

There is indeed a Semaphore class in the JDK that you can use for that — and this is the nice way of doing it, because your code is then saying what it does: "I want to limit the number of requests on this server". A wrong way of doing it would be to use a specific Executor Service for that, and to fix the number of threads to your limit — it will also work, but it's a hidden way to achieve the same result, and it's dangerous for your application. But there is yet a third way, which consists in using a gatherer: streaming your requests, calling Gatherers.mapConcurrent(maxConcurrency, mapper) — each mapping is executed in a virtual thread (perfect for IO requests), and the number of active mappings at a given time is limited by this max-concurrency integer: a "free semaphore" doing the job for you. One last word: do not use this pattern in a parallel stream — it will be a complete disaster; stick to a normal stream — your code will be simpler, and will work as intended.

Semaphore semaphore = new Semaphore(10); // at most 10 concurrent
semaphore.acquire();
try { serveRequest(); } finally { semaphore.release(); }

// the modern alternative (JDK 22+): a gatherer with bounded concurrency
var results = requests.stream()
        .gather(Gatherers.mapConcurrent(10, this::serve))
        .toList();
380

How can you search for an element in a list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a very good pattern for that.

Less short answer

Of course, you can call list.contains() — which will tell you if the object you pass is present or not — or indexOf(), which will give you the first index of this object. These methods are slow, because they scan all the elements of your list, one after the other — so if you have many elements in your list... If what you have is a list that was previously sorted, then you can call Collections.binarySearch(): it works if your objects are Comparable, or it can take a comparator as an argument — the implementation uses a binary search algorithm, that has a log-n complexity. Two caveats: first, if your list is not sorted, it will not throw any exception, and will return something — but it may take some time; and second, if the object you are looking for is present several times in the list, then you will get any one of the valid indexes. If the object is not in the list, then you will get minus insertion minus one — where "insertion" is the index at which this object would have been inserted, without breaking the sorted order; this number is negative, to denote that the object was not found. One last word: only use this pattern on a list — because this log-n complexity is achieved only if you can access any element in constant time.

List<Integer> sorted = List.of(2, 5, 8, 12, 16, 23, 38);
int idx = Collections.binarySearch(sorted, 12);     // 3
int notFound = Collections.binarySearch(sorted, 7); // -3 (it would be inserted at index 2)
381

How can you compare arrays for equality?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

(no short answer in the video — straight to the method.)

Less short answer

When you think about it, it can be a little more complex than what you may think at first. As you know, an array itself is an object in Java — so it has an equals method; but this equals method compares the two references of the array: in that sense, an array can only be equal to itself. Most of the time, what you need is to compare the contents of your arrays — saying two arrays are equal if the elements they contain are equal, and in the same order; so what you need in that case is one of the Arrays.equals() methods: they take two arrays, and can also take indexes, to compare only a portion of the first array and of the second array. And if you think you're done — well, you're not: because an array can itself contain sub-arrays; and if you want to compare them with the same Arrays.equals method, then you need to call another method, which is Arrays.deepEquals(). So for simple arrays, you call Arrays.equals — and for multi-dimensional arrays, you call Arrays.deepEquals. One last word: all these methods support null values in your array — two null values are considered equal — and will not fail in case of nulls... but seriously: don't put null values in your arrays.

int[] a = {1, 2, 3}, b = {1, 2, 3};
a.equals(b);               // false! (compares references)
Arrays.equals(a, b);       // true  (content)

int[][] m1 = {{1, 2}, {3}}, m2 = {{1, 2}, {3}};
Arrays.equals(m1, m2);     // false (sub-arrays are objects)
Arrays.deepEquals(m1, m2); // true
382

How can you get the max of a collection?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are patterns for that (more than one).

Less short answer

There are actually several patterns, with different pros and cons. If what you need is just to extract the max without transforming the elements of your collection, or without filtering them, then Collections.max() is a good choice — it's a factory method: you need to pass your collection, and pass a comparator if you need to. Note that if your collection has null elements, you will get a NullPointerException — and if your collection is empty, you will get a NoSuchElementException. The second pattern is of course based on streams: just call stream, then max, and pass your comparator — you will also get an NPE if there is a null value in your stream; but if your stream is empty, you will get an empty Optional — which is probably better when it comes to error handling. One last word: be careful with the stream pattern, because you still need to pay the price of the creation of the stream — another overhead you may want to avoid; so unless there are specific stream features that you need, using the good old Collections.max is probably your best choice.

var ints = List.of(1, null, 3, 4);
var max = Collections.max(ints);   // NullPointerException (a null in the list)

var empty = List.<Integer>of();
Collections.max(empty);                        // NoSuchElementException
empty.stream().max(Comparator.naturalOrder()); // Optional.empty()
383

What is JLink?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is a standard tool of the JDK.

Less short answer

The JDK gives you two tools that work together, to package and distribute your application as an executable, or as an installer. The first tool is called jlink — it is part of the JDK distribution, and it does exactly what it says: if your application is a modular application, it lists all the modules your application depends on — and it does that even for the modules of the JDK itself; so you get only the modules you need, stripping the ones you do not depend on. It also means that you need to be careful with the modules you depend on: depending on a several-megabytes module, when all you need is a single class or two, may not be the best idea. One last word: once you have that, you can use jpackage to create an installer, to distribute your application — it works on Windows, macOS, and various flavors of Linux; but that will be for another time.

jdeps --print-module-deps app.jar        # discovers the needed modules
jlink --add-modules java.base,java.net.http \
      --output my-runtime                 # a lean, customized runtime
384

How can you use the preview features?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are two options for that: one for the compiler, and another one for the JVM.

Less short answer

You need to activate preview features at two levels. The first one is the compiler level: you add --enable-preview — and then you need to specify either the version of the source you're using, with --source, followed by the version — which could be between 8 and the version of the JDK you're using — or the version of the bytecode you want to generate, with --target, followed by the version. And then you need to specify that you wish to run this code with the preview feature enabled, by adding the same option, --enable-preview, to the java command. So in a nutshell: you cannot use preview features by just using the Java code — you need to tell the compiler that you want to activate them, and at runtime you need to tell the JVM that you want to run them. One last word: this preview feature mechanism is useful, because you can activate them on demand, without having to download some separate elements of the JDK; so it's easy to check for them, to play with them — and to provide feedback on the OpenJDK mailing lists; if you feel something is missing or wrong, this is something you should definitely do.

javac --enable-preview --release 25 Main.java
java  --enable-preview Main
385

What is a group in a regular expression?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

Something very useful to analyze strings of characters.

Less short answer

Groups are actually a feature of regular expressions, specified outside of the JDK. A group is just a portion of your regular expression between parentheses, like in this example. With such a regular expression, you can analyze a string of characters, match it to get a match — and if you have a match, then get the different elements you need from it. The nice thing is that you can give names to your groups, to make your code more expressive: you can specify the name of this group in that way, in your regular expression — and then, if you have a match, you can get the values of the different groups by their names, which makes your code much more readable. One last word: remember that you have a pattern to analyze a long text with a regular expression in a lazy way — if what you're looking for is the first occurrence of something, then this pattern is the one you want to use.

Pattern p = Pattern.compile("(?<day>\\d{2})/(?<month>\\d{2})/(?<year>\\d{4})");
Matcher m = p.matcher("Reference: 12/25/2025");
if (m.find()) {
    System.out.println(m.group("day"));    // 12
    System.out.println(m.group("month"));  // 25
    System.out.println(m.group("year"));   // 2025
}
386

What is a canonical constructor?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It is the constructor that takes all the components of a record.

Less short answer

A canonical constructor has to do with records. When you declare a record, you do not need to write its constructor: you just declare the components of this record, and the compiler generates the constructor that takes these components for you — this constructor that takes all the components is called the canonical constructor. Records have been designed in such a way that you cannot create a record instance without calling its canonical constructor: even if you create more constructors for your record, then you need to call this canonical constructor — and even deserialization follows this specification. So if you have validation rules in a canonical constructor, you have the guarantee that they will be executed for all the instances of this record, without any exception. One last word: when you think about it, a record is the only kind of class that offers this guarantee — for regular classes, deserialization does not call any constructor, so it's not possible to validate your instances in that case.

record Range(int min, int max) {
    Range {                          // the compact form of the canonical ctor
        if (min > max) throw new IllegalArgumentException("min > max");
    }                                // the min/max assignment is automatic
}
// Range r = new Range(10, 5);  -> always throws, no escape hatch
387

What is the stack of a thread?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A portion of memory.

Less short answer

Fortunately, this is something you do not need to take care of in Java, as it is managed for you by the API. In a nutshell, the stack of a thread is the portion of memory used by a thread to store everything it needs. It stores all the local variables created by the code it executes — and these variables can have references to the heap. The references themselves live on the stack, and the referenced memory lives on the heap. What lives on the stack is not shared: each thread has its own — which is not the case for the heap, of course, where all the threads of your application can read and write data — and where race conditions can happen; no race condition for what you have on the stack. One last word: threads are a system resource, and the size of this stack is fixed at your system level — it may vary from one operating system to the other, but it's typically several megabytes of memory.

void process() {
    int local = 42;              // on the current thread's stack
    var order = new Order();     // the reference on the stack, the object on the heap
    // only the current thread sees 'local' and 'order'
}
388

How can you format a string of characters?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

There are several solutions given to you by the JDK; the simplest one is probably the String.format() factory method: it takes the format as a first argument, and then the objects you want to pass this format to, to render them as a string. This format is inspired by the "horrible" format from the C printf function, that everybody knows; it is described in the JavaDoc of the Formatter class — and there are some differences between the C printf and the Java format: for instance, the errors are not handled in the same way, and some customization has been made. One last word: thread safety may be an issue when it comes to formatting strings: you may think that sharing your Formatters is a good idea, and will save you some resources — but you need to be careful, because they carry some mutable state; something you need to be aware of.

String s = String.format("Hi %s, you have %,d messages", "Ana", 32000);

// Formatters carry state: create one per use/thread
var fmt = new SimpleDateFormat("MM/dd/yyyy"); // do NOT share between threads
389

How can you replace all the elements of a list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There is a method for that.

Less short answer

The method is Collections.replaceAll(list, oldValue, newValue): it takes the list, the value to replace, and the new value — and that's basically it. It supports the replacement of null values, which is great — because you can use this pattern to replace the null values you may have in your list with a default value, for instance. If you check the implementation of the method, you will see that it's also kind of optimized: depending on the size and the nature of your list, it may use indexes to access your elements, or a list iterator. One last word: this method returns true if the element to be replaced was found in your list — which does not mean that your list was modified, because you could have asked to replace it with the same element. Why would you do that? I don't know — but the API designers thought about it.

List<String> names = new ArrayList<>(List.of("ana", null, "bob"));
Collections.replaceAll(names, null, "guest");       // [ana, guest, bob]
boolean found = Collections.replaceAll(names, "ana", "ANA"); // true
390

Why should you favor immutability?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

For many good reasons.

Less short answer

Let me go through some of them. When your objects are non-modifiable, you don't need to bother about the state they carry — it is the state you created them with. So debugging is much simpler, because you do not need to track when the state was modified. And it is simpler also in a concurrent environment: non-modifiable comes with built-in thread safety — race conditions can only occur when you have a write operation, and here, the only write operation you have is the creation of your objects. So if you are using records, or classes with final instance fields, then the creation of your objects is protected against race conditions; non-modifiable makes your job much easier when it comes to bug hunting in your applications. One last word (with the presenter's joke): immutability will even bring better performance when Valhalla delivers the value classes — "I'll stop making fun of Valhalla not coming anytime soon, because it is coming sooner than you expect".

// A record: immutable by definition — no setters, final fields
record Money(BigDecimal amount, Currency currency) { }

// A classic immutable class: final fields, no setters, defensive copies
final class Period {
    private final LocalDate start, end;
    Period(LocalDate start, LocalDate end) {
        this.start = LocalDate.from(start);   // a defensive copy
        this.end = LocalDate.from(end);
    }
}
391

How can you reverse the elements of a list?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

It's a little more tricky than what it seems.

Less short answer

There is a factory method in the Collections factory class, simply called reverse(), that does exactly that — but note that there are several traps in this method. First, this method takes a List — and not a Collection — just because a collection is not ordered: so it does not make sense to try to reverse a collection. Second, this method modifies your list — so your list needs to be modifiable, and it also needs to have a list iterator that supports the set() operation; it's the case for an ArrayList, a LinkedList, and even the list you get when you call Arrays.asList() — but this is still something you need to keep in mind, if your application uses other implementations. But you also have a reversed() method, added as part of the SequencedCollection interface (Java 21), that List extends: this reversed method returns a view on your original list, without modifying it — so it also works on non-modifiable lists; and note that this view is modifiable if the original list is itself modifiable — and in that case, modifying the view modifies the list, and modifying the list modifies the view. One last word: using Collections.reverse may be costly, as it needs to process all your list to reverse it — creating a view is a lightweight process, because what you get is a wrapper that operates lazily on your list.

List<Integer> nums = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
Collections.reverse(nums);      // modifies in place: [3, 2, 1]

List<Integer> view = nums.reversed(); // Java 21+: a view, no copy
view.set(0, 99);                      // modifies the original list too
392

What are the characteristics of a collector?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

They are defined in Collector.Characteristics.

Less short answer

And are used by the Stream API implementation, to use your collector properly. The first one is CONCURRENT: it says that these collectors support concurrency — for the Stream API, it means that a parallel stream can use your collector normally. The second one is UNORDERED: it means that you are collecting your data in a container that does not care about the order in which it receives elements — that is the case for the Collectors.toSet() collector, for instance; this property can be used to relax some constraints for the computation of parallel streams. And the last one is IDENTITY_FINISH: it means that the finisher of your collector is the identity — so the implementation does not need to call it; that's the case for Collectors.toList() or toSet() — but it is not the case for Collectors.joining(), for instance. One last word, and an important one: unfortunately, setting the CONCURRENT characteristic to true does not magically make your collector concurrent — providing a thread-safe implementation is your responsibility.

Collector<Characteristics, ?, ?> c = Collectors.toSet();
// toSet() declares:  Characteristics.UNORDERED, IDENTITY_FINISH
// toList() declares: Characteristics.IDENTITY_FINISH
393

How does ScopedValue inheritance work?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

The presenter does not see a short answer to this one. (laughs)

Less short answer

This "inheritance" term comes from ThreadLocal variables — but scoped values work in a different way. First, you bind a scoped value to a value, and then you call a method that sees this binding. Now the question is: if this method creates new threads, can these threads see the scoped value bindings that you defined? And the default answer is no — but there is one important exception: if this method creates a StructuredTaskScope, then the bindings are seen by the virtual threads created by this structured scope. The reason is that none of these virtual threads can escape the scope of the original method call — so the bindings cannot escape this scope either. One last word: scoped values are a great replacement for ThreadLocal variables — you should use them now; and because you can control their lifecycle when you bind them, they are much safer.

private static final ScopedValue<User> USER = ScopedValue.newInstance();

ScopedValue.where(USER, user).run(() -> process());
// inside the scope — and in StructuredTaskScopes created in it:
try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnFailure()) {
    // the child virtual threads INHERIT the USER binding
    scope.fork(() -> loadProfile(USER.get()));
    scope.join();
}
395

What are the differences between collections and streams?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

There are different interfaces.

Less short answer

The two concepts are in fact fundamentally different: a collection carries elements — a stream does not. The Collection interface defines how you can add, remove, and iterate elements; the Stream interface, on the other hand, defines how you can process them — typically map, filter, and reduce them. So the two concerns — managing elements, and processing them — are very cleanly separated in two different interfaces. This separation is very powerful, because it allows streams to connect to any source of data: collections, of course — but also strings of characters, regular expressions, files, file systems, network sockets... the limit is your imagination. One last word: connecting a stream to a custom source of data is not that hard — you need to implement the Spliterator interface (not easy, but perfectly possible), and pass this spliterator to the StreamSupport stream factory class.

Collection<String> names = List.of("ana", "bob"); // carries elements
Stream<String> processed = names.stream()          // processes elements
        .filter(n -> n.length() == 3)
        .map(String::toUpperCase);
398

How can you generate repeatable random numbers?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A series of random numbers is always repeatable — just provide the same seed.

Less short answer

When you create an instance of the Random class, you initialize the series of random numbers that you can get one at a time, with the various methods of the class — nextInt, nextDouble, and the like. If you give a seed when you create this instance, then for a given seed, you will always get the same series — this may be very useful, for instance, to write deterministic tests. If you do not provide any seed, then the seed is generated from System.nanoTime(). One last word: predictability is a different topic — having non-predictable random series is harder than it seems; for cryptographic and security applications, use SecureRandom instead of Random — the series it generates still depends on the seed, but it is much harder to predict.

Random random = new Random(42);          // a fixed seed: a repeatable series
System.out.println(random.nextInt(100)); // always the same value for seed 42
System.out.println(random.nextInt(100));

SecureRandom secure = new SecureRandom(); // for security/cryptography
399A

What are the components of a collector?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

An initializer (supplier), an accumulator, a combiner — and an optional finisher.

Less short answer

That makes three mandatory components, and one that is optional. In a nutshell, a collector collects elements in a mutable container, and can optionally transform it before returning it. So you need an operation to create this mutable container: that's the initializer — and it's modeled by a Supplier. Then you need a way to add the elements that are pushed to this collector, to this container: that's the accumulator — and it's a BiConsumer; it modifies the container. In case this accumulation is performed in parallel — you may have one instance of your container per worker — and at the end of the day, you need to combine them: that's the job of the combiner, which is a BinaryOperator; it may or may not modify the containers it gets. And the last element maps the final modifiable container to something else — and that's a job for a Function; as well, this function is the identity function, it can be omitted. One last thing worth noting: the Collector interface does not depend on the Stream API — it is completely independent, so you can use a collector to collect your data as you see fit; and by the way, that's also the case for the Gatherer interface — that will be for another time.

Collector<String, ?, String> joining = Collector.of(
        StringBuilder::new,            // initializer (supplier)
        StringBuilder::append,         // accumulator
        (sb1, sb2) -> sb1.append(sb2), // combiner
        StringBuilder::toString);      // finisher (optional)
399B

What is Java?

▶ Watch video
Short answer

A programming language.

Less short answer

An object-oriented programming language — with some zests of functional programming in it, actually more and more. Object-oriented programming means four things: abstraction and encapsulation, inheritance, and polymorphism — Java supports all that, with one restriction: it does not support multiple inheritance of classes, meaning that one class can only extend one other class. And one last one for you: Java is a statically typed language — meaning that the type of any variable you create or receive is known at compile time... even if it is the compiler that guesses the type of this variable for you (var).

// Java in one line: static OO with functional touches
record Customer(String name) { }               // OO + immutable
var active = customers.stream()                // functional (Java 8+)
        .filter(Customer::active).map(Customer::name).toList();

Note: this is the series opener ("First question") — the channel numbered it 399, the same number as the collector-components question (Question 399 A).
